#3121. Can Netflix’s new AI tool stop your TV-streaming doomscrolling? How to try it

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Home & Office > Home Entertainment > Streaming & Services > Video Streaming Services

Netflix is adding a new Discover feature, which recommends tailored streaming selections. Here's how it works.

AI tools are all over the internet, in the workplace, in your apps — and now there’s one coming on Netflix. The streaming giant just announced a new AI feature for the TV app called AI Discover, which will help users find titles that match their moods and interests. It’s initially being rolled out to a small group of users in the US whose language is set to English. To find out if you have access, check your Netflix navigation bar for the word “Discover” with a red gem icon next to it and a “New” label.

Also: Roku is taking away a fan-favorite screensaver

The AI Discover tab will be centered around themes. You’ll be able to pick from a range of themes depending on what you feel like watching, or how long you want to watch. You’ll also see selections that match what you’ve been watching lately and what’s popular.

You can refresh the list of suggestions or dive deeper with more complex search prompts. If you’d like to start over, you can pick the “Something different” option. To support AI Discover, Netflix is also adding a new button next to Play and My List that’ll let you tune your recommendations. You can press this button to keep a recommendation or to request that you don’t be recommended a title again.

Most streaming apps already rely quite heavily on machine learning and AI algorithms to offer user recommendations, even if they’re not marketed as AI features. Though most rely on traditional AI algorithms, like Prime Video’s “Recommended for you” tab, they personalize title recommendations based on your viewing history, likes and dislikes, and rental behavior.

Other platforms offer generative AI and features more similar to Netflix’s AI Discover. For example, YouTube features Gemini-powered searches that let you ask complex questions or use open-ended prompts to get results you want.

Also: The best early Black Friday TV deals are all at Best Buy

Some smart TV interfaces — including including Roku, Fire TV, Google TV, and, coming soon, Apple TV — have also adopted generative AI in their operating systems to let you use voice search with more naturally worded requests, and the system generates cross-platform results.

While I am itching to test this feature to see just how accurate it can be, Netflix hasn’t made it widely available yet. The company will gradually grant users access over the coming weeks, so stay tuned.

#3120. Bausch + Lomb: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Premium product adoption is lifting margins faster than revenue growth, a sign of pricing power in a competitive market.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Oct. 28, 2026, Bausch + Lomb (BLCO +2.85%) announced that revenue grew 8% on a constant-currency basis to $1.375 billion. For long-term investors, sustained growth across pharmaceutical, surgical, and vision care businesses demonstrates broad commercial traction across diverse eye care markets. Three things management said stand out.

Adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation, and amortization (EBITDA), a key measure of core operating profitability, expanded as higher-margin product sales grew faster than operating expenses.

Operating cash flow reached $233 million in the quarter, enabling Bausch + Lomb to pay down debt and lower its net leverage ratio toward its strategic target.

Sales of premium intraocular lenses (IOLs), artificial lenses implanted during cataract surgery, surged alongside prescription dry eye therapies, lifting the overall product mix and profit margins.

Momentum continues across all three operating segments, with full-year revenue and profit targets rising. Proprietary drug formulations, high customer switching costs in vision care, and flexible lens manufacturing support future value. Management is monitoring research pipeline progress, operating expense efficiency, and debt-reduction discipline.

Targeted investments in next-generation lens technology reinforce long-term market position.

#3119. Safari finally gets 3 popular browser features – and I can’t wait to try them

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

New developer beta release notes point to deeper Siri AI integration and more Safari customization.

Developer betas for MacOS 27.3, iPadOS 27.3, and iOS 27.3 are here, with a few major upgrades to the Safari browser. Vertical tabs, adaptive dark mode, and deeper Siri AI integration are on the way to the public, but are first in beta for testing.

Also: Your Apple Watch has 2 new controversial listening features – but you have to opt in

Siri AI is expected to be further woven into Music, Messages, Photos, Calendar, and Safari. Adaptive dark mode will allow for any website to be viewed in dark mode, even if the site doesn’t support it. Finally, vertical tabs are pretty straightforward, enabling vertical navigation and drag-and-drop tab reorganization.

Here’s what you can expect when the software goes public in mid-December.

Also: 3 handy iPhone features that make my life easier – even doing laundry

Siri AI gains more access to your Apple devices, allowing more cross-app functionality and the ability to execute complex, multi-step tasks within your apps.

According to 9to5Mac, you can ask Siri AI to create a playlist in Music and save it to your library, request messages to be scheduled for a later send, organize and label photos, and create a detailed calendar invite — all with your voice.

The same reporting also states that Siri AI features will appear in Maps, Weather, Shortcuts, and Notes, signaling that an AI-supercharged Siri will live in every crevice of your Apple devices.

Also: iOS 27 will organize your open Safari tabs now

I’m not one to complete tasks like the ones mentioned above on my iPhone — I’m far more likely to do them on my MacBook Air, since I associate the laptop with productivity. Every blue moon I’ll rearrange my entire Apple Music catalog, every month I fill out my calendar, and sometimes I’ll go through Photos and try to organize it. I can see myself automating certain steps in these tasks, and I’m excited to try it.

A WebKit blog post details that adaptive dark mode is coming to Safari, how it will work, and what users can expect. According to the post, 40% of web page loads are from dark mode users, but only 10% of web pages natively support it.

I’m particularly excited about this feature, as I set my Apple devices to automatically enable dark mode at 7 p.m. to encourage a more relaxing evening scrolling vibe, especially while reading. I read a lot on my MacBook, and in a dark room, a bright page can feel too bright, but dimming my laptop’s display doesn’t quite do the trick.

Also: Your AirPods got better in iOS 27 – three new ways I’m using them

The WebKit blog post states that adaptive dark mode uses “a color matrix to invert the luminosity of colors while preserving hue” and distinguishes between when a page’s color is purely decorative and when it carries substantial meaning, to ensure the user experience remains consistent between light and dark modes.

Vertical browser tabs aren’t a new concept, but they’ll soon be new to Safari. I see the value in vertical tabs: a list view is easier to scan, web page titles aren’t smushed when several tabs are open, and they’re particularly useful when you’re working on a widescreen monitor.

Many people who spend hours using their Mac or MacBook may find this upcoming feature useful. I do believe that Safari is the superior browser for personal use, given its integration with your Apple devices, especially with features like Handoff.

Also: 7 AI-free iOS 27 features that make old iPhones exciting again

However, I do have one major gripe with vertical tabs: they’re usually locked to the left panel, and I prefer the right. If Safari allows this customization, I’ll seriously consider switching from horizontal tabs.

This iconic Florida critter appears to have swallowed an Xbox just a couple of weeks before the release of GTA 6.

See you later, alligator? Maybe, if you’re the one gamer who wins Microsoft’s Xbox Series X-Bite, a playable Xbox console inside a realistic, 5-foot-long animatronic alligator, complete with the green Xbox logo shining out of its eyeballs.

Yes, it’s real, and there’s only one. To create the critter, Microsoft hired animatronics specialists from John Nolan Studio, which has designed other creatures for The Witcher, Jurassic World Dominion and HBO’s upcoming Harry Potter series.

To the outside world, it looks like a real taxidermied alligator — except it has a custom Xbox Wireless Controller (complete with bite marks) in its jaws and an illuminated Xbox Series X inside.

There’s also a tie-in to the long-awaited Grand Theft Auto 6, which can be played on Microsoft Xbox. GTA 6, which comes out Nov. 19, is set in a fictional version of Florida, famed for its gators. However, because Sony, maker of the PlayStation, has an exclusive marketing partnership with Rockstar Games (the studio behind GTA 6), Microsoft can’t mention the game explicitly in its ads.

But Microsoft can certainly hint at the game. The promos for the giveaway say, “This November, Xbox is heading to Florida,” and feature GTA 6 props, including a baseball bat, duffel bag, playing cards, dominoes and a shot glass with a lipsticked rim. An alarm clock blinks the time 3:05, and Florida’s most famous area code is 305.

Winners must be 18 or older and reside in the US. Your account must be public, and you’ll have to monitor it regularly. Once the winner is notified, they have 48 hours to claim the prize. The sweepstakes is open now and runs through 11:59 p.m. PT on Nov. 10.

I gave up on thermal brushes, then Wavytalk’s Steamlift Pro gave me salon volume in just 15 minutes

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

I had officially closed the book on volumizing hot brushes. Like half the internet, I fell victim to the Revlon One-Step craze a few years back. While it certainly had the smoothing factor down, it felt like it was actively ripping chunks of hair from my scalp with every pass.

My Dyson Airwrap attachment was undeniably gentler, sure, but it never quite delivered the rich, bouncy volume I was actually chasing. Eventually, I resigned myself to heatless curl rods to fake the blowout aesthetic — only to spend the rest of the day mourning the catastrophic amount of curl shrinkage.

Recently, I tested Wavytalk's Steam Sesh. After watching that flat iron tame my hair in 10 minutes flat, I haven’t shut up about steam tech to anyone within earshot. Naturally, my expectations for its sister tool, the Steamlift Pro, were high.

I’m relieved to report it didn’t just meet the bar, it completely cleared it.

The first sign of progress arrived before I even plugged it in. One minor gripe I had with the Steam Sesh was the trial-and-error setup, which practically forced you to scroll TikTok just to figure out how to seat the water tank properly.

Wavytalk clearly took notes. Right inside the box of the Steamlift Pro was a printed visual guide, removing any guesswork.

The mechanics will feel instantly familiar if you’ve used the straightener: pop out the removable tank, add water (distilled remains the smart play if you want to avoid mineral buildup), power it on, and turn to your target temperature. If you want that hit of hydration, hit the dedicated steam switch.

Get instant access to breaking news, the hottest reviews, great deals and helpful tips.

Down from $129 to $99, this 23% saving makes the Steamlift Pro a solid value. If you want bouncy, snag-free 90s volume with gentle steam tech instead of frying your curls on dry heat, grab it while it’s under $100.

Where hot air brushes usually fail curls is tension. Round blow-dry barrels rely on dense, stiff nylon bristles that grab too aggressively, catching on textured coils and snapping fragile strands mid-pull.

The Steamlift Pro swaps out that brutal bristle setup for an integrated 3D comb system built right into the body of the brush. Instead of violently tugging, the teeth firmly grip and separate each individual hair strand as the heated surface glides through.

For someone prone to breakage, the difference in hand-feel was immediate. There was zero scalp-tugging resistance, no terrifying crunch of snapping ends, and no tangled bird’s nest trapped around a rotating barrel. The steam continuously releases through the comb, softening coarse cuticle layers so the hair yields without brute physical force.

My routine was simple. I detangled thoroughly, split my hair into two manageable tiers (top and bottom), and worked in generous sections.

Rather than jumping straight into styling curls, I started by using the Steamlift Pro as a standard smoothing tool, gliding it straight down from roots to mid-lengths to stretch out my natural curl pattern.

Once the baseline was uniform, I went back through the ends to roll and bevel them under for that bouncy, layered finish.

The entire process took 15 minutes. My hair came out silky, glossy, and entirely frizz-free. Most importantly, I retained my actual length instead of watching several inches vanish.

Finding tools that respect coarse, curly, and fragile hair without requiring professional-level arm acrobatics is rare. At this stage in my hair routine, the Steamlift Pro is easily the most capable blowout styler I’ve tested. If you have thick, frizz-prone curls and have spent years burned by viral hot brushes, do not sleep on this steam lineup.

#3116. Disneyland Is Training a Class of AI Robot Interns

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

From Pixar robots to help you park your car to droids roaming Star Wars land, physical AI is popping up all over Disneyland.

If you’ve ever driven to Disneyland, you know that parking is a challenge. When the parks open each morning, long lines clog the nearby streets as engines idle, families waiting anxiously to enter the parking structure so they can start their day at the parks.

Parking attendants can only do so much to handle the tens of thousands of cars arriving every day at the Disneyland Resort. But what if a cutesy autonomous robot from Pixar’s Cars series could help direct traffic flow and guide you right into your parking spot?

That’s a project being tested with FieldAI, a company that builds the AI brains for robots. In a demo video at the Disney Accelerator Demo Day on Tuesday, Disney and FieldAI showed footage of robots that would assist with guiding cars through the parking process.

“Imagine if a character from Cars or robots from Wall-E direct you where to park when you arrive,” Gary Daniels, senior vice president of products and experiences at Disney Experiences Technology, said. “The work we are doing now is a great way to see how the robots function, and, as important, how people respond.”

Other companies build the robots; FieldAI slaps its physical AI solution in a box on top of them, having worked so far with around 30 different robot types, shapes and sizes. The box is equipped with multiple cameras that serve as the robot’s “eyes and ears,” helping the AI model process what it’s experiencing in real time. The robots also collect data while operating.

During the event at Walt Disney Studios, FieldAI showcased a Boston Dynamics robot dog and a miniature version of the BBX droids from The Mandalorian and Grogu that roam Star Wars: Galaxy’s Edge at Disneyland. FieldAI-enabled robots have already been deployed in Disneyland at Galaxy’s Edge on the BBX droids, though they hedged that this is in its early stages.

“Our intelligence is on the bigger version and operating in the park already,” FieldAI CEO Ali Agha told reporters.

David Min, vice president of Disney Innovation, told me it could also be used on characters that interact with guests at parks, naming Olaf and The Hulk as examples.

Another project with Disney involves getting multiple robots to talk to each other to coordinate missions.

Headquartered in Irvine, California, with offices in the San Francisco Bay Area, Boston and Pittsburgh, FieldAI primarily works in construction environments and energy facilities to complete tasks like inspections, material transport and supporting construction. But this is easily translated to a theme park environment, where paths and crowds are constantly changing, and autonomous robots need to account for that without needing mapping or GPS.

“Our BBX proof of concept explores how to bring that navigation together with Disney character performance,” said Doug Fidaleo, lab director, Disney Research, Walt Disney Imagineering R&D. “That’s what drew us to FieldAI: deep experience deploying robots in demanding, unstructured environments with uneven terrain, changing layouts and unexpected obstacles.”

Some of the use cases Agha gives include robots supporting security, perimeter patrols, helping cast members backstage and enabling more in-park experiences — even delivering items to people waiting in line for a ride.

“This is a highly self-autonomous system that operates without a positioning system,” Agha said.

Physical Intelligence (Pi) is another company that builds AI brains for robots. Also showcased during Disney’s Accelerator Demo Day, Pi is focused less on roaming droids that can help or entertain guests and instead on robots helping Disney cast members with backstage tasks.

Namely: picking up, folding, cleaning and sorting the 3D glasses used for rides like Toy Story Midway Mania and shows like Mickey’s PhilharMagic.

It’s an unpleasant task that’s a major pain point for cast members who have to spend a long time in a small room completing it several times a day. It also requires “dexterous manipulation,” with Pi previously working on general-purpose robots that perform tasks like folding and stacking, using robots that have grippers rather than five-digit hands.

Sourish Jasti, a technical staffer with Pi, said the company recently spent 12 days at Disneyland between midnight and 6 a.m. collecting data, testing logistics and moving its robots in and out of the park to see whether it could help with sorting the 3D glasses.

“This was like a robot internship,” Jasti said, adding that physical AI is like a toddler, in that it learns from actually doing things.

The robots — which, much like FieldAI, Pi doesn’t build but instead installs the brains for — could be either a humanoid on wheels or just a mechanical arm. There are cameras on the base and cameras on the wrists to help the AI model “see” what it’s doing as it picks up and sorts the glasses.

“We intend to have robots interning in many different spaces with Disney,” Jasti said, pointing to not only the theme parks but also Disney’s studios, hotel rooms and cruise liners, which he calls “mini cities.”

For both Pi and FieldAI, Min said the benefit is that using AI can train the robots much faster to do the tasks Disney needs them to do — and that going to companies like these, which are already well-established in fields like construction and manufacturing, makes it easier to transfer those skills to the theme parks.

Whether they’re roaming around parking structures or lands, or backstage helping workers, the AI-equipped autonomous robots are solving problems for Disney.

“The connection is through the core capabilities that we’re developing for our robots,” Fidaleo said. “Robots that can perceive the world around them, can act in the physical world and adapt when things change.”

Disney is continuing to push technology from the audio-animatronic figures used on rides like Pirates of the Caribbean, making them more sophisticated to build an immersive experience.

“From the expressive Na’vi shaman to a robotic Spider-Man flying through the air, to a walking baby Groot, each advance changes how we can experience a character in the physical world,” Fidaleo said. “The park of the future will be teeming with robot characters.”

#3115. Want an AI job? Deployment engineers are earning executive-level pay – up to $1.45M a year

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

AI has ushered in new jobs, and some of them are paying handsomely, according to a new study.

AI engineers are making well over six figures in salary — and plenty more in bonuses and equity, according to data from a new report by executive search firm Christian & Timbers.

Enterprises now pay “elite” AI deployment engineers between $586,000 and $1.45 million annually in total compensation, with senior leaders in the field earning up to $2.25 million, according to the report. This figure approaches the median compensation of executive officers of public companies, the authors note.

Also: ‘So perfect, they are literally identical’ – Your AI-polished resumes are recruiters’ new top stressor

Christian & Timbers is an AI executive search and leadership advisory firm, recruiting CEOs, board members, technology executives, and AI engineers for enterprises. The new report draws on more than 550 interviews with engineers, deployment leaders, and executives conducted from January through June 2026, along with closed and current open offers. The compensation bands are Christian & Timbers’ estimates and represent ranges rather than statistical averages.

“Enterprise CEOs are under pressure to deliver returns for their AI investments. The most important decision is how to build the team that delivers on that goal,” said Jeff Christian, founder and CEO of Christian & Timbers, in a press release.

Also: What workers are really using AI for in 2026 – and what they aren’t

The report noted that the staggering salary range includes base salary, bonus, and annualized equity. Christian & Timbers said it defines an “elite” forward deployment engineer (FDE) as “a senior AI-native deployment builder, typically with 7 to 15 years of experience, including at least two years building and deploying agentic automated workflows that deliver measurable ROI with a documented enterprise impact of $10M or more,” who has also led a team of other forward deployment engineers.

Also: 50% of companies generate value from AI now – here’s how

The large salary ranges reflect increasing enterprise investments in AI talent and employee development. Microsoft, AWS, and OpenAI have announced more than $7.5 billion in corporate and partner investment in AI deployment over the past six months, Christian & Timbers noted in the release.

Also: Nearly 70% of workers use AI regularly now – but many get no time to upskill

There’s an increasing need to fill roles such as Senior Principal AI Engineer, AI Solutions Engineer, and Head of Deployment — but there is a limited pool of qualified candidates. The firm estimates that only 2,000 of the most senior deployment leaders exist in the US market, according to a previous Christian & Timbers study, which does not meet demand — so companies are competing for the same small group of professionals, hence the increasing salaries.

While the report underscores lucrative yields for certain engineering roles, the firm said that public job titles make comparisons more challenging.

“Christian & Timbers’ elite category spans several titles, while advertised FDE roles cover a broader range of experience and responsibilities. Experienced deployment engineers may be recruited directly or promoted into the work under titles such as staff software engineer or principal AI engineer,” the study said.

Plus, equity and bonus make up 52% to 66% of senior engineer pay, and most job postings don’t include that value in the salary, the report noted.

Can the $179 Fitbit Edge beat the $249 Apple Watch SE 3?

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

I recently tested the fitness tracking performance of the Fitbit Edge vs. Apple Watch 12, and much to my surprise, the more affordable Fitbit came out on top against one of the best smartwatches in 2026.

But can the Fitbit Edge pull a repeat win against the Apple Watch SE 3? It is, after all, a more direct competitor to the Edge, both in terms of price and features, compared to the Series 12.

To find out, I walked exactly 5,005 steps (manually counted) with the Fitbit Edge logging my efforts from my left wrist and the Apple Watch SE 3 recording the workout from my right wrist. As a control for distance, climb, and pace, I also recorded the walk in the Strava app on my iPhone 16 Plus.

You can find the results of my Fitbit Edge versus Apple Watch SE 3 walk test below, along with the five biggest takeaways from the data, including an overall winner declared at the end.

But first, here's a quick rundown of the key differences between these two popular wearables. The Apple Watch SE 3 starts at $249, is iOS-only, and balances impressive health and fitness features with smart features galore.

The Fitbit Edge starts at $179, works with Android and iOS devices, and also boasts comprehensive holistic and workout-logging chops, but few smart features. That said, the more petite Fitbit Edge gets roughly a week of battery compared to about a day for the Apple Watch SE 3.

When it comes to logging a basic walking workout, both devices are evenly matched with onboard GPS and barometric altimeters for precise location and elevation tracking, along with continuous heart rate monitoring.

Get instant access to breaking news, the hottest reviews, great deals and helpful tips.

The Fitbit Edge logged 5,016 steps during my roughly 45-minute walk, just eleven steps off from my actual total. The Apple Watch SE 3 didn't do too shabby either, logging 109 step fewer than my manual count.

As always, my rule of thumb for these tests is that any step count within roughly 100 steps of my actual total is considered excellent and well within a reasonable margin for error. While the SE 3 is just a hair outside this threshold, this is still a solid result for the entry-level Apple Watch.

One of the biggest differences between the Fitbit Edge and its predecessor, the Fitbit Charge 6, is that the newer model has an altimeter for logging climb data; the older model doesn't. More importantly, the Edge logs uphill efforts with proven accuracy, from my testing, and this demo only adds to that conclusion.

The Apple Watch SE 3, meanwhile, inflated my climb by a similar margin to the Apple Watch 12 in my previous walk test versus the Edge.

The Apple Watch SE 3 (worn on my right wrist) logged higher heart rate data than the Fitbit Edge; however, the Fitbit Edge calculated more calories burned during the walk.

Could this discrepancy in heart rate be a result of the devices tracking my workout from different wrists? Probably not. After all, the best Apple Watches and best fitness trackers, including the Edge, are designed to be worn on the wrist of your choice.

That said, it's up to you to set your chosen wrist preference in the settings/companion app, as I did for this test, to ensure the best possible data.

The Fitbit Edge has the Apple Watch SE 3 easily beat when it comes to overall battery performance; the former lasts up to a week per charge compared to 18-24 hours per charge for the latter.

However, when it comes to GPS battery life, the two are apparently quite evenly matched. Both devices burned through 6% battery during my ~45-minute walk, an outcome I wasn't expecting.

Ultimately, the Fitbit Edge outperformed the Apple Watch SE 3 in this 5,005-step comparison of fitness tracking performance.

The Edge calculated nearly the same amount of steps taken as the manual tally and nearly identical elevation gain data too. That said, the SE 3 was a tiny bit closer to the control distance-wise.

However, even with the Edge coming out on top, I'd still happily use either of these modern wearables as my go-to health and fitness tracker, which begs the question: which do you prefer, the Apple Watch SE 3 or the Fitbit Edge? Let me know in the poll below.

#3113. Here's What $10,000 Invested in SpaceX and Nvidia Could Be Worth by 2030

These AI stocks have serious room for growth.

Space Exploration Technologies (SPCX -3.81%) and Nvidia (NVDA -3.17%) are two of the largest companies in the world. While they may seem unrelated, they're actually close partners, as SpaceX has a massive and growing artificial intelligence (AI) segment. In fact, it is planning to use Nvidia's latest-generation Vera Rubin processor architecture exclusively in its new data centers.

SpaceX and Nvidia offer two completely different angles on AI investment: Nvidia is infrastructure, while SpaceX is application. Investing in both is a great way to get exposure to two of the primary facets of the AI trend. But what could a $10,000 investment split evenly between the two of them today be worth by 2030?

Although AI has found its way into a wide array of use cases, the reality is there are a lot more out there. We've only seen the tip of the iceberg of what AI is capable of, and there still isn't enough computing capacity available to run all of the AI workloads that are popping up. As a result, there will be several more years of heavy AI infrastructure spending. This bodes well for Nvidia, as it means there will be several more years of strong demand for its GPUs. In fact, Nvidia projects that global data center capital expenditures will reach $3 trillion to $4 trillion by 2030. For reference, the big five AI hyperscalers are expected to spend nearly $800 billion on capex in 2026. So, even if we estimate that the entire world spent $1 trillion on data center capital expenditures this year, that forecast implies that total data center spending could rise threefold to fourfold over the next few years.

One of those big spenders will be SpaceX. A few months before it went public, it bought another of CEO Elon Musk's companies: xAI. That acquisition has rapidly become a huge part of SpaceX's business. xAI trains and operates the Grok AI model, along with all its product offshoots (such as Grok Bots). Because SpaceX is on the application side of AI, its runway is practically unlimited.

Moreover, business is good. In the third quarter, revenues from SpaceX's artificial intelligence segment, which it has renamed its "super intelligence" (SI) segment, soared 830% year over year. That shows just how quickly AI tools are being deployed, and the future looks bright for SpaceX.

For Nvidia, projecting its rise is a bit clearer. If total AI spending in 2030 reaches the bottom end of its projection, don't be surprised to see Nvidia's business grow threefold as well. That would transform a $5,000 investment in Nvidia into $15,000 if the stock grows in parallel to the business (which it should over the long term).

SpaceX is a bit trickier, as it couldn't sustain the rapid growth rate it achieved in Q3 for multiple years without its revenues getting absurdly large. However, CEO Elon Musk made the bold projection that SpaceX could have $500 billion to $1 trillion in annual recurring revenue by the end of next year, with its growth driven by the AI boom. Investors should take Musk's predictions with a grain of salt, but he's not wrong about the potential of AI and the rapid pace of its adoption. If we push that revenue prediction out to 2030, and settle at the bottom end of the range, it still points to impressive returns.

If SpaceX can generate $500 billion in 2030 in revenue and if the stock trades then at 10 times sales, that would value the company at $5 trillion, up from around $1.8 trillion today. That would transform a $5,000 investment today into nearly $14,000.

So a combined $10,000 investment in these two could yield about $29,000 by 2030. That's a great return in a short time frame, making these two AI stocks smart buys now.

Keithen Drury has positions in Nvidia. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Nvidia. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3112. Researchers asked people to use AI chatbots for election info – results were mixed

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Chatbots can skew election-related information and act as an intervening layer in a voter's search.

Most search tools now put a layer of AI between you and the information you’re looking for, especially Google.

Also: Tired of AI Overviews? I found 9 Google Search alternatives

In a new study, Yale researchers found that a third of users asked a chatbot to help them pick a candidate. Grok often recommended candidates on its own without any information from users, while ChatGPT recommended candidates most often overall, and twice as much as Claude.

The researchers asked nearly 2,500 people in 35 states with Senate races to query ChatGPT, Claude, Grok, or Gemini about the election. Participants in the study, which ran for a few weeks in October leading up to the Nov. 3 midterm elections, were adults who already use chatbots weekly.

Most users asked chatbots for basic facts about the election, including who was on the ballot. But 36% asked a chatbot to help them pick a candidate to vote for, often by describing their priorities and asking the chatbot to assess which candidates aligned with them.

The former query has slightly less room for error, because the internet has a ton of data on the basic facts of an election, such as who is running where. Chatbots interpreting candidate positions, on the other hand, is a bit more dicey, and relies significantly on the sources a chatbot uses (a statement from a candidate’s own website, for example, is more reliable than a social media clip of an opponent characterizing them in a certain way).

Also: How to keep your chatbot conversations as private as possible

In one example, a user asked ChatGPT to give them the “pros and cons” of each candidate in Virginia, an inherently subjective question.

When it came to recommending candidates, chatbots varied widely.

“When users asked who to vote for but had not yet said anything about themselves, only Grok regularly recommended a candidate, in 14% of chats,” the researchers wrote. “Once users shared their views, ChatGPT recommended a candidate in 79% of chats and Claude in 40%, with Grok and Gemini in between.”

Pew Research found that 42% of US adults surveyed use chatbots to search for information. That’s a broad umbrella that can include election information — but Pew also found that only 8% of US adults reported using chatbots to find election-related information in this year’s election.

What those users are looking for matters, though. Pew found that of that group, 76% were inquiring about where candidates stand on certain issues, and 71% were fact-checking information they’d heard about candidates or issues.

Also: OpenAI will merge ChatGPT’s chat and work tabs

Given that chatbots are still not reliable summarizers, the nature of these questions poses a misinformation risk if users don’t also check a chatbot’s linked sources. An overwhelming majority of users don’t, at least when it comes to Google’s AI Overviews. At least one study found AI platforms gave users “outdated or inaccurate” information that could mislead voters in some cases by missing a deadline or failing to mention a step about voting logistics.

This election cycle was particularly plagued by AI-generated deepfakes and misinformation as tools have become much more sophisticated since the 2024 election. OpenAI and Anthropic reportedly set up election “war rooms” to monitor misuse and information accuracy, according to exclusive reporting by Politico and Semafor.

While an August study from the Brennan Center for Justice found that chatbots did push back surprisingly well against certain falsehoods related to election disinformation campaigns, it also found that AI models easily generated “misleading election-related images and video” when asked.

“Many of their safeguards for preventing this were ineffective, inconsistently applied, or functionally absent,” noted the study, which tested ChatGPT, Gemini, Grok, Meta AI, Runway, and Flux.2.

Also: 50 AI agents tried to build a thriving Minecraft civilization – it didn’t end well

Apart from these factors, however, there is a broader theme in AI use that is equally consequential for civic engagement: cognitive offloading. The political media landscape can already be dense and hard to parse for a voter trying to stay informed. Layering a chatbot — which has opaque training data sets, filters, and other settings determined at the discretion of AI labs — on top of that information search process can further muddy the waters while appearing like a convenient and neutral summarizing tool.

While some studies test how AI chatbots respond to election questions by using standardized questions, this approach let users themselves engage with chatbots as they normally would. While the Yale researchers’ queries and standardized study queries show a lot of similarities, the distinction is valuable for evaluations.

“This is a small step toward evaluations that measure AI’s political influence on the questions users actually ask,” the researchers noted.

Outside of users self-reporting what they asked a chatbot, only AI labs have records of what users are really asking about elections. In October, Anthropic analyzed 250,000 conversations with Claude and determined people “rarely ask Claude whom or what to vote for,” finding that “0.51% of all conversations are specifically about elections or voting,” though it expected election-related queries to increase closer to Nov. 3.

Also: 6 AI agent workflows I use to hand off everyday tasks

“More often, we observed people asking Claude questions that could help them reach their own conclusions, like where candidates stand on a particular issue. Requests that violated our election integrity policies were even less common, appearing in about 0.002% of conversations,” Anthropic’s report said.

The Yale researchers said the next step for a study like theirs is determining how accurate and unbiased models are in their responses, as well as what models learn about users, and whether their recommendations actually match users’ needs.

“In multi-turn chats, users often answered the chatbot’s questions, and those answers led to its recommendations. The questions a model asks shape what users tell it and what it recommends,” the researchers explained.

No doubt there will be more hints forthcoming, as Nothing gears up to persuade you that its products belong just as much in your office as at a warehouse rave in Berlin, and that its approach to AI is essential to your workflow above and beyond the offerings of its strait-laced competitors.

(An article in The Financial Times that the company is targeting insurance companies for further debt financing apparently didn't help things either.)

The most bearish action was in CoreWeave, where traders bought just shy of 200,000 puts, compared to 106,000 calls.

One standout trade happened around 10 a.m. ET when someone opened a new position of 26,000 37.5-strike puts expiring Dec. 18 that traded near the ask price, suggesting the puts were purchased, according to SpotGamma data.

"These are bubble puts," said Scott Bauer, CEO of Chicago-based Prosper Trading Academy. "These are not rip-ups that you buy thinking they'll go to zero. This is the hub, the center of the AI trade, and likely some kind of margin play for someone to hedge their exposure in highly correlated stocks or maybe even the Nasdaq."

The contracts sold for 74 cents apiece for a total of $1.9 million and need CoreWeave to erase another 42% over the next two months to pay off. Prior to this trade, there were just over 1,000 open contracts, meaning the position is a new one.

CoreWeave IPO'd in March 2025 at $40 per share.

"Maybe this is someone that was in early or in on the IPO saying this is my breakeven level," Bauer added. "Maybe they couldn't get this kind of protection in other stocks, the index, or other products."

#3107. Bread as a Sponge for Oil Is the Best Kitchen Hack I Learned All Year

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

Oily pans, stews and sauces are no match for the bread sponge trick.

If you want to degrease a pan or the top layer of an oily sauce without ruining a sponge, a single slice of bread could be your new favorite cleaning sidekick.

While we have vastly improved how we dispose of most things, excess cooking grease still presents a problem. Down the sink? Not advisable. Neither is the dishwasher—even the garbage bin can become a gnarly affair.

Getting the bulk of the grease out of a pan after cooking a batch of bacon or burgers is a good first step, but that greasy pan will either take down a sponge or half a roll of paper towels. What if a humble slice of past-its-prime sandwich bread was the all-natural solution for greasy pans you’ve been looking for?

It’s cheap and easy, and you can toss the used-up bread into a compost bin or countertop food waste processor without disrupting its yuck-free operation.

So, is it ever OK to pour excess grease or used cooking oil down the sink? Even if you run the hot water tap at the same time? Short answer: no. Long answer? Here’s an expert for the “no.” “When grease cools, it solidifies, coating the interior of the pipe,” says Jacylyn Basilone, chief operating officer of Brooks Plumbing. Even if you’re running hot water, the grease will eventually cool further down the line and cause future problems. “Over time, the solidified grease in the pipes can catch debris, thus making a partial clog grow larger and larger,” she says, reducing the pipe’s circumference little by little if you do this repeatedly.

Unlike a clogged shower drain, which you can usually clear easily, “a normal sewer machine or ‘snake’ can’t alleviate this type of stoppage,” says Basilone. “While grease solidifies, it can’t be captured by the claw head. Grease removal is best remediated with a hydrojetter, which tends to be more expensive than the traditional drain-cleaning services.”

Bread is the real answer, and I can’t believe I haven’t been doing this all along. (*Hangs head in shame, fully guilty of relying on the obviously very sketchy hot water method.*)

Bread has always been a superb grease-mopping tool; we usually think of it in a culinary context rather than for waste removal. Just as you’d drag a crusty baguette or hunk of ciabatta across the drippings on your plate, bread is an excellent sponge for all manner of oil, whether or not it is appealing to consume. Using bread to soak up excess oil essentially turns liquid food waste into a solid, making disposal much easier.

If it’s not obvious, this is also a great way to use bread that might otherwise go to waste: the ends of the loaf you can’t bring yourself to eat, or bread that’s gone stale or is starting to show the first mold spores. If you’re going to discard the bread anyway, at least put it to good use.

The bread trick comes in handy in almost endless ways. If there’s a pool of oil, it’s the mop for the job. I initially tried it after cooking bacon. A single, small slice of bread was enough to absorb what was easily four to six ounces of liquid grease left in the pan. I dragged the slice around the pan like a mop, but even after letting it sit a few extra moments, it kept working and eventually soaked up all the liquid grease. (If you’re feeling indulgent and used a fresh bread slice for this purpose, the temptation to make pretty epic croutons here is quite strong — another upside.)

If your air fryer has space between the drawer and the basket, you can place the bread there to soak up grease while cooking. Even for oily, slow-cooked proteins like pork shoulder, you can press the bread onto the meat to soak up grease before serving.

Once the bread has absorbed the oil and needs discarding, responsible food-waste methods are much easier to manage. Depending on how much oil the bread has absorbed, you can safely put the soaked bread in the compost bin or countertop composter without risking a major oil spill and a mess to clean up later. Oil can slow decomposition, so composting large amounts isn’t recommended. Small amounts, such as a few tablespoons at a time, are safe.

Even if you have no other option but to throw it away — and if that is the case, please consider getting a small composting appliance — at the very least, you’re not involving an easily recyclable glass container.

For more home tips, learn this neat trick for reusing frying oil and learn the 10 worst things to pour down your drain.

#3106. Linux Mint 27 arrives next month, and I like it already: New features and other upgrades to expect

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Tech > Services & Software > Operating Systems > Linux

It starts with a surprise version number: Linux Mint 27 instead of 24. But the next release brings far more changes than that.

Linux Mint, my favorite Linux desktop distribution, is ready for a fresh start.

Its lead developer, Clement “Clem” Lefebvre, announced earlier this year that the team behind it was considering slowing Mint’s release cadence because the old twice-a-year release cycle was time-consuming and constrained their ability to make major changes. So it is that the next release, Linux Mint 27, will appear in your Christmas stocking.

Also: 5 premium Linux distros absolutely worth paying for in 2026

The developers officially changed the designation on Oct. 13th when Mint changed both its release cadence and its numbering. Clem’s happy about the change: it “was a tough decision to make, but we’re really starting to see it pay off. We’re taking on projects and implementing changes which would previously have been considered too large or ambitious.”

The new Mint will use Ubuntu 26.04 Long Term Support (LTS) as its base. As before, it will offer Cinnamon, Xfce, and MATE desktops.

Earlier development snapshots had been labeled Mint 23 “Alfa.” That title was explicitly a placeholder while the team decided its release strategy. Its final name is Adrien. Moving ahead, release numbers will be based on the upcoming year. Clem said, “We’ll no longer use point releases, so the release after that will be 28. You’ll upgrade from 27 to 28 to 29, and so on.” Instead of girls’ names, Mint will use boys’ names for distro titles.

On Halloween, Clem announced the top changes in Mint 27. It starts with Mint bringing hibernation back into its supported desktop configuration through a dedicated Sleep Modes page in its System Administration tool. The page is intended to make enabling and configuring hibernation easier, including addressing its swap-space requirements.

It may not sound like much, but Clem explained it’s actually rather a big deal. “When you close the lid of your laptop, it goes into a sleep mode called “Suspend”. This mode is very useful… but it has a major limitation: It uses power.” People often forget it’s not really off and that your laptop will eventually run out of power. When that happens, “the outcome is worse than a normal shutdown. The session is gone, and the battery is empty.”

Also: Linux Mint vs. RefreshOS: I compared the two best distros for new users

That’s where “Hibernation comes into play. It saves the session to disk and completely shuts down the computer. No power is used. When you reopen the lid, the session is as it was, and so is the battery.”

Another new feature is Superlight. This fresh system-monitoring application replaces the older GNOME System Monitor. It comes with CPU, RAM, swap, GPU, network, disk I/O, temperature, and fan-speed graphs. It also includes per-application and per-process resource views. Battery-equipped systems also get charge and power-flow history.

Its storage analysis is particularly interesting: it can break down disk usage into files and folders, Steam games, and Flatpak applications. That brings several common troubleshooting tasks — “What’s using the CPU?” and “Where did my disk space go?” — into one interface. I’ve been checking it out on a Mint virtual machine (VM), and I like it already.

The Nemo file manager is getting a media preview pane, toggled with F7 or a status-bar button. It supports pictures, audio, and video, and displays associated information — for photographs, that can include the capture date and camera model. I like this feature a lot. I’ve always done a lot of work from file managers going all the way back to the classic Norton Commander and its Linux successor, Midnight Commander.

I’ve said it before, I’ll say it again. You don’t need to ever touch a Linux shell via a terminal to use Mint. But if, like me, you’re an old-school Linux user and you like using the terminal, Velocitty, Mint’s new terminal application, looks interesting. Its announced features include:

The point is to make recurring projects and SSH sessions easier to find and reopen. If you like other terminal programs, my latest fave is the Rust-based WexTerm; you don’t have to use Velocitty.

Unfold is a new archive manager for viewing, creating, and extracting archives. It uses 7-Zip and 7zip-RAR and supports the encryption formats those backends handle, including password-protected archives. Its design goal is simplicity rather than a sprawling feature set.

The new Mint uses Ubuntu 26.04 LTS as its foundation. Mint is also moving from Ubiquity to the Linux Mint Debian Edition live installer for installing the operating system. This fresh installer comes with support for OEM bulk-produced installations, installation on both BIOS and EFI PCs, support for Secure Boot, and LVM/LUKS configurations.

Also: Linux Mint vs. Zorin OS: I’ve tried both Windows alternatives, and here’s my winner

For the Linux kernel, Mint 27 will use version 7.0. Which one exactly? Good question. We’ll see closer to Christmas.

Wayland compatibility remains the goal, but X11 isn’t disappearing. As Clem said in June, “In an ideal scenario, we manage to make Wayland work just as well as X11.” But they’re not there yet.

The central change is a cleanup of existing code. Mint’s longer cycle gives developers room to overhaul several parts of the desktop together, rather than squeezing individual improvements into six-month windows. As Clem put it in the September newsletter, “I don’t think I’ve ever seen so many changes and new features implemented for a single release.”

What that means for you and me will be better laptop power management, more capable built-in utilities, and a more complete collection of everyday desktop applications. For example, I expect the updated Mint will ship with GIMP 3.2.2 as its default photo editor, instead of Mint 22.3’s years-old GIMP 2.10.

#3105. Scientists Build Biocomputer That Runs on Bacteria to Solve Hard Problems

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

The study relied on the bacterium E. coli and V. natriegens to run the calculations.

Move over, microchips. The next generation of supercomputing might just run on living bacteria. For 20 years, an international team of scientists has been developing a biocomputer that uses bacteria to process information. Scientists from McGill University in Montreal announced their findings on Nov. 3 and published a paper detailing the results earlier this year in Nature.

Researchers from Canada, Europe, the United Kingdom and Australia contributed to this work, which combines biology with computer science and engineering. The researchers say the resulting biocomputer could be used to solve problems that give standard computers a hard time.

“Electronic (sequential) computers are not meant, or designed, to solve decision trees,” Dan Nicolau, a McGill bioengineering professor and senior author for the research paper, told CNET via email.

Nicolau noted that if electronic computers are to solve these types of problems, the computers will need a lot of RAM.

The study relied on the bacterium Escherichia coli and Vibrio natriegens to run the calculations. The researchers used E. coli because it moves through fluid in a predictable, measurable way and V. natriegens because it reproduces very quickly.

By using live bacteria rather than digital processing, the team says the biocomputer can solve big, complex problems at a small fraction of the memory and resources that regular computers require.

The researchers tested the biocomputer with a seemingly simple math problem called the Subset Sum Problem, which famously becomes exponentially harder as the number of inputs increases.

When the Subset Sum Problem inputs grow too large, the problem is known to give traditional computers issues, such as taking centuries, or even millions of years, to solve the problem.

The researchers translated the problem into a graph that branches off into each possible combination of inputs. Next, the team created a tiny physical structure that mirrored the Subset Sum Problem graph.

Inside the structure, they built a network of tunnels to mirror the graph branches. Each fork in the tunnels represents a decision point, and every possible route through the tunnels represents a different solution to the problem.

The researchers unleashed bacteria, which rapidly reproduce, inside the tunnels. The fast-growing bacteria moved through the tunnels, exploring many routes to the solution simultaneously.

Compared to digital processing, the bacteria are slow. But rather than tackling a problem sequentially, as traditional computers do, the bacteria can examine many solutions at once. And if you need more computing power, bacterial reproduction within the tunnels creates additional processing units to help solve the problem.

The team used automated image analysis to follow the bacteria through the correct tunnels. In this way, the researchers were able to tell which paths through the network satisfied the problem’s constraints.

The researchers were also able to adjust temperatures to prevent bacteria overcrowding while traveling through the network.

Enterprising fans have figured out how to run Doom, the groundbreaking 1993 first-person shooter video game, on everything from E. coli to a thermostat. Nicolau says Doom could probably run on the new system, with an important caveat concerning the biocomputer’s size.

“(The biocomputer) must be huge!” Nicolau said. Because bacteria make slow processors, you would need a massive biocomputer to host enough bacteria to run a video game.

But because no RAM is needed, Nicolau said biocomputing could help to solve the global RAM shortage, and even broaden our understanding of what a computer could be.

“A computer is a device which accepts (input) numbers and gets out (output) numbers. Full stop,” he said. “All other uses of the word computer (are) from our point of view, well, poetical.”

→ Baca artikel asli

#3104. Data Center Backers Suffer Near-Total Midterm Defeats Across the Country

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

More than 40 voting initiatives yielded almost nothing but resistance to data-center projects.

The 2026 midterm election blue wave, which shifted the balance of power in Congress and across state races toward Democrats, can be seen as a repudiation of AI and the controversial data centers that provide its computing power. Data center proponents have heavily backed President Donald Trump and Republican candidates, underscored by xAI CEO Elon Musk investing hundreds of millions of dollars into Texas midterm campaigns to support the industry.

But in many parts of the country, voters didn’t need to make their voices on data centers heard through candidates. They had direct initiatives on the ballot this week that would decide whether data center projects would move ahead in their districts.

In line with a growing backlash against data centers and AI, voters supported about 40 election-day measures that would restrict the construction of new data centers, with only one result — in Harwood, North Dakota — favoring data center expansion.

(Disclosure: Ziff Davis, CNET’s parent company, in 2025 filed a lawsuit against OpenAI, alleging it infringed Ziff Davis copyrights in training and operating its AI systems.)

As broken down by the website Data Centers on the Ballot, Ohio had the most number of voting measures: 21, all of which were in favor of restrictions.

Maine, Illinois, Kansas, New Jersey, California, Florida, Nevada, North Dakota and Wisconsin were the other states with data center voting.

Six elections were held earlier in 2026 in California, Michigan, New Hampshire and Wisconsin, with all of them finding voters opposed to allowing data center builds to continue without new restrictions.

Not all the proposed restrictions were the same. In some elections, voters decided whether to impose a megawatt cap on data center energy use, while other measures addressed specific zoning issues or whether data center projects should require voter approval. Some proposed an outright ban on new data centers, and six of those were successful.

Polls by groups including Pew Research have found Americans have soured on data centers due to high electricity bills, water use and environmental concerns, among other reasons. It’s also hard to separate concerns about data centers from concerns about AI affecting jobs. AI has also become a divisive enough issue to affect midterm voting.

The election results were so bad for the industry that Politico declared data centers to be “the midterms’ other big loser” next to Republicans. Not only did voters enact additional restrictions on builds across the country, but data centers were mentioned in Democratic victory speeches.

“I do not work for data centers. I do not work for Wall Street. I work for you, I work for Ohio,” Senator-elect Sherrod Brown said in his victory speech Tuesday night.

USA Today reported that two mayors, one in Oklahoma and one in Ohio, were recalled after public backlash over proposed data centers.

The midterms might have longer-term effects for tech companies and data center firms. It’s likely that with Democratic control of Congress, there’ll be more scrutiny.

“Public policy is going to reflect public opinion, and there’s been a very substantial change in public opinion just over the last year,” Darrell West, a senior fellow at the Brookings Institution, told Politico.

Political headwinds from the midterms could lead local officials across the country to gain public favor by delaying or canceling data center projects or putting pressure on the companies behind them to offer more attractive deals to communities. The days of huge tax breaks and incentives for data centers could be coming to a close.

“Now, (communities) are pushing data centers to pay taxes, create their own power plants, pay for their use of electricity and water and contribute to local community resources like education funds,” The New York Times reported.

#3103. ‘You Can See Everything’ … in Space? A24 Sends Elizabeth Holmes Doc to ISS

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

Nathan Fielder's documentary about the former Theranos CEO will screen for astronauts in outer space.

The popularity of You Can See Everything has apparently surpassed Earthly measures. Nearly three weeks after the nearly 3-hour documentary about disgraced former Theranos CEO Elizabeth Holmes premiered in theaters domestically, studio A24 is screening it in outer space.

The studio said in an A24 Instagram post that it has granted NASA’s request to screen the film for astronauts aboard the International Space Station.

A representative for A24 didn’t immediately respond to a request for comment.

I’d say this was surprising news, yet after I saw the impact the movie had on Halloween, it makes perfect sense. You Can See Everything is a phenomenon.

At my daughter’s school, I saw a toddler dressed as Holmes, which certainly was a choice. People all over my social feeds were costumed as the infamous scrambled-egg bowl from the movie (IYKYK). Heck, the friend I saw the movie with took my idea and dressed in all black, wearing old-school headphones and an emo hairstyle, and carried a laptop to channel Nathan Fielder.

You Can See Everything, co-directed by Nathan Fielder and Lance Oppenheim, was initially slated for a limited theatrical release in the US. After three weeks, the documentary hit No. 1 at the box office last weekend, beating DC’s Clayface for the top spot.

Now, audiences around the world are clamoring to get a taste of the movie.

They still have to wait, though. UK moviegoers won’t be able to see it until Nov. 20. It’s coming to France on Dec. 30, and while Sony Pictures acquired the rights to release You Can See Everything in countries like Germany, South Korea and Latin America, no dates have been announced yet.

That said, those lucky astronauts in low Earth orbit will soon be side-eyeing yellow mixing bowls, questioning every move made by Holmes’s partner, Billy Evans, and simply wanting more Amanda Seyfried in their lives.

For a movie that is already super disorienting, one has to assume that removing gravity from the equation will make watching You Can See Everything a next-level viewing experience.

#3102. Mortgage Rates Today, Friday, October 30: Noticeably Lower

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

TL;DR: Mortgage rates slid enough today to get home buyers' attention.

Mortgage rates have mostly recovered today after yesterday's jump scare.

The average interest rate on a 30-year, fixed-rate mortgage dropped to 7.18% APR, according to rates provided to NerdWallet by Zillow. This is 14 basis points lower than yesterday and two basis points lower than a week ago. (See our chart below for more specifics.) A basis point is one one-hundredth of a percentage point.

Mortgage rates were elevated this month. The average 30-year mortgage rate for all of October was 7.3%, compared to September's monthly average rate of 6.98%.

While the economy never sleeps, markets are closed on the weekends. The rates you see Friday are unlikely to change much (if at all) until Monday. Happy Halloween!

» Take the next step: Compare mortgage rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

Mortgage rates are constantly changing, since a major part of how rates are set depends on reactions to new inflation reports, job numbers, Fed meetings, global news ... you name it. For example, even tiny changes in the bond market can shift mortgage pricing.

Traders expected that the Fed would vote to hold the federal funds rate steady on Wednesday, so that wasn’t much of a factor for mortgage lenders as they set rates.

In his post-meeting press conference, Fed Chair Kevin Warsh said that September’s rate increase is “starting to work some” toward moving inflation down to the Fed's 2% goal, but that central bankers don't have any firm conclusions yet. He also acknowledged that the Fed’s monetary policy actions hadn’t achieved price stability yet, but that they’re “working on it.”

This hints that if inflation data continues to trend upward, central bankers might raise the federal funds rate again at their December 8-9 meeting.

The September Personal Consumption Expenditures price index (PCE), the Fed’s preferred inflation gauge, dropped yesterday— a day too late for central bankers to consider it in their decision-making process.

The data shows inflation was 3.6% year-over-year in September, up from 3.4% in August.

September’s federal funds rate hike came in the middle of the month, so October’s inflation data will really capture its effect on inflation. The October PCE report will come out on November 25.

Next week, the Nerds will be watching the October jobs report, which comes out Friday, Nov. 6. If it shows that employment remained steady (or even improved), that will build an even stronger case for the Fed to raise the federal funds rate again in December, because it would suggest that the labor market could withstand higher rates.

The Fed has a dual mandate to control inflation while supporting maximum employment. If they can work to lower inflation without hurting employment too badly, central bankers may feel more confident to act.

» Learn more: How the Fed affects mortgage rates

Refinancing might make sense if today’s rates are at least 0.5 to 0.75 of a percentage point lower than your current rate (and if you plan to stay in your home long enough to break even on closing costs).

With rates where they are right now, you may want to start considering a refi if your current rate is around 7.68% or higher — but there just aren't too many people for whom that's true.

Also consider your goals: Are you trying to lower your monthly payment, shorten your loan term or turn home equity into cash? For example, you might be more comfortable with paying a higher rate for a cash-out refinance than you would for a rate-and-term refinance, so long as the overall costs are lower than if you kept your original mortgage and added a HELOC or home equity loan.

If you're looking for a lower rate, use NerdWallet's refinance calculator to estimate savings and understand how long it would take to break even on the costs of refinancing.

» Time to refi? Compare refinance rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

There is no universal “right” time to start shopping — what matters is whether you can comfortably afford a mortgage now at today’s rates.

If the answer is yes, don’t get too hung up on whether you could be missing out on lower rates later; you can refinance down the road. Focus on getting preapproved, comparing lender offers, and understanding what monthly payment works for your budget.

NerdWallet’s affordability calculator can help you estimate your potential monthly payment. If a new home isn’t in the cards right now, there are still things you can do to strengthen your buyer profile. Take this time to pay down existing debts and build your down payment savings. Not only will this free up more cash flow for a future mortgage payment, it can also get you a better interest rate when you’re ready to buy.

» Is now a good time to buy? See NerdWallet’s analysis

If you already have a quote you’re happy with, you should consider locking your mortgage rate, especially if your lender offers a float-down option. A float-down lets you take advantage of a better rate if the market drops during your lock period.

Rate locks protect you from increases while your loan is processed, and with the market forever bouncing around, that peace of mind can be worth it.

#3101. CoreWeave credit default swaps hit distress levels as investors sour on AI debt

CoreWeave's credit stress continued to spiral on Thursday amid a broader sell-off in AI-related names.

CoreWeave — a so-called neocloud company — has taken on more than $51 billion of debt and obligations to finance its AI infrastructure build-out. It's now facing a rebellion from stock and bond investors as concerns mount about AI overspending and debt levels.

Yields on CoreWeave debt have continued to climb, with the yield to maturity on its 9.75% bond due in 2031 reaching 15.78%. Bond yields move inversely to prices. Meanwhile, the spread on its five-year credit default swaps, which are insurance against default, at one point soared above 1,140 basis points. It last stood at nearly 1,038 bps — a 16% week-over-week jump and 164% higher than a year ago.

Credit concerns are spreading into AI equities too. CoreWeave's stock dropped during trading Thursday before recovering and closing 5.94% higher. The moved followed Wednesday's 15.5% nosedive.

Other neocloud stocks were under similar pressure. Nebius also sank before climbing higher to close 1.29% and and Iren ultimately ended the day down less than 1%.

Anxiety about AI overspending is also spreading. For instance, shares of Micron Technology and Advanced Micro Devices sold off following a big sell-off in South Korean chip stocks. Micron closed up 1.87%, while AMD fell 1.5%.

While AI debt worries have been rising steadily, investors have suddenly grown skittish about the underlying finances of companies borrowing heavily to buy chips, build data centers, and rent out computing power.

Coreweave's credit default swaps are one place to see that stress.

Credit default swaps are contracts where buyers pay for protection in case the borrower can't repay its debt. The spread widening in CoreWeave's CDS means investors view the debt as riskier.

The market is "basically saying 'no mas' to AI debt without operating cash flow support," the Evercore ISI trading desk said.

A spread level of 1,000 basis points or above is generally a sign of credit distress, said Gavan Nolan, executive director of fixed income pricing at S&P Global Market Intelligence.

No one is questioning whether demand for CoreWeave's services is real, since the company has contracts with all the big hyperscalers, Nolan said. The question is whether the cash flows will ultimately arrive.

"It's obviously heavily negative free cash flow at the moment, and it needs that cash flow to arrive really to justify all the capex that it's spending," Nolan said.

CEO Mike Intrator dismissed the market moves on Wednesday, according to a memo obtained by CNBC.

"When you are building a new type of technology company there will be periods of time when the market misunderstands and acts irrationally," he wrote. "We are confident in our backlog which more than supports all of our debt obligations."

The backlog is over $100 billion, with around two-thirds of it with investment-grade counterparts including Microsoft, Meta, IBM and Caterpillar, Deutsche Bank said in a note Thursday. The firm reiterated its buy-rating on the stock Wednesday, calling the dislocation a buying opportunity.

"The problem is this kind of concern can prove to be a self-fulfilling prophecy, and risks triggering broader issues in other AI-related names," said Jim Reid, global head of macro and thematic research.

Domino effects may already be happening. Oracle's five-year CDS spread also spiked this week. It was last at 282 basis points, a roughly 10% increase in one week.

"A downgrade from its BBB rating would risk pressuring IG-only investors into forced sales," Reid said.

#3100. Why Horace Mann Educators Stock Soared Today

The niche financial services company delivered an estimates-crushing third quarter.

Horace Mann Educators (HMN +5.75%) released its latest quarterly results after market close on Tuesday, and the market reacted strongly the next day. Thanks to significant top- and bottom-line beats, that reaction was very positive, and the company's stock raced to a nearly 6% gain on Hump Day.

Horace Mann, a niche insurance and financial company which specializes in serving the educational market, booked revenue of just over $439 million in its third quarter. That was marginally higher on a year-over-year basis.

The company didn't hesitate to mention that it notched a new all-time high for "core" profitability, i.e., net income not under generally accepted accounting principles (non-GAAP, or adjusted). This metric, which strips out portfolio losses and one-time expenses, zoomed 50% higher to more than $85 million, or $2.06 per share.

Both line items crushed the average analyst estimates. Pundits tracking Horace Mann stock were modeling only $323 million for revenue, and $1.08 per share for adjusted profitability.

Horace Mann quoted its CEO Marita Zuraitis as saying that the company's far better-than-expected performance was due mainly to "excellent underwriting performance in our property and casualty business, higher net investment income, and continued growth across our diversified business."

With that, Horace Mann raised its full-year guidance for core earnings per share (EPS) for the second quarter in a row. This is now $5.75 to $5.95, up substantially from the previous forecast of $4.60 to $4.90.

The company's clearly doing well in its ever-important insurance operations and directing its float into very productive investments. Given that, I'd say the investor reaction to the quarterly results was understated, if anything.
