#3098. How to Make a $50 Holiday Gift Feel Like $100

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

Trade time and a little extra thought to enhance gifts this holiday season.

Each month, NerdWallet suggests one wallet win to help you feel more confident about your money. This month’s topic is making holiday gifts feel more valuable, and we’re going to show you ways to elevate a gift without overspending.

Making an inexpensive gift feel fancy is a winter challenge I relish.

As a relatively frugal person who often gets sticker shock when shopping for gifts, I’m always looking for creative ways to save money without diminishing the present. Over the years, I’ve developed a few strategies to make a $50 gift feel like it’s worth at least double that.

In the spirit of the season, I’m sharing these strategies with you. I hope you — and your recipients — find them as celebratory as I do.

What does your gift recipient love to do? It helps if they have a favorite hobby, sports team or other interest.

Let’s say your recipient likes to fish. Giving a gift card to a niche fishing store makes it feel far more valuable than the standard Amazon card because it shows that you considered who they are. And there are specialty stores for every hobby these days.

Maybe gift cards aren’t your go-to. You can easily enhance other gifts, too.

Paying $5 extra to engrave or embroider their name on the gift — whether it’s stationery, jewelry or a backpack — makes the item feel far more valuable than its price tag because it’s customized to the individual.

If you choose a gift that couldn’t have been given to anyone else, it increases its value.

Time required: A few extra minutes to think about your recipients’ preferences and apply them when shopping.

A vintage or thrifted scarf can be a wrapping paper alternative that also functions as a gift. Similarly, you could wrap a gift in tea towels, artwork or online prints that can be reused by the recipient.

Another idea: Wrap a gift in printed sheet music that contains a special meaning for the recipient.

Customizing the presentation of the gift elevates it without adding much to the expense.

Time required: Since you have to wrap the gift anyway, this method doesn’t need to take extra time. You’ll just need to find the cloth or paper you plan to use.

One of my favorite gifts in recent memory involved turning a family photograph into a Jane Austen-era image set on a bucolic English estate with the empire-waisted gowns to match. My mom is a big fan of Jane Austen, so I knew she would appreciate seeing the family in a Regency-style photo.

I hired an Etsy artist to transform my original image, but you may be able to do it yourself with a photo app. Similarly, you could create a custom song, illustration or other form of art made specifically for your recipient.

Adding your own creative spin on a gift shows you spent extra time thinking about the recipient to make something extra special for them.

Time required: Applying your own creativity can be time-intensive, but the process is enjoyable so it goes quickly.

I like to call this technique the hybrid gift approach: You buy something, then get crafty to transform the gift into something one-of-a-kind.

One of my favorite ways to do this is with a photo. I’ll buy a nice but inexpensive frame and then put a photo of the recipient, or the recipient alongside loved ones, inside it.

If the person you’re buying for posts on social media, you might not even have to ask them for a photo in advance. Instead, you can find a favorite one that they already shared and surprise them.

The options are endless, especially with online tutorials to help hone your crafting skills. Adding your own customization to a purchased gift makes it feel special, but doesn’t have to cost much.

Time required: Baking, crafting or creating can take several hours, but again, it’s an enjoyable process.

Experiential gifts tend to go over well, and you may even get to have some fun, too.

One year, I gave my mom a membership to a nearby art museum and tacked on an offer to attend an exhibit with her. We had something on the calendar to look forward to together, and she got the option to visit more times throughout the year.

My parents have given me memberships, too. More recently, they gave me one to a local nature center. It’s great because I can visit on my own or attend kid-friendly events at a discounted rate. It makes me feel part of the community, and I love supporting an organization that helps kids learn more about nature.

Another option is to donate to charity in a loved one’s name and then make plans to volunteer at the organization together.

Experiential gifts help create more family memories together, which is one of my favorite ways to celebrate the holidays.

Time required: Setting aside time to visit a museum or other destination can take an entire day, but it’s time well spent.

The bottom line is that giving a highly personalized gift adds value without eating too far into your own budget. Spend $50. Make someone feel like a hundred bucks.

If you’re following along with us on this monthly financial challenge, start by testing out the tips we shared today over the next few weeks. We’ll be back again next month to learn about setting money goals for the new year. And if you missed last month’s edition, take a look back at how to shop the holiday sales.

#3097. Why Duolingo Stock Skyrocketed Today

The education company's patience is paying off.

Shares of Duolingo (DUOL +25.99%) spiked 26% on Wednesday after the popular learning app operator announced a surge in users.

Duolingo's daily active users soared 29% to 64.9 million in the third quarter. That's a significant acceleration from the 23% growth the education company reported in the second quarter and 21% in the first quarter.

"This year, we're prioritizing teaching better and user growth, and we're seeing that strategy paying off," CEO Luis von Ahn said. "Our user growth in every region of the world and in every subject we teach shows that we still have an enormous opportunity ahead of us."

Yet Duolingo's monetization efforts are beginning to bear fruit. Revenue rose 14% to $309 million. Operating cash flow also increased by 6% to $89.4 million.

Duolingo's upbeat guidance helped assuage investors' fears about artificial intelligence (AI)-driven threats and bolstered their confidence in management's long-term growth strategy.

#3096. Brian Sullivan: Why the 'real' price of oil is likely $30-$40 higher than where it is now

Oil, in reality, is $125 per barrel or higher.

That's the real cost of a barrel of oil because buying the physical commodity is a very different story from a number on traders' screens.

Oil futures are just that: paper contracts indicating a price today, tomorrow, next month or further out.  If you want to buy the physical commodity, it may be a totally different story — and price.

As I've reported, prices for oil delivery have skyrocketed. Last week, a VLCC (aka a "supertanker" in non-shipper parlance) was chartered from the U.S. Gulf Coast to China for $76 million. Another very large crude carrier went for $77 million from the U.S. to South Korea. That's 10 times more than the price of the same trip less than one year ago.

Assuming these ships hold about 2 million barrels of oil, that charter price means each barrel on the ship costs about $38 over the price of the oil itself.

I'm certainly not saying to avoid looking at oil futures prices, but you need to know that the price on the CNBC screen or CNBC.com isn't always the full price being paid. These days, any company that needs oil shipped by sea, rail or truck will pay a hefty premium over the price of the oil itself. So, while the volume of oil moving through Hormuz is closing in on pre-war levels, costs are not. And there's no sign that they're going anywhere but up.

You know what else is up? The stocks of the shipowners. Look at their moves this month and this year.

On a more serious note, I want to express my condolences for the awful attacks on the airport in Riyadh, Saudi Arabia. The cowardly strike on civilians—some of whom were religious pilgrims—took the lives of at least 14 people and injured more than 300.

The airport was attacked at least three times as travelers and energy industry professionals from around the world were flying into Riyadh for the World Petroleum Council conference. I was scheduled to fly in on Saturday, but the trip was canceled because of the strikes. The WPC went on as planned — here's one panel that took place Tuesday — and I look forward to seeing all my global energy friends again soon.

Thanks for reading—and watching. Be safe in these crazy and often scary times.

The supercap oil and gas stock to watch right now is Shell (SHEL). Shell is not a shell of its former self. It's back to being what energy investors wanted: a major player in global energy, focused on what it does best. Shareholders are being rewarded.

Shell hit a new high this week. While analysts remain bullish, the stock is getting close to its target price.

Shell may be underappreciated for its huge liquefied natural gas business. RBC analyst Biraj Borkhataria writes: "Without a sustained resumption in Qatari volumes and with European storage levels below seasonal lows heading into winter, European gas prices continued on an upward trajectory in 3Q26. With HH broadly unchanged throughout the summer, export margins have increased to the widest levels seen since 2022, on our estimates nearly double the level in 2Q26 (~$13/mmbtu vs. $7/mmbtu)."

Borkhataria expects Shell and other companies with huge U.S. LNG positions — TotalEnergies (TTE) and BP (BP) — to benefit from wider margins on exports to Europe.

Speaking of RBC and energy, the firm lists ConocoPhillips as one of its top 30 global stock ideas. The company is covered by analyst Scott Hanold, who believes it has a "returns-focused value proposition, a strong balance sheet, and peer-leading distributions." He loves its "global and diverse" asset base and management's clear "investment propositions." Hanold has a $152 target on COP. That's more than 20% upside.

In oil and gas, refiners and big energy stocks are generating cash for investors, and Morgan Stanley is optimistic more is coming.

The firm says the diesel shortage is now spilling over into gasoline, potentially marking another leg of the "golden age" for refiners. Morgan Stanley has been revising its earnings estimates higher for companies including ExxonMobil (XOM), Valero (VLO), Phillips 66 (PSX) and PBF (PBF).

Fun Fact →  While nearly every other company calls Texas home, refiner PBF is headquartered in a suburban office park just off a highway in New Jersey.

Two big calls to quickly highlight in power production.

First, Truist says you have to buy First Solar (FSLR). The analyst upgrades it to Buy with a $270 target. Tariffs should help the Phoenix-based company, giving it pricing power for at least four more years. The Section 232 tariffs—which are too complicated to detail here—should help First Solar raise solar module prices and boost earnings. We agree, having written about the company and stock back in July.

Another call on the Power Insider radar: TD Cowen is starting coverage of TeraWulf (WULF) with a Buy rating and a $21 target. That indicates about 50% upside for the stock, but it's actually one of the Street's lower price targets. TeraWulf and other AI power companies have seen their stocks hit hard following months of big gains, but Cowen analyst Michael Elias says the valuation is now attractive and a likely lease in the second half of 2027 at a Kentucky location could be a catalyst.

In the wake of the terror attacks on Saudi Arabia, we spoke with RBC Capital Markets' Helima Croft about the attacks, energy and more.

The entire energy world is watching Europe's natural gas storage levels. The question is whether the continent will have enough gas to make it safely through the winter. Power Insider wrote about the topic in the last issue.

The fact that JPMorgan highlights expected weather patterns should tell you how much temperature matters when it comes to gas storage. The early forecast calls for October and November to be average or slightly warmer than the 10-year average. The red line represents the 10-year average.

Europe has been largely "saved" by mild temperatures over the past few years. Natural gas storage levels are lower this year than in years past, so the thermometer will matter a lot when it comes to how well countries can, ahem, weather the winter. Random, but interesting.

#3095. I Don’t Buy With a Little Help From My Friends

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

To save money on holiday gifts this year, Americans are using these 5 strategies that require coordination with friends and family.

Gift giving can bust a budget, so some Americans plan to have conversations with friends and family this year about setting limits around holiday gifting.

This isn’t as Scroogey as you might think: 55% of holiday spenders plan to use at least one money-saving strategy that likely involves coordinating with loved ones, according to a new NerdWallet survey conducted online by The Harris Poll.

Money can be a taboo subject, especially among family and friends. But a focused discussion on gift boundaries could be the difference between staying within your budget and piling up debt this holiday season.

What other Americans are doing: 28% of holiday spenders plan to set a per-gift dollar limit with family or friends.

Why this works: It’s simple and easy to remember. Unlike some other ideas on this list, you can probably coordinate this one over text.

For best results: If you’re the one bringing up the topic, you should come with a dollar amount that would be reasonable for everyone in your gift exchange group, so no one feels excluded or overextended.

Your circle of friends and family has a lot of kids.

What other Americans are doing: 23% of holiday spenders plan to buy gifts for children in a family, but not the adults.

Why this works: Cutting out adult gifts altogether can rescue a gift budget that has undergone major “kidflation” as new children join the family.

For best results: If you still want to include adults, give a gift that’s fun for the whole family, like a board game or an experience. And make sure your kid-free loved ones are on board.

You traditionally exchange gifts one-on-one with a friend, but the last few gifts you’ve given — and received — were duds.

What other Americans are doing: 18% of holiday spenders plan to skip exchanging gifts with at least one friend or family member.

Why this works: If you’re feeling gift fatigue, there’s a chance they are, too.

For best results: Frame this as an improvement to the gift-giving tradition rather than a scaling back. Propose an alternative way to celebrate your friendship, like swapping out the $40 gift that’ll probably get shelved for a $7 coffee together.

What other Americans are doing: 16% of holiday spenders plan to do a secret Santa gift exchange, or another game that requires purchasing only one gift.

Why this works: 15% of holiday spenders list buying gifts for more people than they originally planned for as among the reasons they’re most likely to overspend this holiday season. This strategy keeps your list in check, even if the number of people playing grows.

For best results: There are bound to be people in your group for whom the rules to the game are important. If you’re the one organizing, be ready to answer questions — can you trade or steal gifts, for example, and, if so, how many times? Also, this works best for family or friend groups who aren’t likely to go rogue and buy extra gifts anyway.

You have no idea what your college friend’s kids (or spouse) are into.

What other Americans are doing: 12% of holiday spenders plan to give one shared gift per family instead of individual gifts.

Why this works: In addition to saving money, this avoids the awkwardness that comes with uninformed gift giving.

For best results: Presentation is key. Don’t sheepishly give an UNO game you clearly bought on the drive over. You may be cutting back, but enthusiasm is free.

Talking about the holidays and talking about money can be stressful conversation topics. About four in five Americans avoid discussing some money topics with family and friends, according to a 2026 survey by the CFP Board. So discussing both at once may seem daunting. But without a doubt, you’re not the only one thinking about them.

Nearly 9 in 10 (86%) holiday spenders plan to use at least one money-saving strategy this holiday season. Start with an icebreaker: “Hey, have you noticed how expensive everything is lately? Would you be open to talking about how we can do gifts this year while keeping spending under control?” This conversation might be the thing that’s secretly on everyone’s wish list.

#3094. Why Accel Entertainment Was a Huge Winner on Wednesday

Investors were clearly impressed by the gaming company's third-quarter performance.

Holding Accel Entertainment (ACEL +13.15%) in a stock portfolio was quite the accelerant for net worth on Wednesday. The company's share price popped by over 13% across the trading session, thanks to a strong quarterly earnings report that featured twin beats on analyst estimates.

Accel, which concentrates on providing electronic casino-type games and ATMs for venues like bars and restaurants, published its third-quarter figures just after market close on Tuesday. These showed that the company's revenue grew by 10% year over year to almost $364 million. Net income not under generally accepted accounting principles (non-GAAP, or adjusted) flew 12% higher to more than $18 million ($0.22 per share).

Those numbers were high enough to exceed the consensus analyst estimates. Prognosticators tracking Accel were modeling under $357 million for revenue, and $0.17 per share for adjusted net income.

Much of the growth came from simple volume. Accel's count of gaming machines deployed was 29,548, 7% higher year over year. Its tally for locations rose 6% to 4,705. The company said it experienced notable growth in markets such as Georgia, Nebraska, and Illinois, with the latter state's Fairmount Park Casino & Racing complex being a particularly strong performer.

In its earnings release, Accel referred to diversifying its business into other markets, which could be a spur to increased growth in future periods. The same could be said for the dramatic expansion of its gaming footprint in Fairmount. I like the company's business model, which is straightforward and reliably produces decent profitability and positive cash flow, so I think investors were smart to snap up the stock following earnings.

#3092. Kyndryl: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Gross margin climbed to 22.5% in the first half, and management expects further gains, pointing to better profit quality beyond sheer deal volume.

On its earnings call, held on Nov. 4, 2026, for its fiscal 2027 second quarter ended Sept. 30, Kyndryl (KD +30.14%) announced that it reaffirmed its full-year financial outlook. Long-term investors care because the company is shifting toward higher-margin advisory work to expand free cash flow, or cash left after spending on operations and capital projects. Three things management said on the call stand out.

Kyndryl is expanding beyond traditional infrastructure maintenance by winning larger enterprise agreements. Over the last 12 months, the company secured 43 contracts worth over $50 million each, with 11 of those signed in fiscal Q2 alone, as customers upgraded core computing systems.

Gross margin, the percentage of revenue remaining after direct delivery costs, reached 22.5% in the first half of the fiscal year. Management expects cost reductions and automation in service delivery to drive additional margin expansion through the rest of the year.

Enterprise clients are turning to Kyndryl Consult, the company's advisory unit, to deploy artificial intelligence (AI) and upgrade cybersecurity defenses. Over the past 12 months, new contract commitments in consulting exceeded realized revenue.

Demand for cloud infrastructure and operational automation remains steady across enterprise clients.

The company retains structural cost advantages from its operational data, which creates high switching costs for customers. Management is monitoring contract pricing discipline and progress toward generating $400 million to $500 million in annual free cash flow. As corporate technology environments grow more complex, Kyndryl is positioning its consulting services to capture broader software management spending.

#3089. The Austin Powers steamroller: How to trade CoreWeave's slow moving crisis

In Austin Powers: International Man of Mystery, a Virtuocon guard famously stands in front of a commandeered steamroller, screaming "No!!" three times before being run over. The joke, of course, is that the hazard moved so slowly he had ample time to step out of the way.

For months, equity traders holding CoreWeave (CRWV) have played that guard. Despite credit markets flashing warnings, equity holders essentially just stood there. Now, after a shocking 27.5% drop from last Friday's close to this Thursday morning's 52-week lows, followed by a whiplash 17% rebound, equity investors, if not squashed like Dr. Evil's henchman, are dazed and confused. Shares are down another 5% today.

With earnings just a few weeks away, massive leverage, a heavily split sell-side, and 15.4% short interest, the stock remains primed for explosive volatility.

The hazard approaching CRWV equity wasn't hidden. The AI cloud infrastructure platform essentially didn't exist five years ago and has accumulated $51.6 billion in debt (~$46 billion net of cash) against a backdrop of zero historical profitability. That was well known, but the CEO's comments that the company aspired to be investment grade so it could borrow "unlimited sums", even as investors already anticipated nearly another $60 billion in debt in the next 18months, spooked folks. (Trick or Treat!) Especially since the debt issued only in June had already fallen 20% since.

From an options trader's point of view, it was hard to reconcile deteriorating credit and rising leverage with falling options premia; as recently as last week, they were in the 6th percentile, 1.5 standard deviations below the one-year mean. At 72%, options weren't cheap in absolute terms, but given everything going on, they should have been higher. The stock also looked somewhat complacent, with the equity chopping around historical support levels dating back to May 2025. The credit side was on tilt:

Because CRWV is levered by more than 2:1, a 10% move in enterprise value translates to a nearly 22% swing in equity value.

Despite the dire credit picture, equity sentiment remains fiercely divided, driving this week's 17% bounce from the lows. For example, HSBC maintains a Sell rating with a $39 price target, essentially calling for the stock to be cut in half. Conversely, banks like BNP and Cantor Fitzgerald (my former employer) suggest these fears are overblown and the stock should more than double from here.

It's not just bankers defending the company either. Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang downplayed systemic concerns, and ARK Investment stepped in to buy 192,000 shares. BofA argues that CRWV's ability to bring active power online at scale, combined with higher pricing from Nvidia's upcoming Vera Rubin deployments, will drive faster-than-expected revenue growth. They described execution risk as a bigger concern than financing; I'm not sure those who bought the 2032 8 1/2s at par would agree.

With CRWV's credit heavily distressed but its equity heavily defended by high-profile dip-buyers, going outright long or short into earnings carries excessive directional risk. A November/January Call Diagonal, currently trading for just under $6 per spread. Almost 9% of the current stock price. Normally, that's a lot, but given this stock has fallen 27.5% and then gained 17% in the past 5 sessions, it isn't.

#3088. Trusted AI Governance Starts Where Intent Becomes Action

CTO at WSO2, Inc., humanizing tech and architecting transformation; author & keynote speaker; advocating distributed, agentic systems.

​Most enterprise AI governance conversations still start with the model. Accuracy, safety, bias, explainability and output reviews dominate the discussion. But now, those concerns are no longer enough.

In the agentic era, AI systems are not just generating answers. They are calling tools, invoking APIs, retrieving data, delegating work and taking action inside business processes.

Now, the enterprise also has to determine whether the agent was allowed to take a specific action, under the right authority, with the right context, through the right tool, at the right time and for the right purpose. Trusted AI governance starts where intent becomes action.

Traditional enterprise governance assumes software behavior can be specified before deployment. A service exposes an API contract. A workflow follows a defined sequence. A release can be reviewed, tested and approved before it goes live. Runtime governance usually confirms that the system continues to behave according to what was already specified.

Agentic systems do not fit that model cleanly. An agent can reason over context, call tools, retrieve data, invoke APIs, delegate tasks and decide when to continue, stop, escalate or retry. The output matters, but the path matters, too. The authority used, data accessed, tools called and decision to act all become part of the governed surface.

That creates an inversion. In deterministic systems, governance can start with the output specification. In agentic systems, the enterprise must govern the boundary where intent becomes action and retain evidence after the action. Pre-deployment review still has value, but there is no final output contract that can be verified once and trusted forever.

A model can be powerful, accurate and useful. That does not make it trusted in an enterprise. Trust comes from the architecture around the model, including controls that make agent behavior inspectable, attributable and bounded.

Inspectable means the enterprise can see what happened, including tool calls, data access, applied policies, human oversight and resulting action. Attributable means the enterprise knows who or what acted, and under whose authority. An agent should not act as an anonymous workload or shared service account. It needs identity, delegated authority and traceable actions.

Bounded means the agent operates within defined limits: access, tools, data, policy, cost, jurisdiction and human approval. Without these properties, trust collapses into hope.

Trusted AI governance requires two complementary enforcement models: inline governance and infrastructure-enforced governance.

Inline governance is implemented inside the agent or application flow. Teams add policy checks, validation logic, safety rules, approval steps and logging directly into the system. This matters because it has access to local workflow context, business intent and domain-specific rules.

But inline governance is not sufficient by itself. In a large enterprise, every team implementing its own governance logic creates uneven coverage. Controls get duplicated, drift over time and become hard to audit consistently.

Infrastructure-enforced governance applies controls outside the agent implementation through gateways, proxies, runtimes, service meshes, identity systems, model gateways, API gateways and other enforcement points. This gives the enterprise a common enforcement layer at shared boundaries for model calls, API calls, tool access, data access and agent-to-agent traffic.

The two models are not substitutes. Inline governance gives domain precision. Infrastructure-enforced governance gives enterprise consistency. One without the other becomes fragmented or too coarse.

Authorization is essential, but it is not the same as safety. An agent may be authorized to use an email tool, but that does not mean every email it writes should be sent. It may include confidential information, make an unapproved commitment or send a message to the wrong recipient.

The risk is rarely in the tool name alone. The risk is in the parameters of the action, such as which customer record, which amount, which recipient, which query, which file, which authority and which purpose. Authorization determines access to a capability. Safety determines whether a specific use is acceptable in context.

Agents also need identity. Many enterprise systems still depend on shared service accounts, user sessions and embedded credentials that are difficult to attribute cleanly. That model becomes dangerous when agents take multistep actions. The enterprise needs to know who the agent is and on whose authority it is acting.

For technology, AI and security leaders, the priority is to make governance executable, not to write another AI policy document.

Start with discovery. You cannot govern agents, tools, models, APIs, delegated authorities or agent-to-agent interactions you cannot see.

Then treat agents as first-class actors. Give them identities, make delegation explicit, and avoid hiding agentic behavior behind anonymous workloads or shared accounts. Define the boundaries where intent becomes action, such as the points where agents reach models, APIs, tools, data, workflows and other agents. Combine inline and infrastructure-enforced controls at those boundaries, and capture evidence after action.

The agentic era will not be governed by policy documents alone. Governance has to be designed before deployment, enforced while agents act and proven after action through evidence. Design-time governance defines policies, tools, evaluations and approval paths. Runtime governance enforces boundaries when agents access systems, use tools, invoke APIs, delegate work and coordinate with other agents.

Evidence closes the loop. Without evidence, governance becomes a claim. With evidence, it becomes something the enterprise can inspect, audit and improve. The enterprises that get this right will not simply “trust AI.” They will build systems where trust is inspectable, attributable and bounded by design.​

TL;DR: Mortgage rates kicked off the week with a drop, but they’re still above 7%.

Mortgage rates are cooling off as we roll into November. However, they’re still above the 7% threshold.

The average interest rate on a 30-year, fixed-rate mortgage dropped to 7.07% APR, according to rates provided to NerdWallet by Zillow. This is 11 basis points lower than yesterday and 13 basis points lower than a week ago. (See our chart below for more specifics.) A basis point is one one-hundredth of a percentage point.

This week’s employment data could potentially push mortgage rates one way or the other — more on that below the graph.

» Take the next step: Compare mortgage rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

Mortgage rates are constantly changing, since a major part of how rates are set depends on reactions to new inflation reports, job numbers, Fed meetings, global news ... you name it. For example, even tiny changes in the bond market can shift mortgage pricing.

This week, the Nerds will be watching the October jobs report, which comes out on Friday, Nov. 6. If it shows that employment remained steady (or even improved), that will build a stronger case for the Federal Reserve to raise overnight borrowing rates again at their next meeting on Dec. 8-9.

While the Fed doesn’t directly set mortgage rates, it does set the federal funds rate, which is the fee that banks must pay to borrow from one another (which is how they fund mortgages). Lenders raise mortgage rates any time they think that the federal funds rate is about to go up.

Central bankers hiked the federal funds rate in September, so a relatively steady jobs report would suggest the labor market could withstand higher rates.

The Fed has a dual mandate to control inflation while supporting maximum employment, and raising the federal funds rate is their main tool for bringing inflation down. If they can work to lower inflation without hurting employment too badly, central bankers may feel more confident to act.

The September Personal Consumption Expenditures price index (PCE), the Fed’s preferred inflation gauge, was released last Thursday. The data shows inflation was 3.6% year-over-year in September, up from 3.4% in August.

September’s federal funds rate hike came in the middle of the month, so October’s inflation data will really capture its effect on inflation. The October PCE report will come out on Nov. 25.

Not only will this free up more cash flow for a future mortgage payment, it can also get you a better interest rate when you’re ready to buy.

#3086. Is Broadcom Stock a Buy After Marvell's $115 Billion Deal?

A rival chipmaker is buying a big laser maker. Is the AI chip giant's stock, around 30% under its record, still worth buying?

On Nov. 2, Marvell Technology (MRVL -7.08%) agreed to buy Lumentum Holdings (LITE -7.85%), a maker of lasers and optical transceivers, in an all-stock deal. The firms put Lumentum's enterprise value (its stock market value plus debt, minus cash) at $115 billion, using both stocks' Oct. 28 closing prices.

Marvell says the combination creates a category leader in artificial intelligence (AI) connectivity -- the chips and optical parts that send data between AI processors.

Broadcom (AVGO -2.00%) knows the business well. The chip designer sells custom AI accelerators, Ethernet switches, and optical components for AI data centers. But its stock dipped only about 1% on Nov. 2, while Marvell shares slid about 13%.

At roughly $335 as I write, Broadcom stock trades about 30% under its June 2 record close of $481.57. When I last wrote about the company on Oct. 28, with shares near $342, I said I'd consider buying a little at a time.

Marvell already designs custom AI chips, plus network switches and the electrical and optical interconnect chips that link AI systems together. Lumentum brings high-performance lasers and optical transceivers, along with its own compound semiconductor manufacturing.

Broadcom competes in most of those markets. It designs custom AI accelerators for six customers, including Alphabet's Google and Anthropic. The chips are made for one customer's AI work, as an alternative to general-purpose graphics processing units (GPUs).

Broadcom also sells Tomahawk Ethernet switches. On its fiscal third-quarter earnings call on Sept. 2, CEO Hock Tan said the company had just finished designing Tomahawk 7, which he called the industry's first 200-terabit-per-second Ethernet switch.

"[D]emand for lasers ... is far surpassing supply out there in the industry," Tan said on the call. He added that Broadcom holds "a fairly substantial share of this market."

Put simply, Lumentum's lasers sell into a market where buyers can't currently get enough of them. Sure, shortages like this don't tend to last. But I think it makes the deal more of a long-term threat to Broadcom than a short-term one. A bigger rival could matter more when supply catches up to demand and buyers start shopping harder on price.

Showing how far ahead Broadcom's AI business is, its AI semiconductor revenue hit $16.7 billion in fiscal Q3 (the quarter ended Aug. 2), up 221% year over year. Marvell's total revenue for its own quarter ended Aug. 1 was $2.74 billion, or around one-sixth as much.

And Broadcom's AI growth has picked up every quarter this year. AI revenue growth climbed from 106% in fiscal Q1 to 143% in fiscal Q2, then to 221% in fiscal Q3. Management guided for 236% year-over-year growth, to $21.7 billion, in fiscal Q4, which ended Nov. 1.

Broadcom has its own $115 billion number, and it means something else: the company's forecast for AI semiconductor revenue in fiscal 2027, the fiscal year that started this week.

This would double the almost $58 billion Broadcom expected for fiscal 2026. And Tan said on the Sept. 2 call that the company's already locked in the supply to get there.

"Our demand actually exceeds this outlook, and we will work to improve supply," he said.

The deal won't affect that year directly. Marvell expects the acquisition to close in the first half of calendar 2028, pending shareholder and regulatory approvals. So Broadcom's fiscal 2027 will probably be over before the combined company even exists.

Investors can buy this growth for less than they'd pay for the S&P 500 (^GSPC +1.58%). Broadcom trades at around 17 times analysts' consensus earnings estimate for fiscal 2027.

This compares to about 18.7 times forward earnings for the S&P 500 overall.

True, Broadcom relies on a few very big AI customers, and a stronger Marvell could offer these customers another place to shop for custom chips and lasers. But this option is probably years away. And Broadcom's AI business is already several times the size of Marvell's whole company.

Ultimately, the deal doesn't change my take on Broadcom. It does make the company's laser and switching sales worth watching more closely once the combined company exists.

Meanwhile, the stock is a bit cheaper than when I last wrote about it, and nothing in the deal affects Broadcom's fiscal 2027 outlook. I'd still consider buying shares at this price.

Daniel Sparks and his clients do not have positions in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Alphabet, Broadcom, Lumentum, and Marvell Technology. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3085. ​Houston, We Have A Communications Problem: Why Silence After A Breach Is Killing Trust

By Ian Weisberger, co-founder and CEO of CoinRoutes.

In financial services, 2026 has been the year of the constant cyberattack. Whether it is wealth managers losing clients’ Social Security numbers to stolen logins, employees tricked into revealing customer data or botnets hammering firms with AI-generated password attempts, these threats are hitting companies on a near-weekly basis.

With AI making attacks easier, the pace is unlikely to slow. Financial services companies understand that breaches are becoming a fact of life. What they cannot accept, though, is being left in the dark. In financial services, especially in fintech and fast-growing areas such as decentralized finance, communication after a breach has too often been lacking.

​To understand the problem, it’s worth starting with what good looks like. In September, Revolut was reported to have suffered a data breach involving fraudulent government data requests. The firm acknowledged the incident, said it had contacted affected customers directly and described steps it took to block the fraudulent requests and notify relevant authorities.

Later that month, when Bitget reportedly suffered a hack involving more than $350 million, CEO Gracy Chen publicly addressed the impact, explaining the withdrawal suspension and stating that the company would cover the losses.

Neither company had every answer at the outset, but these examples illustrate an important principle of incident communications: acknowledge what is known, explain what remains uncertain and give customers clear information about the steps being taken. That approach can help preserve trust even when the full picture is still emerging.​​

Not every incident is accompanied by the information clients need to understand the risks. A few weeks ago, we began receiving questions about a cyberattack involving a portfolio management service provider we work with. Our systems were not affected, but we share several clients with the provider, leaving us with questions we could not yet answer.

At the time of writing, we were still seeking details about the incident, including what information may have been exposed and how the attackers gained access. Although the provider had indicated that it would share a post-incident review, we did not yet have the information needed to address our clients’ concerns.

Many clients came to us looking for answers, and we had concerns that trading histories and portfolio data could have been exposed. For a trading firm, that can be enough to work out its strategies, among the most valuable things it owns. We asked for details that would have put those concerns to rest, but they did not arrive.​

In financial services, a provider’s clients can collectively manage hundreds of billions of dollars, and they have their own obligations to investors, shareholders and regulators. If providers cannot explain what data was taken, they cannot tell their own clients, and one firm’s silence puts everyone in a difficult position.

This matters most in the newer corners of finance. Sectors such as decentralized finance are still earning the trust of businesses and consumers, with far less reputation to fall back on than an established bank. When something goes wrong, silence costs them more than anyone.

In our own operations, we’ve made timely incident reporting a contractual obligation, committing to investigate incidents and provide clients with a post-mortem within 24 to 48 hours. Because we run a trading system around the clock, we write one even for five-minute outages. For consumer-facing businesses, I would expect a first message closer to an hour. The principle is simple: Say what you know, admit what you don’t yet know and come back as soon as you know more.

As an industry, we need to agree on what accountability looks like and stop pretending breaches don’t happen. At a minimum, every firm should commit to acknowledging breaches within hours, keeping open lines with key partners and providing continuous updates until the picture is complete.

Holding providers accountable starts before anything goes wrong. These are the questions I’d ask any technology provider handling your data before a breach occurs:

1. If something goes wrong, when will I first hear from you? Not when the investigation is finished, but when you first tell me there is a problem, followed by regular updates as the picture becomes clearer.

2. What exactly will you tell me? Telling me it has been fixed is not enough. I want to know what was taken, how it happened, what has changed and whether it could happen again. Above all, I want to know what the stolen information now allows someone to do.

3. If I ask whether my data was affected, can you give me a straight answer? When one of our clients had a messaging account phished, we pulled the IP addresses that had accessed their account with us, traced what had happened and gave them a clear answer. That is the standard I expect.

4. How are passwords, API keys and other secrets protected? Sensitive credentials should be protected through appropriate key-management systems, with access tightly restricted and unnecessary exposure in application memory minimized. Ask whether keys are protected by dedicated key-management hardware or an equivalent, and who has access.

5. If someone gets into one client’s account, can they reach anyone else’s? Each client’s data and credentials should be kept separate, and logins, credentials, trading and client-facing services should be walled off from one another, so that if one area is compromised, the damage stops there.

6. What can someone do with a stolen key, login or access token? Credentials do get stolen. What matters is how much access they provide, how long it stays valid, whether its use would be detected and whether connections are restricted to approved IP addresses. Stolen customer read-only keys should not allow the attacker to access funds or other clients’ information.

When I first came into this industry in 2017, what attracted me was its openness and the competitive energy that came with it. Today, that openness matters more than ever. As cyberattacks become more sophisticated and easier to carry out, breaches will keep happening, and no firm can promise they won’t. What every firm can promise is to communicate honestly and quickly when they do.​

Forbes Technology Council is an invitation-only community for world-class CIOs, CTOs and technology executives. Do I qualify?

#3084. Ameris: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

The bank's willingness to sacrifice market share for loan pricing power signals how disciplined management can sustain profitability across rate cycles.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Oct. 23, 2026, Ameris (ABCB +1.29%) reported a five-year record high earnings of $1.73 per diluted share. Disciplined balance sheet management and mid-single-digit asset growth allowed the bank to expand profitability without taking excessive credit risk. Three things management said stand out.

Net interest margin (the gap between interest earned on assets and paid on deposits) stays protected when lenders avoid price wars. Prioritizing profitability over market share keeps returns on equity high across rate cycles.

Banks require steady deposit growth to fund new loans without relying on expensive wholesale borrowings. Setting deposit growth as the ceiling for loan expansion preserves cheap funding and keeps net interest income expanding.

A low criticized loan ratio (borrowings flagged for potential credit weakness) protects earnings from sudden provisioning charges. Maintaining asset quality well above regional peers preserves regulatory capital and shareholder equity.

Operating efficiency (operating expenses measured as a percentage of revenue) remains under 50% as revenue growth outpaces overhead expansion across southeastern markets.

Core deposit relationships and conservative credit underwriting anchor future performance as leadership monitors deposit pricing competition and loan yields.

The Motley Fool recommends Ameris Bancorp. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3082. Why Intuit Stock Jumped 24% in October

Shares of Intuit (INTU +3.46%) were moving higher last month as investors rotated out of AI stocks and into beaten-down stocks in other sectors, including Intuit, which has underperformed due in part to the perception that it would be disrupted by AI.

The biggest piece of news out on Intuit was the company's acquisition of Sequence, a revenue management company that automates the end-to-end quote-to-revenue process, which it expects to complement QuickBooks and Enterprise Suite, and give it more exposure to AI-based software.

The company also introduced upgrades to the Intuit Intelligence platform, including embedding AI in QuickBooks, Enterprise Suite, and other products across its platform.

By the end of the month, the stock was up 24% on the news, according to S&P Global Market Intelligence.

As you can see from the chart below, the stock climbed steadily over the course of the month, as investors gave the stock a second look in the AI era.

Intuit rallied in the first half of the month, trending with a number of beaten-down stocks, but the big news out on the company came on Oct. 15 when the stock jumped 6.4% after it announced its acquisition of Sequence, which brings an AI-native platform into its fold.

Investors seem to think the move will boost Intuit's overall platform and offer a good example of how the company can bounce back in the AI era. Intuit's management said, "Sequence will let us deliver mature revenue management to customers, further extending Intuit Enterprise Suite directly into industries that increasingly leverage AI consumption models like B2B software and professional services."

Later in the month, the company announced a new wave of Intuit Intelligence features, including industry-specific editions of its Intuit Enterprise Suite.

Both moves seemed to reassure investors that the company was focused on its AI development.

Despite the recovery in October, Intuit stock is still down 48% this year, showing the broader worries about it being disrupted by AI-native competitors for programs like TurboTax, for example.

Intuit's valuation has fallen to a price-to-earnings ratio of just 21, but its growth has slowed, and earnings per share are expected to fall in the new fiscal year.

In light of those challenges, the acquisition of Sequence and the release of new intelligence tools make sense. If Intuit can use those to gain traction and start growing profit again, then the stock looks like a good bet to continue rising from here.

Even two people with similar credit scores might get different rates, depending on their overall financial profiles.

Maybe — but even personalized rate quotes can change until you lock. That’s because lenders adjust pricing multiple times a day in response to market changes.

#3078. Why Individual AI Gains Don’t Add Up To Company Value

Dr. Son Nguyen is Orient Software’s CTO & co-founded Neurond AI, companies specializing in software development, AI, & data science services

​A few months ago, I asked our DevOps and finance teams the same question: What does it cost us each month to run the cloud infrastructure for one of our larger projects? Using different AI tools and data, they returned with different answers.

I couldn’t tell which answer to trust, where the information came from, whether it was current and whether correcting it would help others.

Now, imagine this across thousands of employees and AI tools.

Individual productivity is clearly improving with AI. A Gallup survey from May 2026 found that 65% of employees at organizations that have implemented AI reported a positive effect on their productivity and efficiency.​

But do thousands of assistants, each holding its own fragment of context, add up to a smarter company? I don’t think it necessarily does. While individual AI use can make each employee faster, only a company brain makes the company smarter.

McKinsey’s August 2026 report on the state of AI found that 89% of organizations are now using AI, and 80% of respondents say AI has improved their individual productivity. However, only 37% attribute at least some earnings before interest and taxes impact to AI, which was unchanged over the previous year.

We have become quite good at giving employees access to AI, but what happens to everything they learn from using it?

Each AI chat is its own island. Employees ask questions, refine answers and share business context. Yet that learning often stays within one interaction or workflow, and the next employee starts from scratch.

MIT’s NANDA initiative calls this the “learning gap.” Its preliminary study of 52 organizations found that most enterprise AI systems struggle to retain feedback, adapt or improve over time. The result? Thousands of employees become more productive without the organization becoming proportionally smarter.

Based on my experience, four issues explain why personal gains rarely become organizational gains:

1. Knowledge is scattered and often leaves with people. An employee develops a useful prompt, workflow or understanding of a customer issue, but that knowledge rarely becomes part of the company’s shared memory.

2. Companies lack a shared definition of what their information means. Without it, AI spreads existing inconsistencies faster.

3. Answers lack evidence. If AI doesn’t show the source of the information, it can produce an answer that sounds right without giving the organization a reliable basis for acting on it.

4. There is no shared learning loop. An employee discovers that an AI answer is wrong and corrects it, but that correction stays in their conversation. The next person can encounter the same error.

This is why I believe the quality of the underlying system matters at least as much as the model’s intelligence.

The BEAVER benchmark found that state-of-the-art AI agent frameworks using GPT-5.2 scored only 10.8% on questions drawn from real private data warehouses. As I discussed in my earlier article on AI system design, the model is only one layer. The surrounding architecture determines how information is retrieved, interpreted, governed and used.

A smarter model does not automatically create a smarter company.

Companies need a system that can turn individual interactions into shared organizational intelligence. I call this a company brain. The goal is to make organizational knowledge usable in decision-making. ​

A company brain is neither a chatbot on top of a data lake nor traditional knowledge management. It is not an off-the-shelf product or a platform project.

Instead, it is an operating model. Vendors can provide technology, but cannot decide what information is authoritative, who can access it or who is accountable when AI gets something wrong. When one person learns something valuable, that knowledge should become available to the right people and AI systems, with appropriate governance and evidence.

Before calling an AI system a company brain, I would test it against five requirements.

• Evidence: Every answer should show its source, owner and last update. I’ve explained before why every AI agent decision requires strong evidence. Without evidence, an AI answer may be useful, but not accountable. This matters most in regulated work, where every figure must be traceable. For high-risk uses such as hiring, credit and education, the EU AI Act requires high-quality data and traceability logs from December 2, 2027, for any company offering such systems in the EU. For those uses, the evidence standard is about to become a compliance standard.

• Meaning: Departments must share consistent definitions. If sales and finance mean different things by “revenue,” connecting their data won’t resolve the inconsistency.

• Permissions: People and AI agents should access only what they’re authorized to see and do, especially as AI moves from answering questions to taking actions.

• Learning: When an error is corrected once, the correction should improve the system for everyone. Outdated knowledge should also have a way to expire rather than remain available forever.

• Ownership: Someone at the executive level must own the outcome, with a dedicated budget and clear accountability. Otherwise, a company brain risks becoming everyone’s priority but nobody’s responsibility.

You don’t need a platform decision to begin building the brain. Start with questions your sales, finance, operations, customer success or engineering teams need to answer every week.

Measure how long it takes to get a trustworthy answer with a verifiable source, owner and date. Call this your time to trusted answers.

Then ask: Who corrects errors? Does that learning reach others? Can AI agents act safely within their permissions? These questions reveal more about AI maturity than license counts or daily usage.

To achieve success with enterprise AI, turning what one person learns into organizational intelligence is more important than making individuals faster. Your job isn’t to ask which model to buy, but what your AI knows about your company, and whether it can prove it.

Forbes Technology Council is an invitation-only community for world-class CIOs, CTOs and technology executives. Do I qualify?

#3076. Top Wall Street analysts are bullish on the growth potential of these 3 stocks

Fears around the artificial intelligence trade and higher pressure on oil prices are weighing on global markets, but a few stocks offer investors solid growth prospects even in choppy times.

Investors with a long-term horizon can look beyond the ongoing volatility and track the recommendations of top Wall Street analysts to pick attractive stocks with strong long-term growth potential.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Health insurer UnitedHealth delivered better-than-expected results for the third quarter of 2026. The company also raised its full-year earnings outlook, reflecting continued progress on its turnaround plan.

Following the Q3 print, Morgan Stanley analyst Erin Wright reiterated a buy rating on UNH stock with a price target of $529. "UNH's beat & raise reinforces turnaround momentum, with improving MA [Medicare Advantage]/Optum Health supporting a higher '26 EPS baseline & upside to '27 consensus," said Wright.

Wright noted that UNH raised the earnings guidance despite incremental investments planned for the fourth quarter of 2026.

Wright raised her 2026 EPS estimate to $20.65 from $19.82 and 2027 EPS to $23.24 from $22.54, reflecting the latest upside and an improving 2026 medical loss ratio to 87.4% from 88.1%. Overall, she believes that UNH's operational performance continues to improve, increasing her confidence in its UHC strategy and Optum Health turnaround execution.

Wright ranks No. 264 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. Her ratings have been successful 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 15%. See UnitedHealth Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Social media giant Meta Platforms has been making headlines since the launch of its Muse AI agent, which is seeing solid traction. Investors look forward to updates on the company's AI strategy when it announces its third-quarter results on Oct. 21.

Heading into Q3 earnings, Guggenheim analyst Michael Morris maintained a buy rating on Meta stock and increased his price target to $850 from $700, reflecting higher estimates for 2027. He expects Muse usage momentum and engagement levels to shape investor sentiment into the Q3 earnings call.

Specifically, Morris highlighted that, according to Apptopia, Muse reached 1.3 million daily active users in five weeks versus 300,000 in the first week. Given that Muse's current adoption represents less than 0.05% of Meta's 3.6 billion daily active user base, the analyst expects management to prioritize boosting usage over near-term monetization.

"Over time, we believe Muse monetizes primarily through businesses paying to reach consumers through their agents, consistent with the economics of Meta's core advertising business, rather than consumer wallets," said the five-star analyst. He added that the checkout integrations with Shopify and PayPal reflect this framework.

While there are concerns that a rival could overtake Muse or the recently launched Muse Wonder and push Meta into a prolonged cycle of overinvestment, Morris remains bullish due to the company's strong record in scaling WhatsApp and competing strongly with TikTok via Reels as well as free distribution to an extensive user base.

Morris ranks No. 629 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.2%. See Meta Platforms KPIs on TipRanks.

After hosting a non-deal roadshow with Marvell's senior management following the Investor Day, Stifel analyst Tore Svanberg reiterated a buy rating on the stock with a price target of $370.

The five-star analyst said that a major focus of the roadshow was the XPU and XPU Attach mix, as Marvell's Custom business expands toward its FY31 revenue target of $30 billion. In this regard, management reiterated that it expects a balanced mix of XPU and XPU Attach revenue in FY31, though it noted that either segment could grow rapidly enough to shift the mix toward 60/40 or 40/60.

While XPU drove most of the nearly $1.5 billion Custom revenue in FY26, Svanberg expects Marvell's XPU Attach business to grow substantially through the end of the decade, leading to a more balanced mix in this business. At the Investor Day event, the company raised its FY29 Custom target from more than $10 billion to over $12 billion, mainly driven by strength in the XPU Attach business.

Svanberg said that another key topic in the roadshow was whether Marvell can secure enough capacity to meet its raised targets. Management said that it met with suppliers ahead of Investor Day to share its five-year goals and received commitments to support these targets. The analyst believes that Marvell's supply chain commitments give it a competitive edge over rivals as customers seek reliable partners for large-scale infrastructure buildouts.

Both the Citi® / AAdvantage® Platinum Select® World Elite Mastercard®  and Citi Strata Premier® Card can help you rack up AAdvantage miles. The former earns them directly; the latter transfers points to American Airlines at a 1:1 ratio. So which one is better for American Airlines flyers?

For earning rewards, the Citi Strata Premier® Card is the winner. Although it's not an American Airlines credit card, it can make it easier to accumulate AAdvantage miles on many purchases with its higher earning rates and the option to transfer ThankYou points to American.

Still, the Citi® / AAdvantage® Platinum Select® World Elite Mastercard® can help you achieve AAdvantage elite status and offers perks when flying American Airlines, including a free checked bag and preferred boarding. In 2027, the annual fee will increase, but the card will add new bonus categories and other benefits.

The Citi Strata Premier® Card is a better fit for travelers who want to earn American Airlines miles without committing all their rewards to a single airline program.

Although the Citi® / AAdvantage® Platinum Select® World Elite Mastercard® is an American Airlines credit card, the Citi Strata Premier® Card can be a faster way to accumulate AAdvantage miles on many purchases.

That's because Citi Strata Premier® Card holders can transfer ThankYou points to American Airlines AAdvantage — and several other loyalty programs — at a 1:1 ratio.

Currently, the Citi Strata Premier® Card offers more rewards than the Citi® / AAdvantage® Platinum Select® World Elite Mastercard® in every bonus category. On Jan. 24, 2027, when the benefits on the Citi® / AAdvantage® Platinum Select® World Elite Mastercard® change, that will change — but only in a couple of narrowly defined categories: select streaming services and eligible Rides and Rails purchases, which include taxis, rideshares, subways and trains. Unless those categories make up a significant portion of your spending, the Citi Strata Premier® Card will likely yield more points overall, even if your ultimate goal is American Airlines miles.

» Learn more: 11 Benefits of the Citi Strata Premier® Card

The Citi Strata Premier® Card also offers a $100 annual hotel benefit when you book a single hotel stay of $500 or more, excluding taxes and fees, through Citi Travel. Using the full benefit can more than offset the card’s annual fee, and hotel bookings through Citi Travel earn 10 ThankYou points per dollar.

Just keep in mind that booking through Citi Travel may mean giving up hotel loyalty program benefits you would receive by booking directly. Whether the $100 hotel perk offers enough value to justify booking through Citi Travel will depend on the property, the rates available and how much you value those hotel loyalty benefits.

One of the biggest advantages of the Citi Strata Premier® Card is the ability to earn ThankYou points, which can be transferred to several airline and hotel loyalty programs. If you want the option to transfer points to American Airlines but also see value in the card’s other airline and hotel partners, the Citi Strata Premier® Card is a better choice.

#3072. The Future Of AI Will Be Decided In The Physical World

Daisuke Okanohara is CEO of Matlantis Coporation, and Co-founder and CTO of Preferred Networks, Inc.

​Every conversation about the future of AI circles back to the same assumptions: bigger models, more data, more compute. We treat progress as a contest of intelligence. Yet the constraint that will decide how far AI goes over the next decade has little to do with intelligence and a great deal to do with physics.​

AI is running into two physical walls that no amount of clever software can talk its way past.

In a modern AI system, far more energy goes into moving data around than into performing the calculations themselves. The calculations inside a chip are remarkably cheap. The real expense lies in shuttling enormous volumes of data between memory and processor, billions of times every second. By some engineering estimates, that movement can consume ten to a hundred times more energy than the computation it serves.​

For two decades, processing power has raced ahead while the speed of feeding data to it has crawled, and today’s large language models are limited less by how fast they can think than by how fast they can be fed.​

The bill lands on the balance sheet and on the power grid. Running the AI we expect to deploy across the economy could require tens of gigawatts of additional electricity. The ceiling on AI’s growth is becoming an energy ceiling, and that energy is spent in a place few people think to look: the distance data must travel.

The second wall sits one level deeper, in the materials our hardware is built from.​

Better chips, better batteries, better energy systems: nearly every hardware advance the AI era depends on eventually comes down to discovering or improving a material. Materials discovery has always been agonizingly slow. The number of possible combinations is effectively limitless, while the number humanity has tested remains vanishingly small.​

That mattered less when hardware progress had room to run elsewhere. It matters enormously now, because materials are precisely where both walls have to be broken: new architectures to shorten the distance data travels, new materials to move electricity with less waste, new chemistries to store energy more densely.

The instinct is to spend through these walls, buying more chips and standing up more data centers. That buys time without changing the trajectory, because it keeps optimizing the very designs that created the bottleneck.​

The more durable path is using AI to redesign the physical layers beneath AI itself.​

For most of computing history, those layers were designed in isolation. Materials scientists handed properties to device engineers, who handed components to chip designers, who handed hardware to system builders. When the binding constraints are physical and cut across every layer, the layers have to be designed together. Having spent years building both AI chips and the AI tools that design materials, I see this cross-layer approach as the defining shift of the coming decade.​

Three changes follow in practice. Organizationally, teams that once optimized their own slice hold a shared goal and work across team boundaries, so a system-level question like halving energy per query gets answered partly at the materials level. In process, sequential handoffs become joint development, with materials treated as design variables from the start and simulation as a standard gate before capital commits to hardware. In budget, funding follows the genuine bottleneck on the path to the goal.​

Materials discovery is where this change is furthest along. Understanding how a material behaves once meant either a physical experiment or a simulation too slow to model anything realistic. AI has broken that trade-off by learning the rules of how atoms interact, reaching near-laboratory accuracy at speeds that were recently impossible. At Matlantis, we have watched work that would once have demanded roughly 20 years of conventional calculation finish in about a week. Companies, including Volkswagen and Panasonic, now use this kind of simulation to explore materials at a pace no traditional lab could match.​

Speed of that magnitude changes decisions by moving materials questions earlier. Screen candidates in parallel with product design instead of ahead of it. Verify early whether materials that are scarce, regulated, or exposed to supply chain risk can be replaced, and whether promising but untested ones hold up. Make go or no-go roadmap calls on simulation evidence in weeks instead of lab evidence in years.

Picture where this leads. AI agents survey the research, propose candidate materials and run thousands of simulations in parallel around the clock, sending only the rare promising candidate to an automated lab for physical confirmation. The bottleneck shifts from human hours to the genuine limits of physics and experiment, a far better problem to have.​

Leaders can prepare now with three moves. First, build an in-house team that can read AI results critically, because the value of ten thousand simulations depends on the judgment applied to the ten that matter. Second, restructure the development cycle around that speed, with simulation running from day one rather than after prototypes exist. Third, choose partners that span layers: the suppliers, foundries and research institutions that own the parts of the stack you don’t.​

A caveat: even far more capable AI will never fully solve the physical world, because reality holds layers of complexity that cannot be predicted from the rules beneath them. The useful question is where irreversibility begins. Trust AI to explore, rank and explain at a scale humans cannot. Reserve for humans every decision that cannot be undone: synthesizing a material, committing capital, taking a product to market. Experiment keeps the final word. AI changes how fast and how intelligently we can run it.​

The decisive edge over the next decade will belong to whomever understands that AI’s progress now runs through the physical world, and who designs across every layer of the stack rather than optimizing a single one. Where physics sets the limit, AI is becoming the way through.​

Forbes Technology Council is an invitation-only community for world-class CIOs, CTOs and technology executives. Do I qualify?

#3071. Acumen Pharmaceuticals: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

A single confirmatory Phase III trial design could streamline the path to regulatory approval if November data validates the oligomer-targeting approach.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Oct. 26, 2026, Acumen Pharmaceuticals (ABOS -7.72%) confirmed that it expects to report top-line Phase II trial results for its lead Alzheimer's drug candidate, sabirnetug, in November 2026. The readout represents the first major clinical test of whether targeting toxic protein clusters called amyloid beta oligomers in the brain slows cognitive decline. For long-term investors, positive Phase II data could validate this central scientific thesis and establish a direct path toward a single confirmatory Phase III registration trial. Three key takeaways from the call highlight what investors need to know.

Acumen is preparing top-line Phase II results for sabirnetug, its proprietary monoclonal antibody designed to neutralize toxic amyloid-beta oligomers rather than clear established plaque. The Phase II trial evaluates two doses against placebo across 18 months, tracking primary cognitive endpoints alongside imaging and fluid biomarkers.

The business finished the third quarter with $79 million in cash and marketable securities, which are liquid short-term investments. This balance provides a financial bridge past the upcoming clinical readout and funds planned research and development into early 2027 without immediate dilution pressure.

Preclinical work on Acumen's enhanced brain delivery (EBD) platform demonstrated improved antibody penetration across the blood-brain barrier, a biological filter that limits drug absorption into brain tissue. Lead candidate ACU-401 generated up to a 40-fold increase in exposure in the frontal cortex of nonhuman primates while preserving oligomer selectivity, paving the way for planned investigational new drug regulatory filings for human clinical testing in mid-2027.

Clinical momentum is accelerating ahead of the November readout, and the pipeline is moving steadily toward its next regulatory stages. Acumen holds key structural advantages through its specialized oligomer-targeting antibodies and a planned single confirmatory Phase III trial design. Management is watching primary cognitive slowing metrics, safety outcomes including rates of amyloid-related imaging abnormalities, and fluid biomarker responses.

Disciplined execution across ongoing clinical trials positions the company's platform for potential long-term value creation in neurodegenerative disease treatments.
