#3070. Management Mistakes That Throttle Innovation On Tech Teams

Tech leaders often say they want their teams to experiment and pursue new ideas. But even seemingly reasonable management practices can make creative risks feel too costly or time-consuming. When those pressures become part of everyday work, employees may become less willing to test unfamiliar approaches or challenge established ways of working.

The disconnect between encouraging innovation and making room for it isn’t always obvious to leaders. Here, members of Forbes Technology Council explore the management habits that can quietly hold innovation back and explain what leaders can do differently to support experimentation.

The mistake is making every experiment a delivery commitment. Having managed program managers and engineers, I’d separate learning goals from ship dates. Only 13% of AI users in Microsoft’s 2026 survey said reinvention was rewarded when results fell short. Set a question, a budget and a stop date; disproving the idea can still be a useful result. - Swapnil Upganlawar, Amazon

Blanket bans and strict restrictions on AI tools kill innovation. Blocking doesn’t stop adoption. It creates an AI lockdown paradox, where employees move their work to personal devices and accounts. Leaders end up with all of the security risk and none of the visibility. Instead of acting as the “Department of No,” leaders should put automated guardrails in place that let teams experiment safely. - Rick Caccia, WitnessAI

Forbes Technology Council is an invitation-only community for world-class CIOs, CTOs and technology executives. Do I qualify?

Only rewarding safe goals undermines innovation. If people are judged only on goals, they make conservative plans and stop proposing big bets. We want people who set goals they might miss and run their area like owners. We judge them on how far they moved the business and what they learned. - Chris Szymansky, Fieldguide

Rushing teams into execution before giving them space to understand, experiment and learn stifles experimentation. Innovation suffers when leaders don’t take the time to deeply understand the problem, connect it to a meaningful business outcome, and bring the right cross-functional teams together. Release in small, meaningful increments; test, learn and iterate. Great innovation needs both freedom to experiment and discipline around outcomes. - Benu Aggarwal, Milestone, Inc.

Punishing failed experiments while celebrating innovation holds teams back. When leaders demand certainty before approving new ideas or judge every experiment by immediate returns, teams learn that playing it safe is rewarded. Innovation requires disciplined experimentation, clear boundaries and permission to learn. The real test of leadership is not how it celebrates success but how it responds when a well-designed experiment fails. - Maman Ibrahim, EugeneZonda

Overengineering experimentation quietly kills innovation. Discipline matters, but every experiment doesn’t need to become a project. Give teams a clear problem, guardrails and the autonomy to test, learn and iterate quickly. The goal isn’t less process. It’s making sure the process serves innovation—not the other way around. - Abhijit Kakhandiki, BMC Software

Filling every sprint to 100% quietly kills innovation. Leaders say they want experiments, then plan the roadmap so tightly that there is no room to try anything. People will not test a new idea when it means missing a committed deadline. If experimentation matters, it needs space on the calendar. Keep some capacity unplanned and protect it when delivery pressure builds. - Mudit Singh, TestMu AI (LambdaTest)

Starting with features instead of user problems kills innovation. When epics and features are defined before the team understands user needs, experimentation is compressed into implementation. UX and delivery teams are left optimizing a predetermined solution instead of using discovery to jointly design a better one. Engage UX strategists from the start. This gives teams the greatest autonomy to design the best solution. - April Ho-Nishimura, Infineon Technologies AG

Prioritizing trends and buzzwords over how comfortable employees are with adopting new technologies can undermine innovation. Leaders may see a new tool or tech stack as an opportunity to innovate, but forcing it on a team without considering their experience can have the opposite effect. Innovation works best when people have the time, support and freedom to explore technology rather than simply follow trends. - Artem Lalaiants, RiskSeal, Inc.

Treating the existing workflow as untouchable quietly kills innovation. In recent banking discussions, leaders emphasized redesigning the process before deciding where AI belongs. Asking teams to experiment while preserving every approval, handoff and metric limits what they can discover. Give teams permission to question how work gets done and test a better way with clear guardrails and outcomes. - Gregg Aldana, Appian

Demanding outcomes without fostering the culture that supports innovation is counterproductive. Leadership needs to put more emphasis on people as half of the factors in successful outcomes. Providing your people with a culture that fosters creativity, open debate and collaboration is necessary for experimentation and for turning that experimentation into commercial products. - Susan Odle, StorMagic

Giving teams a destination when what they need is a direction limits innovation. A list of approved projects only contains what leadership has imagined, so teams never get past those ideas. With AI, we set clear limits on data access, then handed every department a blank page and one direction: Try things and find your own use cases. Ideas came from every function, not from the plan we drew up in advance. - Alex Saric, Ivalua

Requiring a business case before anyone is allowed to try something can shut down experimentation. Leaders say they want experimentation, then ask for projected ROI on a two-week test, which guarantees that only safe, incremental ideas get proposed. Give teams a small preapproved budget and time box for experiments, and ask for a business case only after the test produces evidence. - Agung Dwi Sandi, rankpillar Group

Judging experiments by the same metrics as established products quietly kills innovation. Teams learn to protect quarterly targets instead of testing bold ideas. A space mission needs ground tests that expose weaknesses before launch; tech teams need that same permission to discover what fails. The space mindset: Reward disciplined learning, with clear hypotheses, bounded resources and evidence that informs the next decision. Make learning a performance goal. - Shelli Brunswick, SB Global LLC

Roadmaps and dates can constrain innovation. Often, management is trying to forecast the impact of things on the business, and because of this, they put expectations around dates, delivery and impact. But innovation requires a lot of experimentation, and the first version is often not correct or impactful in the way you expect. It is the constant iteration that drives real results, and that is hard to quantify and predict. - Mark Beare, Malwarebytes

Having to report to a CFO to justify market demand when the technology has not even been prototyped, tested or taken to market is a sure way to kill innovation. While innovation teams need to work in a resource-disciplined manner, finance should not have a seat at the table during the early stages of innovation, concepting and user testing. - Mark Francis, CaregiverZone

Innovation must not be limited by a long list of constraints. It also must be presented to the right audience. Wheeled luggage was patented in 1970 but did not become widely popular until two decades later. How management delivers their message when an innovative idea presented does not meet current needs is critical. For example, “That’s not what I am looking for” versus “How do you see this idea resolving the issues in front of us?” create two different impacts. - Irene Thong, Oglethorpe Power

Requiring too many layers of approval before anyone can test an idea discourages innovation. Having worked through several major technology shifts, I’ve learned that you rarely know exactly what will work until you try it. When every experiment requires multiple meetings and executive sign-off, people stop bringing forward ideas. Give teams room to test things without making them defend every idea first. - Nada Usina, NU Advisory Partners

Rewarding people for knowing rather than learning stifles innovation. When teams are expected to have the right answer before they experiment, they stick to familiar problems and safer solutions. Leaders need to make it easier to be wrong, test assumptions and learn quickly. The point of experimentation isn’t always to succeed. Sometimes it’s simply to find out what doesn’t work and why. - Dhiraj Rajaram, Mu Sigma

Mortgage rates jumped after stronger-than-expected jobs data raised expectations for a Fed rate hike next month.

Mortgage rates are higher today on the heels of strong labor market data released yesterday.

The average interest rate on a 30-year, fixed-rate mortgage jumped to 7.07% APR, according to rates provided to NerdWallet by Zillow. This is 14 basis points higher than yesterday but 25 basis points lower than a week ago. (See our chart below for more specifics.) A basis point is one one-hundredth of a percentage point.

Mortgage rates are always on the move, so if you're tracking rates day-to-day, you're going to see a lot of volatility.

As for today’s jump: Job openings hit a four-month high of 7.5 million in September, according to the Labor Department’s Job Openings and Labor Turnover Survey released yesterday. Hiring picked up, too, while layoffs declined. Friday’s jobs report, showing October data, could potentially push mortgage rates one way or the other.

To understand the link between jobs data and mortgage rates, keep reading below the graph.

» Take the next step: Compare mortgage rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

Mortgage rates are constantly changing, since a major part of how rates are set depends on reactions to new inflation reports, job numbers, Fed meetings, global news ... you name it. For example, even tiny changes in the bond market can shift mortgage pricing.

This week, the Nerds will be watching the October jobs report, which comes out on Friday, Nov. 6. If it shows that employment remained steady (or even improved), that will build a stronger case for the Federal Reserve to raise overnight borrowing rates again at their next meeting on Dec. 8-9.

While the Fed doesn’t directly set mortgage rates, it does set the federal funds rate, which is the fee that banks must pay to borrow from one another (which is how they fund mortgages). Lenders raise mortgage rates any time they think that the federal funds rate is about to go up.

Central bankers hiked the federal funds rate in September, so a relatively steady jobs report would suggest the labor market could withstand higher rates.

The Fed has a dual mandate to control inflation while supporting maximum employment, and raising the federal funds rate is their main tool for bringing inflation down. If they can work to lower inflation without hurting employment too badly, central bankers may feel more confident to act.

The September Personal Consumption Expenditures price index (PCE), the Fed’s preferred inflation gauge, was released last Thursday. The data shows inflation was 3.6% year-over-year in September, up from 3.4% in August.

September’s federal funds rate hike came in the middle of the month, so October’s inflation data will really capture its effect on inflation. The October PCE report will come out on Nov. 25.

For example, you might be more comfortable with paying a higher rate for a cash-out refinance than you would for a rate-and-term refinance, so long as the overall costs are lower than if you kept your original mortgage and added a HELOC or home equity loan.

#3063. Pet Insurance on Your Mind? Buy It Now, Not Later

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

If your pet is young and healthy, the cost of coverage may not seem worth it. But that math can change fast.

Senior Writer & Licensed Insurance Specialist

It feels natural to protect your pet’s health with insurance in the same way you protect your loved ones with health insurance. After all, fur babies are part of the family.

But if you’ve shopped for pet insurance recently, you might have cringed at the cost. Should you really pay hundreds of dollars every year for something you might not use? For some pet parents, the answer is yes, even if their pet is young and healthy. And that’s partly because of how pet insurance handles pre-existing conditions.

A pre-existing condition is any illness or injury that shows symptoms before your pet insurance policy takes effect. Pet insurers usually won’t cover these conditions.

Dr. Rebecca Greenstein, chief veterinarian at Kleinburg Veterinary Hospital and veterinary expert for rover.com, says she’s seeing an increase in claim denials based on pre-existing conditions. “The whole point of insurance is you want broad spectrum coverage for a variety of things,” she says. “By the time they're excluding major organ systems and anything related to that, you may be paying monthly for coverage that doesn't actually help you when you need to go make a claim.”

If you’re considering pet insurance, it’s best to buy a policy before your pet needs it.

Your pet’s pre-existing conditions will determine which treatments and procedures insurers cover down the road.

Say your dog has a history of allergies. If you buy a policy after your four-footed friend has shown symptoms, pet insurers typically won’t pay for allergy shots or other treatments. The exclusion might also apply to related problems like itchiness or ear infections, says Dr. Michael Goldstein, medical director at Billerica Veterinary Hospital and Thrive Pet Healthcare.

He offers the example of a dog who goes to the vet for a hotspot one year and an ear infection the next. If you got pet insurance for your dog between those visits, the insurer might deny your claim because both issues could be caused by underlying allergies.

The best way around this type of scenario is to buy coverage when your pet is young, before any symptoms develop.

» MORE: The best pet insurance for pre-existing conditions

It’s tricky to figure out if pet insurance will actually save you money in the long run. The most common type of plan covers accidents and illness. It costs an average of $836 for dogs and $435 for cats each year, according to the North American Pet Health Insurance Association (NAPHIA). You could pay these premiums for years before an emergency surgery or chronic illness makes its way to your financial doorstep.

It’s impossible to know for sure whether your pet will stay healthy or run up sky-high vet bills. But you can assess the potential value of pet insurance by looking at common chronic conditions that pets develop with age and how much it costs to treat them.

For instance, “cats generally, as younger animals, don't have a lot of problems,” Goldstein says. “But as they get older, there are definitely common issues. Older cats in particular get chronic kidney disease.”

In cats over age 10, the Cornell Feline Health Center estimates that as many as 40% have chronic kidney disease. The annual cost to treat this condition can top $1,200.

Another example is atopic dermatitis from skin allergies, a common ailment affecting as many as 15% of dogs, according to the Cornell Richard P. Riney Canine Health Center. A single vet visit to address these itchy, persistent symptoms can cost $250 or more. And while that’s a fraction of what you might pay for pet insurance, costs of chronic conditions can add up over your pet’s lifetime.

Vet bills can also balloon quickly if chronic conditions require surgery. One example of this is hip dysplasia, a genetic disorder that can cause arthritis and pain in large-breed dogs. Depending on the procedure, you could pay up to $10,000 for hip surgery.

Many pet insurance plans cover treatments for hip dysplasia, including surgical costs, as long as it's not a pre-existing condition. However, some policies have waiting periods of six to 12 months for orthopedic issues like hip dysplasia. If symptoms develop during that waiting period, the condition will be considered pre-existing. That’s another argument for buying your pet’s coverage earlier rather than later.

Most importantly, having insurance means you can choose the best treatment for your pet no matter the cost. “It's really tragic when it's a life and death decision that is needed and it’s based on someone's financial constraints,” Greenstein says. “What is best and what's affordable often are not on the same side of the spectrum.”

One option to keep pet insurance affordable is to buy an accident-only plan. This type of coverage is usually much cheaper, costing about $190 annually for dogs and $112 for cats, according to NAPHIA. These plans cover injuries resulting from accidents, which include not just wounds and broken bones but also objects or toxins your pet might swallow.

However, an accident-only plan won’t help with some of the most common vet expenses. Illnesses, not injuries, are behind most claims submitted to pet insurer Lemonade, the company said in an email. In the past year, more than 60% of Lemonade's pet insurance claims stemmed from illnesses. That’s twice as many as the next-largest claim category, which was preventive care. A similar trend can be found in data from Nationwide, where chronic diseases make up a clear majority of pet insurance claims submitted to the company in 2025.

If you opt for an accident-only plan or no coverage at all, it’s still smart to set aside some savings for those inevitable pet emergencies and illnesses. This pool of funds can keep you from having to make difficult decisions about your pet’s care based solely on the cost of treatment.

#3061. Could Investing $400 per Month in VTI Make You a Millionaire? Here's the Math.

This low-cost index fund could deliver millionaire-making returns.

The Vanguard Morningstar Total Stock Market ETF (VTI +1.54%) is one of my favorite ways to invest. I own it, I believe in it, I'm buying more of it. This remarkably well-diversified exchange-traded fund (ETF) holds 3,519 stocks representing the "total" U.S. stock market.

When you invest in VTI, you don't just get the S&P 500 index. You also get a mix of thousands of other companies, including small-cap stocks, mid-cap stocks, value stocks, and more. I want to own them all, in case some of these smaller companies or undervalued sectors of the market perform better in the future.

But can investing in VTI really make you a millionaire? Actually, yes. It can. Let's look at historical returns for this total stock market ETF and see how much you could earn.

VTI has a long track record. This fund was established in May 2001. Over the past 25 years, it's delivered average annual returns of 9.38%. Future returns are not guaranteed, but let's assume that you could earn that same return for the next 25 years or more.

If you invested $400 a month in the VTI, earning 9.38% annualized returns, in five years, your investment would grow to $28,945. After 10 years, you'd have $74,262. After 20 years, you'd have $256,293. After 30 years, you'd have $702,488. And in year 34, you'd pass the million-dollar mark -- your investment would now be worth about $1.028 million.

What if VTI can deliver stronger returns and get you to millionaire status faster? Over the past five years, this fund has delivered 10.95% annualized returns.

Let's assume you invested $400 per month in VTI and the fund maintained its 10.95% average annual return. After 10 years, you'd have $80,073. After 20 years, you'd have $306,410. After 30 years, you'd have $946,190. And in year 31 you'd become a millionaire, with more than $1.05 million.

Over the past 10 years, VTI delivered even stronger annualized returns: 14.87%. There's no guarantee that such a strong return will keep coming forever. But let's assume you can earn that higher return on your VTI investments -- this rate of return could make you a millionaire even faster.

If you invested $400 a month in VTI, earning an average annual return of 14.87%, after 10 years you'd have $96,841. After 20 years, you'd have $484,211. And after 25 years of investing in VTI, you'd be a millionaire: $1,000,709.

Ben Gran has positions in Vanguard Morningstar Total Stock Market ETF. The Motley Fool has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3058. Colgate-Palmolive vs. Hershey: Which Consumer Goods Stock Is the Better Buy for 2027?

Both of these defensive stocks are growing sales about 6%, but one offers a higher yield and a lower valuation as cocoa costs weigh on its shares.

Defensive investors often look for stable dividends and household names during times of market uncertainty. Choosing between Colgate-Palmolive (CL +3.28%) and Hershey (HSY +4.15%) requires understanding their different approaches to global growth and category dominance.

Colgate-Palmolive offers broad international exposure through its leadership in oral care and pet nutrition. Hershey provides a concentrated bet on the resilient American snacking market with its portfolio of iconic chocolate brands. Comparing these two giants helps reveal which better aligns with your strategy for 2027.

Colgate-Palmolive is a global leader in oral care and hygiene, operating as one of the most recognizable names among consumer staples stocks. The company sells essential products like toothpaste and soaps in over 200 countries and relies on its Hill's Pet Nutrition segment for significant growth.

On a trailing 12-month basis through the September quarter, revenue reached $21 billion, representing roughly 6% year-over-year growth. Net income was $2 billion. This level of profitability reflects the steady global demand for the company's diverse portfolio of household and personal care brands.

As of its September 2026 balance sheet, the company carried a debt-to-equity ratio of 12x, which measures total debt relative to shareholder equity. Its current ratio was about 1x, measuring the company's ability to pay short-term obligations with its current assets.

TTM free cash flow was nearly $3.8 billion, representing cash generated after accounting for all capital expenditures.

Hershey is a snacking powerhouse with a portfolio of more than 70 brands, including iconic sweets, mints, and chocolate products. The company primarily focuses on its North American confection and snacking segments while gradually expanding its international presence in regions like Brazil. McLane Company represents roughly 27% of consolidated net sales as a primary distributor for retail chains, and such customer concentration adds a layer of risk to the business.

TTM revenue for the September quarter was $12.2 billion, an increase of 6.7% over the year-ago period. Net income was nearly $1.8 billion. This performance highlights the company's ability to maintain sales growth and consumer loyalty in the highly competitive snack market.

The debt-to-equity ratio was approximately 1.1x, calculated by dividing total debt by the value of shareholder equity. The current ratio was roughly 1.2x, indicating the company's ability to meet short-term liabilities with its short-term assets. TTM free cash flow was close to $2.1 billion.

Colgate-Palmolive faces risks from international macroeconomic and geopolitical instability, particularly regarding its operations in Ukraine and volatile markets like Venezuela. The company also deals with intense global competition from private labels and must navigate substantial litigation risks related to talc claims. Furthermore, its reliance on a limited number of single-source suppliers for key raw materials could impact production.

Hershey faces volatility in global commodity markets, especially cocoa, which has weighed on its results over the last few years. The company is currently working through international growth challenges and the integration of acquisitions like LesserEvil. Additionally, it must manage technology risks associated with its global ERP system and potential cyber threats.

Hershey currently offers a more attractive valuation based on its lower Forward P/E and its lower P/S ratio, which compares the price to sales over the past twelve months.

Valuation metrics include those sourced from Financial Modeling Prep (FMP) and may differ from those of other data providers.

I would buy Hershey because it offers more value than Colgate shares. Hershey has faced record-high cocoa prices in recent years, which has sent its stock down and dividend yield up. It offers a higher yield at 3.4% compared to Colgate's 2.2% as of Nov. 4, 2026.

That higher yield is also well supported by free cash flow, with Hershey distributing about 55% of its free cash flow over the past year. By comparison, Colgate paid out 44% of its trailing free cash flow. Both are healthy payout ratios that provide ample cushion to sustain and grow the dividend even if profits weaken due to a recession.

Colgate is the leader in toothpaste and owns other household brands, including Hill's pet food. This drives stable sales in almost any economy, but I don't believe people are going to lose their taste for chocolate either. Hershey's revenues have grown for many years, reflecting a strategy to expand its portfolio of candy and snacks, including Skinny Pop. These brands put it in a solid position for long-term growth.

John Ballard has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Colgate-Palmolive and Hershey. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3056. This Janus Henderson fund manager owns CoreWeave debt — here's why he's sticking with it

CoreWeave can overcome the market's skepticism about its debt.

That isn't the consensus view — some Coreweave debt remains deeply distressed with yields above 15%. But Coreweave should power through this tough stretch, according to John Lloyd, global head of multi-sector and corporate credit at Janus Henderson.

"It's a little bit of overreacting," Lloyd tells CNBC, referring to the sell-off in Coreweave bonds, which have tanked along with its equity.

Lloyd owns Coreweave 9.75% bonds due in 2031 in the firm's Multi-Sector Income Fund. The yield to maturity on the debt is now 15.97%, a more than 70% jump since its issuance in April. Bond prices and yields move in opposite directions.

"Bond prices are lower, spreads are wider, yields are wider, and I don't see a huge fundamental change in the ecosystem right now that would warrant that, other than their higher cost of financing, which I think they can get creative with," Lloyd added.

CoreWeave has taken on a massive amount of debt to fund its data center buildout. The company has roughly $51.6 billion in total debt, including about $35 billion in notes, bonds and loans.

The market is getting nervous about how CoreWeave will be able to keep financing its data centers, and at what cost. Its stock has tumbled 20% over the past week, while the spread on its five-year credit default swaps is nearly 1,081 basis points — lower than earlier in the session but up 13% week over week. Spreads of 1,000 basis points and above are generally a sign of credit distress.

CoreWeave shares moved roughly 2% higher on Tuesday, after tumbling over the past several days and hitting a 52-week low earlier in the session. Its bond yields and spreads remain highly elevated.

Lloyd believes the selloff is a bump in the road for the neocloud company.

The market has been spooked by the jump in Treasury rates, which pushes up funding costs for companies, he explained. The focus largely turned to companies like CoreWeave that have refinance risk when rates move higher, he said.

CoreWeave leverage is high with a debt-to-equity ratio of 702%, up from 290% a year ago. Moreover, CoreWeave will spend an estimated $36.4 billion on capital expenditures this year against Ebitda of $7.5 billion, according to consensus estimates.

Revenue and Ebitda will ramp up next year, hitting $26.5 billion and $16.7 billion respectively, analysts estimate. But capex will hit $47.9 billion, indicating the company may need to finance its data center buildout with more equity issuance and/or debt.

Lloyd isn't necessarily worried about CoreWeave's ability to finance its capital expenditures, despite the fact that its capex outweighs its free-cash flow.

"Next year … they're going to be generating operating free-cash flow that'll support some of that capex need," he said.

The company can also turn to other methods of funding, like more GPU financing, which will allow it to borrow against its graphics processing units or rental contracts, he noted. In addition, CoreWeave could ask its biggest customers — including Microsoft, Meta Platforms and OpenAI — to increase their prepayments, he added.

"They can probably get creative enough where they don't even need to come to the high-yield market," Lloyd said.

The company also doesn't have any near-term maturities, which means it isn't facing a refinancing risk anytime soon, he added.

Meanwhile the supply/demand dynamic should remain tight across the AI compute landscape. Overall adoption rates within the ecosystem are running above consensus demand curves, he said. Add to that a bottleneck in supply, with chips and memory power sold out, and it's a nice set up for CoreWeave.

"All data point to the business model functioning very, very well," Lloyd said. "CoreWeave is one of the best-in-class at getting data centers up and actually running them," he added, noting that CoreWeave's financials have been improving throughout the year.

Another turning point could come if Anthropic goes forth with an IPO, expected in November, followed by OpenAI. Successful IPOs for the frontier labs could soothe market nerves and help support the neocloud operators.

"You just need more of those data points where people become more comfortable with the AI demand side," Lloyd said.

CoreWeave still needs to convince markets it can handle higher market rates and get through the financing bottlenecks.

Lloyd sees the company making it, albeit with some bumps along the way. The bonds should recover, he says, "just not in that straight line."

#3052. Datadog: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

All 15 of the top 15 AI leaders use Datadog's tools, and its non-AI business is also accelerating.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 4, 2026, Datadog (DDOG -14.42%) reported total revenue of $1.2 billion, representing 35% year-over-year growth. Broad-based adoption across non-artificial intelligence (AI) enterprise accounts and modern AI workloads underscores the platform's expanding role in software operations. Three things management said stand out.

Core enterprise demand for software monitoring expanded independently of the AI boom. That steady expansion provides reliable recurring cash flows while the company sells additional software modules to existing accounts.

Landing larger upfront contracts accelerates top-line growth and reduces reliance on expanding existing accounts. New accounts are deploying multiple software products from day one, driving higher initial deal sizes.

Artificial intelligence adoption is expanding rapidly across every customer cohort, and market demand for software observability tools is broadening.

Platform consolidation, deeper public sector security certifications, and automated operational tools position the business to capture long-term enterprise spending. Management is watching pricing discipline as fast-growing clients expand usage.

#3049. UFP Technologies: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

The Dominican Republic production shift at AJR is still waiting on customer qualification, so its cost-benefit has not yet reached the margins.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 4, 2026, UFP Technologies (UFPT +17.66%) announced sales of $172.4 million, an 11.6% year-over-year increase. Organic sales growth, which measures revenue expansion excluding acquisitions, reached 11.1%. Three takeaways from management's comments stand out for long-term investors.

Medical technology sales expanded 12.5% in the quarter. UFP Technologies relies on broad demand across cardiovascular, orthopedic, infection control, and patient-handling products rather than on a single customer contract.

Management is shifting AJR's labor-intensive manufacturing, its medical product assembly business, to the Dominican Republic to reduce expenses. Customer qualification requirements have extended the timeline, creating a temporary headwind for gross margin, which measures profit after direct production costs.

The business ended the quarter with a leverage ratio, which measures debt relative to earnings, well under 1x. Management is expanding its credit facility to pursue larger target companies while funding internal growth.

Operational momentum builds into 2027 as new manufacturing capacity comes online in the Dominican Republic and recent niche acquisitions broaden specialized printing and molding capabilities. Management is focusing on operational execution and program integration across its decentralized operating model.

Long-term growth relies on expanding capacity for single-use medical components while maintaining disciplined capital allocation for acquisitions.

#3045. ASML vs. Marvell Technology: Which Artificial Intelligence Stock Is a Better Buy Going into 2027?

ASML's near-monopoly on chipmaking lithography and Marvell's AI data push sit on very different rungs of the industry, and their valuations and risks tell divergent stories.

The race for better artificial intelligence continues to reshape the market, making investors choose between equipment giants and chip designers. Should you buy ASML (ASML -3.60%) or Marvell Technology (MRVL -7.08%) today?

ASML provides the complex lithography systems used by the world's leading foundries to print the most advanced microchips. Marvell specializes in infrastructure solutions, connecting and moving massive amounts of data across cloud and carrier networks. Both companies are pivotal to the future of semiconductor technology, but they occupy very different rungs on the industry ladder.

ASML manufactures the lithography systems that are essential for the production of advanced microchips found in everything from smartphones to data centers. It is currently the sole supplier of extreme ultraviolet (EUV) lithography machines, which its latest annual report, filed for the fiscal year ended Dec. 31, 2025, notes are critical for cost-effective microchip scaling. The company serves the global semiconductor stocks ecosystem by delivering hardware and software for EUV and cheaper deep ultraviolet (DUV) applications.

In the fiscal year ended Dec. 31, 2025, revenue reached $36.7 billion, representing a growth of 15.6% compared with the prior fiscal year. This expansion in the top line helped the company generate a net income of $10.8 billion. The net margin, which measures the percentage of revenue remaining after all expenses are paid, reached 29.4% during this period.

According to its December 2025 balance sheet, the debt-to-equity ratio of nearly 0.2x shows the company uses very little borrowed money relative to its equity. The current ratio of approximately 1.3x indicates it maintains sufficient short-term assets to cover immediate liabilities. Free cash flow, which is the cash left over after accounting for capital expenditures, reached $12.4 billion for the fiscal year ended Dec. 31, 2025.

Marvell Technology designs data infrastructure solutions that power artificial intelligence, cloud computing, and automotive industrial end markets across the globe. The company reported in its latest annual report, filed for the fiscal year ended Jan. 31, 2026, that its revenue is highly concentrated among a limited number of buyers. Customer concentration like this adds a layer of risk to the business, as one distributor represented nearly 37% of revenue and a single direct customer accounted for roughly 14%.

In the fiscal year ended Jan. 31, 2026, revenue reached $8.2 billion, representing year-over-year growth of 42.1%. This sharp increase in sales helped the company achieve a net income of $2.7 billion. The net margin for the period reached 32.6%, reflecting a significant improvement compared with the losses reported in the prior fiscal year.

As of its January 2026 balance sheet, the debt-to-equity ratio of roughly 0.3x indicates a healthy balance between debt and equity. The current ratio of approximately 2.0x measures its ability to cover short-term obligations, and free cash flow for the fiscal year ended Jan. 31, 2026, was nearly $1.4 billion. Note that stock-based compensation (SBC) represented 33.8% of operating cash flow, which inflates reported cash generation since SBC is a non-cash expense added back in the cash flow statement.

ASML faces potential volatility in the global chip market, where cycles of high demand for new equipment can be followed by periods of overcapacity. Geopolitical tensions also pose a significant threat, as trade restrictions on high-end lithography equipment can limit sales to certain geographic regions. Additionally, the extreme complexity of its machines means any production delays or technical hurdles in next-generation systems could impact its long-term growth trajectory.

Marvell Technology faces risks from significant revenue concentration, as a few large customers account for a majority of its annual sales. Strategic and integration risks are heightened by large-scale acquisitions, notably the announced $115 billion all-stock acquisition of Lumentum Holdings (LITE -7.85%). Additionally, the company faces intense competition from Broadcom (AVGO -2.00%) and potential supply chain disruptions due to the geographic concentration of its manufacturing partners.

ASML appears more affordably valued based on its Forward P/E and its P/S ratio, which measures market value against sales over the past twelve months.

Valuation metrics include sourcing from Financial Modeling Prep (FMP) and may differ from other data providers.

ASML and Marvell Technology are both great stocks to own for exposure to the artificial intelligence boom. The former is an attractive AI stock since it holds a legal monopoly over the EUV lithography machines needed to construct AI chips, as competitors have not been able to successfully replicate the technology at scale. However, if I had to choose between these two, I would invest in Marvell.

Not only did Marvell report 42% year-over-year revenue growth in its 2026 fiscal year (FY), in its Investor Day held on Oct. 6, management set a FY 2031 target of $70 billion to $90 billion in sales. That's incredible growth compared to FY 2026's $8.2 billion, and indicates the company is seeing strong customer demand for its AI solutions.

That's where Marvell's bid to acquire Lumentum comes in. Lumentum reported fiscal Q1 revenue of $1.3 billion on Nov. 2, which represents a jaw-dropping 137% year-over-year increase. Its optical connectivity offerings enable rapid data transmission, a critical feature for data-hungry AI systems.

Marvell's acquisition caused its share price to fall, as Wall Street weighed the dilution risk of the all-stock deal. This creates an opportunity to pick up Marvell shares on the dip, as I believe Marvell's addition of Lumentum makes the company stronger over the long run.

Robert Izquierdo has positions in ASML, Broadcom, and Marvell Technology. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends ASML, Broadcom, Lumentum, and Marvell Technology. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3040. Geron Corp (GERN) Q3 2026 Earnings Call Transcript

First profit as a commercial-stage biotech, on RYTELO growth and cost cuts.

Need a quote from a Motley Fool analyst? Email [email protected]

Geron Corporation (GERN +16.13%) reported its first quarter of profitability since its transition to a commercial-stage biopharmaceutical company, supported by RYTELO adoption and reduced operating expenses. Management stated that the company is focused on deepening its penetration into the U.S. community oncology market, where it sees thousands of additional eligible patients. The company confirmed its full-year revenue targets while lowering expense projections following a strategic workforce reduction implemented in late 2025. Strategic priorities now include evaluating business development opportunities to expand the hematology pipeline following the discontinuation of the Phase 3 IMpactMF trial.

Operator: Hello, and welcome to the Geron Corporation Third Quarter 2026 Earnings Call. [Operator Instructions] Please be advised that today's conference is being recorded. I would now like to turn the call over to Dawn Schottlandt, SVP, Investor Relations and Corporate Affairs. You may begin.

Dawn Schottlandt: Good morning, everyone. Welcome to the Geron Corporation Third Quarter 2026 Earnings Conference Call. Before we begin, please note that during the course of presentation and question-and-answer session, we will be making forward-looking statements regarding future events, performance, plans, expectations and other projections, including those related to our 2026 financial guidance, our current RYTELO commercialization strategy and related opportunities in the U.S. and the EU, the therapy potential of RYTELO, other anticipated clinical and commercial events and related time lines, the sufficiency of our financial resources and other statements that are not historical fact, which, of course, involve risks and uncertainties that could cause actual events, performance and results to differ materially from those contained in these forward-looking statements.

Geron undertakes no duty or obligation to update its forward-looking statements. Joining me on today's call are several members of Geron's management team; Harout Semerjian, Chief Executive Officer; Ahmed ElNawawi, our Chief Commercial Officer; Dr. Joseph Eid, Executive Vice President, Research and Development and Chief Medical Officer; and Michelle Robertson, our Chief Financial Officer.

With that, I'll turn the call over to Harout to discuss Geron's progress in the third quarter.

Harout Semerjian: Thank you, Dawn, and good morning, everyone. We continue to execute our strategy to build a leading hematology company. We're delivering consistent RYTELO demand growth, reinforcing our confidence in the low-risk MDS U.S. commercial opportunity and RYTELO's growth trajectory over the next several years. We also initiated a gated commercial launch in targeted European markets that gives us the optionality to scale up RYTELO's availability through partnerships. Last week, we announced the discontinuation of the IMpactMF trial evaluating imetelstat in relapsed and refractory myelofibrosis following a recommendation by the independent DMC. We believe this is the right course of action for patients and for Geron.

In the third quarter, we continued to see increased prescribing from community practices leading to a quarter-over-quarter demand growth of 10% and net revenue growth of 5% or $60.1 million. #3037. Coupang vs. Five Below: Which Consumer Stock Is a Better Buy Going Into 2027?

Coupang's cash-rich Korean delivery empire goes head-to-head with Five Below's teen-focused bargain stores, but their valuations tell very different stories.

Investors looking for growth often weigh domestic value against international scale. Choosing between Coupang (CPNG +11.95%) and Five Below (FIVE +6.70%) requires balancing South Korea's e-commerce dominance against a rapidly expanding U.S. discount retailer.

Coupang operates a massive logistics network to deliver everything from groceries to electronics across South Korea and Taiwan. Five Below focuses on high-energy, low-cost treasure hunts for American youth. Both companies aim to capture market share in the evolving retail landscape, though their geographic footprints and operational models offer distinct paths for long-term growth.

Coupang provides retail, restaurant delivery, and video streaming services through brands like Rocket Now and the WOW membership program. The company serves millions of active customers in South Korea and Taiwan, leveraging its own logistics infrastructure to ensure rapid delivery. Its strategic partnership with Sartori Cheese highlights its focus on distributing perishable U.S. goods to international markets.

In its 2025 fiscal year (FY), revenue reached $34.5 billion, marking an increase of approximately 14.1% compared to the prior year. The company reported a net income of $208.0 million for the period. This growth follows a steady upward trajectory in sales despite fluctuating net margin levels in previous fiscal cycles.

As of its December 2025 balance sheet, the debt-to-equity ratio was 1.0x, meaning its total debt is equal to its shareholder equity. The current ratio, which measures the ability to pay short-term obligations with current assets, was 1.0x. For FY 2025, free cash flow reached $522.0 million. Note that stock-based compensation (SBC) represented 26.8% of operating cash flow, which inflates reported cash generation since SBC is a non-cash expense added back in the cash flow statement.

Five Below operates a value-based retail model targeting kids, teens, and pre-teens with trend-right merchandise. The company manages over 2,000 stores across the U.S., offering products in categories like tech, sports, and fashion. It works with roughly 1,000 vendors to maintain its diverse inventory without relying too heavily on any single supplier.

In FY 2025, revenue reached $4.8 billion, representing growth of 22.9% over the previous fiscal year. Net income for the period was $358.6 million, with a net margin of 7.5%. This performance reflects the company's ability to drive sales through both new store openings and existing locations, a key strength among retail stocks.

Based on its January 2026 balance sheet, the debt-to-equity ratio was 0.9x, indicating total debt is slightly lower than shareholder equity. The current ratio stood at 2.0x, indicating a strong capacity to cover near-term liabilities with liquid assets. In FY 2025, the company generated free cash flow of $411.7 million, providing capital for further store expansion.

Five Below is exposed to inflation and rising product costs, which can impact its low-price value proposition. The company depends on merchandise manufactured outside the U.S., making it sensitive to tariffs and trade volatility. Furthermore, it faces legal risks from class action lawsuits regarding growth and inventory management misstatements that could impact future results.

Five Below appears cheaper based on future earnings estimates, while Coupang offers a lower P/S ratio, which measures market value against sales over the past twelve months.

Valuation metrics include sourcing from Financial Modeling Prep (FMP) and may differ from other data providers.

Between retail players Coupang and Five Below, I would buy Five Below. Coupang's e-commerce business relies heavily on its South Korean customers, yet the company suffered a black eye when it was hacked last year, prompting investigations by the Korean government.

In addition, since the incident was revealed in November of 2025, Coupang's sales growth has steadily declined. Before the data breach was revealed, the company's revenue in the third quarter of 2025 grew 18% year over year. That dropped to 11% in Q4 after the incident was publicized. Sales continued falling in 2026, and in Q2, year-over-year sales growth had slowed to 4%.

Coupang saw some recovery in Q3 of this year, as revenue rose 7% year over year to $10 billion. Even so, investors should monitor the situation to see if the company continues to gain steam in subsequent quarters.

Meanwhile, Five Below is enjoying robust growth in 2026 despite the volatile macroeconomic background. In its fiscal Q2, ended Aug. 1, sales grew 23% to $1.3 billion. The company also raised its 2026 full-year revenue outlook to a range between $5.6 billion to $5.7 billion, up from 2025's $4.8 billion. Five Below's business is doing well and shows no signs of slowing down.

Robert Izquierdo has positions in Amazon. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Amazon. The Motley Fool recommends Coupang, Farfetch, and Five Below. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3035. BlackRock's Rick Rieder sees 'historic' opportunity in high-quality bonds

Investors may be wary of the volatility hitting parts of the bond market right now, but they have an "historic" opportunity to grab attractive yields in high-quality credit, Rick Rieder, BlackRock's chief investment officer for global fixed income, told CNBC in an exclusive interview.

Hypothetically, it's now possible for investors to build a diversified portfolio that yields more than 7% with less than three years of duration and an average credit quality of A-, he said. Duration is a measurement of a bond's price sensitivity to fluctuations in rates, and issues with shorter maturities tend to have lower duration.

"Not only is it historic in that these yields are where they are, but the only time these yields are ever here is when there's some problem, like an economic problem or default problem," Rieder said in an interview about his fourth-quarter outlook, released Wednesday.

That isn't the case now, where the economy remains strong, he said. "So you don't really have to worry about defaults of any significance."

While Rieder didn't specifically identify the credits in the aforementioned portfolio, the iShares Aaa - A Rated Corporate Bond ETF (QLTA) has a 30-day SEC yield of 5.68% and an effective duration of 6.48 years — higher than Rieder's low duration focus.

The iShares Flexible Income Active ETF (BINC), which is managed by Rieder, has a 5.66% 30-day SEC yield and three-year effective duration, but its portfolio extends beyond investment-grade credit.

Rieder sees the best opportunity right now in investment-grade corporate bonds — which he pointed out he hasn't said "in a really long time." Investment grade bonds are rated AAA through BBB- by S&P and Fitch, while Moody's rates them Aaa through Baa3.

That includes hyperscaler bonds, which have flooded the market with issuance this year as they ramp up their AI infrastructure. Alphabet, Amazon, Meta Platforms and Oracle have issued a total of about $228.4 billion in bonds year to date, as of Oct. 20, according to LSEG.

"They're very low leverage. They have tremendous amounts of cash flow coming in," Rieder said of the hyperscalers in general. "You're now getting to buy these entities which are higher quality, paying you a very attractive yield to get it."

Rieder's iShares Flexible Income Active ETF has upped its allocation to investment-grade corporates to 14.3% of its portfolio. In July, that stood at 8.5%.

That said, investors need to be patient and sit tight since rates could still move higher from here, he advised.

"Your starting point is by far your best barometer of what your return is going to be," he noted. "So if you get seven and a quarter, maybe you get eight, maybe you get six, but ... if your holding period is a year or longer, you're going to get that yield."

Caution should also be exercised outside of investment-grade credit, which requires more selectivity and deep research, he warned. That includes AI names.

"There's parts of the data center supply that's not hyperscaler guaranteed or hyperscaler backed," he said. "That we've been more cautious around, and we'll continue to be."

The bond market, particularly high-yield credit, has been rocked as investor concern grows over the massive amount of debt some companies are taking out to fund their artificial intelligence build-out.

Junk-rated CoreWeave has become the poster child, seeing its stock drop, bond yields jump and credit-default spreads widen. Oracle, which is sitting in the lower end of investment grade and is seen as a bellwether on the broader AI market, has also taken some hits.

Rieder is also keeping an eye on the Federal Reserve, which is expected to raise its federal funds rate again in December to combat inflation. The market is currently pricing in a nearly 90% probability that the central bank will hike at that time, according to the CME Group FedWatch tool.

The money manager expects that to happen, although he does not agree with it. Higher rates are hurting parts of the economy, like housing, while not having much of an impact on other areas — such as tech companies' capital expenditures, which have become a big driver of growth, he said.

"They're not borrowing off the overnight funds rate," Rieder noted. "There's a lot of floating rate finance in housing, credit card, auto loan finance, and you're seeing those areas already under pressure."

Instead, the market is and will continue to price the AI supply coming into the market, he added.

Rieder sees a number of possible risks ahead, including rising levels of government debt across the globe. In the United States, the budget deficit has grown to $2 trillion for fiscal 2026.

"The more the central banks raise the rates, the cost of debt to these to already indebted governments is something we've got to be careful about over time," he said.

Bond supply coming from AI companies should also be watched.

"With the amount of supply that's coming — data center, hyperscaler — there's some crowding-out effect that's taking place," he said. "With government rates moving higher, that is something that we just got to keep an eye on."

In the meantime, Rieder would just continue to reap the rewards of high investment-grade yields "and smile a lot," he said.

#3033. Celsius vs. Chipotle Mexican Grill: Which Consumer Goods Stock Is a Better Buy for 2027?

Celsius trades at a cheaper valuation but depends heavily on Pepsi for distribution, while Chipotle comes with its own set of risks.

Investors often weigh the hyper-growth of beverage upstarts against the steady expansion of established restaurant chains. Choosing between Celsius (CELH +2.57%) and Chipotle Mexican Grill (CMG +5.83%) requires balancing immediate revenue surges with long-term stability.

Celsius focuses on functional energy drinks that appeal to health-conscious consumers, while Chipotle dominates the fast-casual dining space with its fresh-ingredient philosophy. Both companies rely on strong brand loyalty to navigate competitive retail environments. We will examine their financial health and market risks to determine which aligns better with your 2027 investing goals.

Celsius sells functional beverages through major retailers, fitness centers, and e-commerce platforms. Its growth strategy relies heavily on a partnership with Pepsi, which serves as its primary U.S. distributor and helps manage brands like Rockstar. Customer concentration like this adds a layer of risk to the business, which is something to keep in mind.

In FY 2025, revenue reached nearly $2.5 billion, representing growth of approximately 85.5% over the previous year. This rapid expansion helped the company achieve net income of roughly $108 million.

Chipotle Mexican Grill operates a massive network of company-owned restaurants, focusing on a "food with integrity" mission. According to its latest annual report, filed for the period ending December 31, 2025, the company owned over 4,000 locations globally. It reaches customers through physical stores and digital channels, including platforms owned by Amazon (AMZN +6.12%).

In FY 2025, revenue reached $11.9 billion, a growth of roughly 5.4% compared to the prior fiscal year. Net income for the period was nearly $1.5 billion, resulting in a healthy net margin of approximately 12.9%. This consistent profitability highlights the company's ability to manage costs effectively across its large-scale restaurant operations.

Celsius faces significant revenue concentration with Pepsi, creating a heavy reliance on a single distribution partner for its core business. The company also deals with operational risks from third-party co-packers and intense competition from Monster Beverage (MNST +3.33%). Rapid growth could also lead to brand cannibalization as it integrates acquired businesses or expands its product lines.

Chipotle Mexican Grill is vulnerable to food safety risks that could damage its brand reputation due to its reliance on fresh, unprocessed ingredients. It relies heavily on third-party delivery services and faces pressure from large competitors like McDonald's (MCD +6.53%). Rising labor costs and potential supply chain disruptions also present ongoing challenges for its business model.

Celsius appears more attractively priced based on traditional metrics.

This is an interesting stock battle, as each company could fit into a portfolio to serve different purposes. With Celsius, you're looking at more of a growth stock. It's carving out a niche in the energy drink market, which is expected to be a global industry valued at $157.2 billion, according to Fortune Business Insights. Celsius can continue to capture that growing revenue opportunity by having more products on the shelves, as it acquired the energy drink maker Alani Nutrition in 2025.

Chipotle, on the other hand, is a more mature company, but that comes with its own set of benefits. As we saw earlier, Chipotle generates more revenue than Celsius, hauling in $11.9 billion in fiscal 2025 compared to Celsius's $2.5 billion. It's also capturing more profits, with Chipotle's net income over that period totaling $1.5 billion while Celsius reported $108 million in net income.

Between the two, heading into 2027, I would rather own shares of Chipotle. With plenty of uncertainty still swirling around the markets, Chipotle offers more stability as a more established business, but it also still has revenue growth opportunities. The restaurant chain has a long-term goal of operating 7,000 locations in North America, so that can help sales keep climbing. And while Celsius may have more stock price growth potential ahead, that also comes with greater risk. For example, over the last 12 months, shares of Chipotle are up 9.4%, while shares of Celsius are down nearly 48%. While both are underperforming the S&P 500's 13.6% return over that period, Chipotle's positive returns indicate greater stability than Celsius, a high-risk, high-reward stock.

Jack Delaney has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Amazon, Chipotle Mexican Grill, and Monster Beverage. #3030. Americans have said 'NO TO WAR,' Iran speaker says as Democrats take Congress

Iran's speaker of parliament Mohammad Bagher Ghalibaf said Americans have said "NO TO WAR," as projections from U.S. midterm elections showed that Democrats have flipped both houses of Congress.

Posting on X after projections showed that Democrats will have a majority in the House and Senate over the coming two years, Ghalibaf said that "Conscience or the cost of living, Americans said NO TO WAR, and we endorse this message."

"If the [U.S. government] betrays their public will to serve international paymasters and blackmailers, it will learn our NO comes with a Nightmare."

The X account of Iran's Supreme Leader Ayatollah Mojtaba Khamenei posted: "The process of the US's collapse has accelerated."

"If there are still those who entertain the slightest hope that the hegemonic system may change behavior or shifts in its political parties may bring about change, or that by making concessions to the US, they can persuade it to abandon its hostility, they're gravely mistaken," the account added.

Last Saturday, Khamenei had called for Tehran's armed forces to meet "any level or type of threat" with "an immediate, impactful, and geographically unrestricted response."

The Iran war has been a central issue for voters in the midterms, after U.S. President Donald Trump launched joint strikes with Israel against the Islamic Republic in February. The conflict has pushed up energy prices across the U.S., which has led to cost of living concerns.

Trump is also blaming the Iran war in part for the Democratic sweep of both chambers of Congress, telling the Wall Street Journal that "Iran was a piece of it" when asked if the war contributed to the Republicans losses.

However, he maintains that prosecuting the war was the right decision, saying, "I don't think about it. I did the right thing. I would have done it again."

Washington has not explicitly stated it plans to resume attacks on Iran, but reports from The Wall Street Journal and The New York Times indicate the U.S. is preparing for possible renewed military actions after the midterm elections.

With their new majorities, Democrats might be able to push through a War Powers Resolution, which will direct Trump to end the conflict with Iran should Congress not authorize it.

In June, both chambers — which were then Republican controlled — passed a concurrent resolution to end the conflict, although a concurrent resolution is not binding and does not have the force of law. Previous attempts to impose a binding War Powers Resolution had been defeated.

#3028. Mine, Enrich, Build, Operate: Which Link in the Nuclear Supply Chain Is the Best Stock Right Now?

With demand for electricity growing at a rapid clip, nuclear power is increasingly important.

Oklo (OKLO -5.83%) is looking to build a business around small modular nuclear reactors, putting it at the forefront of the nuclear power industry. NextEra Energy (NEE +4.89%) sells power from nuclear power plants it owns, with its proposed acquisition of Dominion (D +4.45%) set to make it the number two player in nuclear power in the United States. And then there are intermediaries, like fuel supplier Cameco (CCJ -1.21%) or service provider Westinghouse, which is owned by Cameco and Brookfield Renewable Partners (BEP +2.01%).

For most investors, keeping your nuclear power exposure simple and diversified is probably the best choice. And that suggests that NextEra Energy or a competitor like Constellation Energy (CEG -6.71%), the largest U.S. nuclear operator, could be the best option. Here's why.

Oklo is a money-losing start-up with new nuclear technology. Only the most aggressive investors should even consider it. Cameco's business is heavily driven by uranium prices, which can be highly volatile. While its Westinghouse investment should help to provide more reliable revenues, that's just one part of the business. Meanwhile, Brookfield Renewable Partners is really a much broader bet on clean energy. Westinghouse, which it partially owns with Cameco, is a relatively small part of the business.

Meanwhile, NextEra and Constellation are both heavily invested in nuclear power to support the growth in electricity demand. They are more direct plays on the space in some ways, while also offering a more conservative profile. NextEra is a regulated utility, while Constellation sells power under long-term contracts. Both are set to benefit as electricity demand, including demand for their large nuclear operations, is projected to grow 60% between 2025 and 2045, up from just 10% in the prior 20-year period.

That said, Constellation and NextEra are very different businesses. NextEra offers a well-above-market 2.9% yield backed by 31 annual dividend increases. It's probably the best choice for more conservative income investors looking to add nuclear exposure to their portfolios. Constellation's yield is just 0.6%, while selling power at market rates would allow it to benefit more from demand-led price increases. It's more appropriate for growth investors.

While Oklo or Cameco would be more exciting and more closely tied to nuclear's future, they also involve much more risk. Brookfield Renewable also sells power under long-term contracts, but it is really a highly focused play on clean energy. Nuclear giants NextEra and Constellation probably offer the best balance of risk and reward, with the option to lean toward income or growth depending on your preference.

Reuben Gregg Brewer has positions in Brookfield Renewable Partners. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Cameco, Constellation Energy, and NextEra Energy. The Motley Fool recommends Brookfield Renewable Partners and Dominion Energy. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3025. Kraft Heinz: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Rising logistics and fuel costs leave shelf-level pricing as the open question, with productivity gains the first line of defense.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 4, 2026, Kraft Heinz (KHC +3.75%) announced a definitive agreement to divest its North American coffee business to One Rock. The transaction streamlines operations so management can concentrate capital on core brands with higher growth potential. Three key takeaways from the call stand out for long-term investors.

Divesting non-core operations allows management to allocate resources where returns are highest. Kraft Heinz retains a 30% noncontrolling stake in the coffee business, preserving upside while freeing operational focus.

Management is evaluating initial results from its $700 million reinvestment plan across brand lines. While certain promotional tweaks failed to boost some types of sales, data-driven adjustments are refining future trade spending.

Rising logistics expenses and fuel costs are pressuring gross margins heading into next year. Management plans to offset input cost increases through internal productivity gains before implementing targeted price increases.

Freight expenses and commodity pressure are squeezing margins, making internal cost discipline critical. Kraft Heinz relies on trade efficiency and retail partnerships to protect profitability, although price increases next year appear inevitable.

Spring shelf resets and innovative brand extensions offer a foundation for volume recovery. Management tracks market share dynamics as consumer demand evolves.

#3023. Why McDonald's Stock Popped Today

New value menus are bringing bargain hunters back to the burger chain's restaurants.

Shares of McDonald's (MCD +6.53%) jumped more than 6% on Wednesday after the restaurant giant's profits topped investors' expectations.

McDonald's global systemwide sales rose 4% to over $38 billion in the third quarter, driven by new store openings and higher sales at existing locations.

Global comparable sales, which measure revenue at restaurants open for at least thirteen months, grew 1.5%, fueled by growth in international markets. Yet U.S. comps were down slightly by 0.2%.

McDonald's, like many restaurant chains in the U.S., is dealing with slowing traffic as cash-strapped consumers dine out less often. McDonald's is revamping its value-priced offerings in response to this trend. The strategy appears to be working.

"In the U.S., performance improved as the quarter progressed, with comparable sales turning positive in September, following a slightly negative result in July and August," CEO Chris Kempczinski said during a conference call with analysts.

All told, McDonald's adjusted operating income climbed 8%. Its adjusted earnings per share, in turn, jumped 11% to $3.57. That was nicely above Wall Street's estimates of $3.38.

This strengthening profitability enabled the passive-income-producing stalwart to raise its quarterly cash payout to investors by 4% to $1.93 per share. McDonald's has now increased its dividend for a remarkable 50 straight years.

#3022. 5 midterm election takeaways: Tech billionaires suffer as Democrats drub Republicans

Democrats decisively took control of Congress Tuesday night, delivering a stinging rebuke to President Donald Trump and his economic record as voters voiced deep frustration with rising prices, government corruption and the AI data center build-out.

The party scored an extraordinary pair of victories in traditionally Republican Texas. James Talarico defeated Ken Paxton, becoming the first Democrat elected to the U.S. Senate from the state in nearly four decades. Gina Hinojosa defeated three-term Gov. Greg Abbott, making her the first Democratic governor of Texas since Ann Richards left office in 1995. Texas became the clearest measure of how far the blue wave reached: Democrats swept the state's marquee races in a Republican stronghold long considered beyond their grasp.

Follow CNBC's live coverage of the 2026 midterm election results

The victories were part of a broader wave of Democratic gains. In South Carolina, Democrat Annie Andrews upset Darline Graham in a race Republicans had expected to win. In Ohio, Sherrod Brown defeated Republican Sen. Jon Husted. Democrats also flipped Senate seats in Iowa and North Carolina and won key races in Georgia and New Hampshire.

The results marked a striking collapse for Trump's candidates. Paxton, Graham and Husted were among the president's prominent picks who lost after he made dozens of endorsements.

The election also sharpened the political fight over data centers and artificial intelligence infrastructure. Texas is a major center of planned data-center construction, and Hinojosa campaigned for a pause on new projects until the state adopts tougher rules. Her victory gives Democrats more influence over Texas' approach to the build-out, though the state Legislature remains in Republican hands.

The results also delivered a blow to the tech billionaires and corporate interest groups that poured hundreds of millions of dollars into backing Republican candidates who might be friendly to the massive AI data center projects they hope to build around the country. Elon Musk, the world's richest man and the cycle's largest individual political donor, spent record sums in a failed effort to help Republicans retain power, including in Texas.

Musk's spending may have become a liability as voters raised concerns about government corruption and the growing influence of billionaires in exit polls. Talarico repeatedly attacked Musk on the campaign trail, accusing him of trying to buy "his very own personal United States Senator."

"Tonight's results are a wake-up call for corporate special interests and billionaires who have been rigging the economy," said Rep. Chris Pappas, who defeated former Republican Sen. John Sununu to win an open Senate seat in New Hampshire.

The outcome is poised to reshape the technology sector's relationship with Washington. A more adversarial Congress is likely to intensify scrutiny of tech companies and the AI data center build-out. Markets reacted as the scale of Democratic gains became clear: Dow futures fell and S&P 500 futures slipped Tuesday evening.

Economic frustration was also central to Democratic gains. Many voters blamed Trump's tariffs and the war with Iran for worsening their economic situation, while the cost of living remained a persistent strain.

Democratic control of both chambers gives the party a powerful check on Trump in the final two years of his term, including the ability to block parts of his agenda and intensify congressional oversight. The results sent a clear warning: promises to bring down costs did not overcome voters' frustration with prices, corruption and the country's direction.

Silicon Valley spent record sums trying to shape the election. Instead, it got a Democratic House promising to haul its CEOs before congressional investigators.

Many of the winners ran against not just Trump, but the billionaire donors who helped bankroll Republicans. In North Carolina, Cooper told supporters that North Carolinians "will always matter more than the billionaires and the insiders."

In Texas, Talarico defeated Paxton despite more than $200 million from SpaceX CEO Musk to stop him. On the same ballot, Hinojosa defeated Abbott after blaming him for the rapid buildout of data centers across Texas, saying the governor for putting Big Tech interests ahead of local communities.

The GOP had a commanding advantage among the election's biggest donors. According to a CNBC analysis of Federal Election Commission records, 14 of the 20 largest donor groups backed Republicans exclusively or nearly so. Crypto, AI, and online gambling interests poured more than $377 million into races.

Not all of Silicon Valley's money ended up on the losing side. LinkedIn co-founder Reid Hoffman, one of Democrats' biggest donors, cast the results as "an overwhelming wave against Trump and his cronies" and took a direct shot at Musk.

"We should get money out of politics," Hoffman wrote on X late Tuesday. "And while people like Elon are spending outrageous amounts to keep authoritarian clowns in power, those of us who believe in the ideals of America cannot yield."

With control of the House comes the power to launch investigations, issue subpoenas and examine the Trump administration and its allies. That's going to be a challenge for tech companies to navigate as they find themselves in Democratic crosshairs.

Democrats are already calling for AI executives to testify under oath.

Among the first to seize on the results was Rep. Greg Casar, the Texas Democrat who chairs the Congressional Progressive Caucus.

"The House Democratic majority must make it a top priority to protect Americans' jobs, freedom, and lives from AI billionaires," Casar said in a statement after Democrats were projected to win the House. "AI company CEOs should be called to testify under oath."

Rep. Robert Garcia, D-Calif., the presumptive next chair of the oversight committee, put it more simply: "Lawyer up," he said in a statement.

The results also present headwinds for the infrastructure undergirding AI.

The Texas results appear to be a warning sign for the AI industry in a state projected to overtake Virginia as the world's biggest data center market by 2030, according to JLL. Abbott had long courted data center development before shifting his position this year as the backlash grew. Talarico and Hinojosa both seized on that anger, winning over Republican voters fed up with data centers going up in their communities. Hinojosa has promised to force lawmakers to regulate the industry.

In a CNBC Voter Poll, 56% of voters said data centers were a crucial or major factor in their decision, with Democrats ranking the issue higher than Republicans.

Trump personally pushed states to redistrict their congressional seats mid-decade to cement a Republican majority in the House. The effort largely flopped.

Many of the GOP's redrawn districts ended up falling to Democrats, despite the unprecedented effort to defend or flip them by redrawing boundaries mid-decade.

That includes seats in Texas, Florida and North Carolina, which Democrats won despite Republicans making them safer. In Texas, where Republicans began their redistricting war that then spread to other states, Democrats ended up winning most of the gerrymandered seats.

The redistricting push was always risky because no recent census was available when the maps were drawn.

Trump rolled out the red carpet for Paxton, only for Republicans to lose a Senate seat in Texas for the first time in over three decades.

Talarico's victory in Texas came after Trump endorsed Paxton over the incumbent Sen. John Cornyn, R-Texas, who held the seat for decades. In endorsing Paxton, Trump said Cornyn had not been sufficiently loyal.

Paxton prevailed in a bitter primary, and Trump's move to tip the scales spurned Senate Republicans, who saw Cornyn as a better and less expensive candidate than Paxton — who had a laundry list of scandals that plagued his campaign.

Sen. Darline Graham, R-S.C., who Trump handpicked to replace her late brother Lindsey Graham, fell to Democrat Annie Andrews. And Republican Michael Whatley lost to former Gov. Roy Cooper in North Carolina.

Democrats linked voter concerns over the cost of living to corruption in government in the lead-up to their colossal victory. The mix of the two helped propel corruption to be a top voter issue rather than a downballot concern.

Public polling indicated before Tuesday night that the issue of corruption was landing with voters. CNBC's All-America Survey conducted in mid-October found corruption was voters' top concern. Exit polls on Tuesday also tied the two issues: 69% said benefits flow to the wealthy in CNBC's Voter Poll.

The issue of corruption has exploded into public view during the second Trump term, as reports of Trump family business deals and the president's own ballooning wealth while in office dominate headlines.

In victory speeches across the country, Democrats made clear they are committed to battling a "rigged" system — a line reminiscent of once-radical Bernie Sanders presidential campaigns.

"We must get big money out of our politics, so no billionaire or trillionaire can buy our elections. We must unrig this economic system that only benefits the top one percent,"  Talarico said. "The real fight in this country is not left versus right. It is top versus bottom."

#3019. Toast: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

The 41% overtime cut in early scheduling tests shows the AI agents are already trimming costs, not just promising to.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 3, 2026, Toast (TOST +5.49%) announced that recurring gross profit streams grew 27% year over year. The restaurant software vendor is expanding into automated agentic platform tools that directly manage labor scheduling and customer marketing. These automated service agents target customer spending categories beyond traditional administrative software. Three takeaways from the call detail this operational strategy.

Toast is building artificial intelligence (AI) software agents to automate back-office restaurant tasks such as labor scheduling and marketing.
