Meanwhile, AI-driven automated support tools helped expand subscription gross margins by 320 basis points year-over-year.

Profitability momentum in the core restaurant business is funding long-term bets in retail and international locations. Management maintains strict capital allocation discipline by keeping customer acquisition payback periods under 20 months while expanding core operating margins.

Future compounding relies on leveraging a single technological architecture across multiple customer categories. The platform foundation allows new market segments to scale with higher capital efficiency while preserving core transaction momentum.

#3014. Biotech stocks are surging. How investors can play the new drug innovation boom

Moderna gave investors a scare as its stock plunged on Thursday. But it's still one of the hottest biotechs this year. And it's hardly alone – the sector is on a roll and looks poised for more gains.

The iShares Biotechnology ETF (IBB) is up 24% through October 23, building on a 28% gain in 2025. Two dozen of the ETF's 233 holdings have more than doubled year-to-date. Moderna leads the pack with a 587% surge — even after an 18% decline following a leak of clinical data for its cancer vaccine (which was actually positive).

Analysts say a new biotech cycle is underway, fueled by clinical trial results, M&A activity, and promising technologies such as genomics, mRNA — and AI.

"We're in the midst of a powerful innovation cycle," said John Hall, portfolio manager of the T. Rowe Price Biotechnology ETF. "What's super exciting is we're seeing newer modalities start to make it to market." Companies "understand the disease biology better," he adds, and that could ultimately lead to more novel drugs making it to market.

Investors are excited about technologies like mRNA and genomics, along with a steady stream of new drug approvals. The FDA approved about a dozen novel biologic drugs in 2025, out of 46 approvals in total. Roughly 66 novel drugs are in the pipeline for FDA approval, including potential blockbusters like Moderna and Merck's cancer drug for melanoma, and a next-generation obesity drug from Eli Lilly.

Analysts say AI could also lead to more approvals as companies deploy the software to speed up drug development.

"Biotech is a phenomenal application for artificial intelligence and machine learning," says Piper Sandler analyst Ted Tenthoff. "You can set up exciting predictive models that, frankly, can do things that we've never been able to do in the space in terms of novel drug discovery."

Granted, the industry is recovering from a fallow period. The sector's last strong performance was during the Covid era when ultra-low interest rates encouraged investment in early-stage drug developers, and mRNA vaccines fueled a revenue surge for companies like Moderna, Pfizer and Biontech.

The sector then went cold as many early-stage companies fizzled. Demand for Covid vaccines evaporated, and surging interest rates put a chill in IPOs and M&A.

The risks haven't evaporated – this is still a sector whose products take years to develop, resulting in far more failures than successes. But analysts say the outlook is healthier now. While that's reflected in strong performance and richer valuations, there's still good reason to invest.

Recent clinical trial results are stoking excitement that novel drugs in the works could tackle some of the toughest problems in medicine.

Moderna and Merck's melanoma drug, for instance, uses mRNA to trigger an immune response in patients, targeting tumors that have been surgically removed and genetically analyzed. It's not a preventive vaccine, but it uses a similar immunology mechanism and showed a 42% reduction in cancer recurrence or death (used in conjunction with Merck's cancer drug Keytruda).

While the Phase 3 results disappointed Wall Street, oncologists say the outcomes were still highly positive. And they see the technology as a potential game changer, ushering in an era of personalized treatments for cancers. Moderna and Merck have said they aim to submit the drug for FDA approval and get it launched in 2027.

Inflammation treatment is another promising area. AstraZeneca and Amgen recently released positive Phase 3 results for another use for their drug Tezspire, an injectable approved for nasal polyps and asthma. In clinical trials, it showed that it successfully relieved swallowing symptoms and healed microscopic and visual tissue damage in patients with severe throat inflammation.

Roche, meanwhile, notched a win after the FDA approved its drug Enspryng, a therapy to treat rare inflammatory and autoimmune diseases. It became the first and only at-home injection for adults with thyroid eye disease.

Obesity drugs are also advancing beyond the first generation of GLP-1s. Eli Lilly's retatrutide, a "triple agonist" targeting three different hormone receptors, cleared two more clinical trials over the summer. The drug produced 28%-30% weight loss at the highest doses in the study, Lilly said. The company plans to submit the drug for FDA approval in the first quarter of 2027.

Much of the innovation isn't just happening in drug-making – it's also in areas like gene sequencing, mRNA, and high-tech instruments.

Illumina has more than doubled this year. The DNA and gene sequencing company is seeing accelerating demand for its NovaSeq X high-end sequencing platform. Pharmaceutical companies including Bristol-Myers Squibb, Merck and Kura are working with Illumina to develop precision oncology solutions.

Another big winner is 10x Genomics, up more than 300%. The biotech firm offers its lab instruments to help scientists research cancer, immunology and neuroscience. The company's Atera platform is helping produce data sets to train AI models, a process that helps accelerate personalized therapies.

On the AI front, companies are using the technology for more efficient drug discovery, says Shreefāl Mehta, president of Guidant BioTherapeutics. Using AI, "you can now bring a drug to clinical trials with less capital. More investment is also going into companies that are demonstrating that," he said.

Global AI in drug discovery will grow from a $20 billion market in 2025 to more than $160 billion by 2035, according to forecasts by Towards Health, a research and consulting firm.

One other catalyst for the sector could be M&A. Dealmaking for biopharma hit $134 billion in the first half of 2026, up from $112 billion for all of 2025, notes Jeet Mukherjee, biotech analyst at BTIG. That's a positive signal, indicating more drugs in development are showing clinical promise.

It also helps that Big Pharma has a "patent cliff" ahead. Products representing $180 billion of revenue will lose exclusivity by 2030, according to research firm RTW Institute. Companies with looming expiries "are looking for novel products and innovative products to put into their pipeline," Mehta says.

GLP-1 blockbusters have also left some companies flush with cash for acquisitions. Eli Lilly alone has completed 10 acquisition deals in 2026 worth $25 billion.

ETFs can capture the investing theme, though they aren't all alike. The iShares Biotech ETF is one of the lower cost funds. It tracks a market-cap weighted index of biotech stocks, led by companies like Gilead Sciences, Amgen, Vertex Pharmaceuticals, and Moderna.

The State Street SPDR S&P Biotech ETF holds stocks in equal weight, offering more exposure to small- and mid-cap names. Its top holdings are Moderna, Natera, Twist Bioscience and Halozyme Therapeutics.

Piper Sandler calls Illumina and 10x Genomics the "primary infrastructure plays" in the life science arena, saying their technologies are "driving the next generation of precision medicine."

Piper analyst David Westenberg has a buy rating and $305 target on Illumina, implying 14% gains from recent prices around $267. He has a hold rating on 10X with an $80 target, above recent prices around $70.

Twist Bioscience is another name to consider. The company makes customized synthetic DNA and sells DNA sequencing tools. Twist has partnered with Eli Lilly's AI and machine-learning platform and linked up with Anthropic in August to start testing its AI model, Claude, for protein development.

Twist isn't expected to turn an EPS profit until 2029, according to consensus forecasts. But revenue is expected to go from $572 million in 2027 to $833 million in 2029. Analysts have an average target of $168 on the stock, implying 21% returns from recent prices around $138.

In the small-cap space, Piper Sandler's Tenthoff likes Monte Rosa Therapeutics, a pioneer in protein degradation that selectively destroys disease-causing proteins.

The firm has partnered with Novartis to develop a drug for inflammatory conditions and autoimmune diseases. The companies announced a Phase 2 clinical study in September for an autoimmune disease treatment, which has been "triggering milestones to Monte Rosa," Tenthoff said.

Tenthoff has a buy rating and $37 target on the stock, which recently traded around $10.70.

Also in the small-cap arena, BTIG's Mukherjee recommends Faeth Therapeutics.

The biotech is developing a novel two-drug regimen to treat breast cancer and other tumors. The regimen "has the potential to address safety and dosing limitations of another recently approved drug for breast cancer," he says. And it could become "a mainstay of the future breast cancer treatment paradigm and other cancers."

Faeth is a clinical-stage company that has yet to bring a drug to market. Yet the stock is up 93% year-to-date on positive clinical trial results, trading around $18. Mukherjee has a $55 target on it.

Kymera Therapeutics is worth considering too. The company is developing an oral version of the blockbuster drug Dupixent (made and co-developed by Regeneron Pharmaceuticals and Sanofi). Dupixent is an injectable, however, and oral agents "are frequently preferred by patients," Mukherjee says. "The drug has the potential to not only take share from patients taking Dupixent for numerous immunological diseases but also expanding the market to those not taking systemic biologic drugs," he tells CNBC.

Kymera has said it doesn't expect a patent fight over Dupixent because its drug is distinct, not a biosimilar or biologic copy.

T. Rowe's Hall also likes Kymera for its potential "to unlock disease targets that have been classically considered un-druggable." The stock is the second largest holding in T. Rowe's Biotech ETF, accounting for 4.2% of the portfolio.

#3013. Better Nuclear Energy Stock to Buy After the Sell-Off: Oklo vs. BWX Technologies

Nuclear stocks are in a sell-off, and there might be opportunities for investors at these prices.

Electricity demand is rising due to artificial intelligence (AI), and nuclear energy is set to meet it. At least, if you look at the charts of nuclear power stocks last year, that's what you would assume was the case. Now, not so much.

Nuclear power stocks have faltered in 2026, even though the AI infrastructure build-out continues. Oklo (OKLO -5.83%) is down 86% from highs, while BWX Technologies (BWXT -7.83%) is down 45%. Is either nuclear energy-related stock a buy after this sell-off? It is clear when you look closer at the numbers.

You may have never heard of BWX Technologies, but it has been a mainstay in the nuclear energy field for decades. It builds nuclear reactors for the U.S. Navy, powering aircraft carriers and submarines. These contracts have long lead times and span multiple decades, meaning BWX Technologies can generate reliable revenue from them for many years. It is also working on futuristic nuclear power technologies for the U.S., including space-based nuclear propulsion and microreactors.

On the commercial side, BWX has a durable business selling components to the nuclear power market in Canada and is trying to sell more components for small modular reactors (SMRs), already having contracts with GE Hitachi and other players, although it is still very early days for the segment. Commercial revenue grew 25% year over year to $314 million.

Overall, revenue grew 10% year over year in the third quarter to $953 million. It is expected to generate around $350 million in free cash flow this year, implying a price-to-free cash flow (P/FCF) ratio of 34x based on its current market cap. Next year, free cash flow is expected to be at a similar level, which is perhaps why BWX Technologies is down this year, but the long-term growth trajectory for its place in the nuclear power sector is rock solid.

Speaking of SMRs, Oklo is a start-up trying to bring this technology to life. The company has raised billions of dollars to build a modern SMR business for power, fuel, and isotopes. It wants to have nuclear reactor facilities directly built for AI data centers.

Oklo does not have any reactors built today. In fact, it does not have a nuclear reactor design officially approved by the Nuclear Regulatory Commission (NRC). Without NRC approval, it will not be able to build actual nuclear reactors.

This puts the business many years away from full operations. It is working to produce nuclear fuel and isotopes, which may add some revenue to the business, but the core thesis centers on Oklo's Aurora Powerhouse nuclear reactor, which it hopes to sell by the boatload in the years ahead to power AI data centers and other electricity needs in the United States.

Right now, Oklo's revenue is basically zero. It will likely stay there for many years. Even if it gets approval from the NRC tomorrow, it will take years before a nuclear reactor is actually built and operational.

Oklo's stock has crashed this year, much more than BWX Technologies'. It has a market cap of $4.3 billion, compared with $12 billion for BWX Technologies.

Even though Oklo is now at a much lower price, BWX Technologies looks like a better buy because of its diversified bets across the nuclear supply chain. The military contracts will remain steady, and its forays into new commercial opportunities are potentially promising if SMRs truly are the future of the sector.

For this reason -- along with the lack of revenue generation at Oklo -- BWX Technologies is a better stock to buy on the nuclear energy sell-off.

Brett Schafer has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends BWX Technologies. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3011. Voters rebuff data centers in every ballot measure except one during the 2026 midterms

The artificial intelligence data center boom hit a roadblock on election night, further complicating the tech industry's historic expansion plans.

There were 41 local ballot measures in 10 states up for a vote on Tuesday that would place restrictions on the construction of data centers in different ways, according to Ballotpedia.

Voters sided against data centers in each measure except one in Harwood, North Dakota, according to an analysis by Ballotpedia and additional results gathered by CNBC on Wednesday. The lone exception was Harwood, North Dakota, where voters rejected an added review of industrial projects, including data centers.

Ohio alone accounted for more than half of the measures, with 21 on the ballot. The issue also played out at the top of the ticket, where Democrat Sherrod Brown ousted Republican Sen. Jon Husted after casting the incumbent as "the face of data centers."

The Buckeye State, which was named America's Top State for Business in 2026 by CNBC, has become a key battleground for the issue.

OpenAI announced in August that it secured 8 gigawatts of computing capacity at the PORTS-Pike Technology Campus in Ohio, the largest planned AI data center campus in the world. The first phase of construction is slated to begin this year, and capacity is expected to come online in phases starting in 2028, according to the project's website.

SoftBank and SB Energy are developing the Ohio data center campus in partnership with the U.S. Department of Energy. SoftBank is a major investor in OpenAI, and the two companies announced plans to invest $1 billion in SB Energy earlier this year.

Nvidia is backstopping the project, which could cost more than $500 billion in total.

Those plans could now run into resistance from the governor's mansion. Ohio's top office flipped blue on Tuesday, as Democrat Amy Acton, a physician, defeated Republican businessman Vivek Ramaswamy. Acton supports a "conditional moratorium" on data centers to ensure they cover 100% of utility costs and "exclusively build with union labor."

Heading into the election, the AI trade was already faltering on Wall Street, with chipmakers, neoclouds and other data center-related companies selling off in October on concerns about rising interest rates and overspending. The hyperscalers — Alphabet, Amazon, Microsoft and Meta — are each spending hundreds of billions of dollars a year on AI infrastructure to meet what they say is insatiable demand. All of those companies except Microsoft have turned cash flow negative in the process.

Getting data center projects off the ground may have just gotten a lot harder.

Communities in California, Florida, Illinois, Kansas, Maine, New Jersey, Nevada and Wisconsin also went to the polls to weigh in on the issue, according to Ballotpedia, and data centers were a key motivating factor for voters in matters up and down the ballot.

In a CNBC Voter Poll, 56% of voters said data centers were a crucial or major factor in their decision, with Democrats ranking the issue higher than Republicans.

Democrats who campaigned on pausing or regulating new projects won key races in Ohio, Wisconsin, Georgia and Texas, which is on track to become the world's largest data center market by 2030.

In one of the most significant results of the night for Democrats, Gina Hinojosa unseated Gov. Greg Abbott after blaming the Republican incumbent for the rapid buildout of data centers across Texas, saying he put the tech industry's interests ahead of local communities.

The losses came despite record spending from Silicon Valley to shape the 2026 midterms, with the GOP boasting a commanding advantage among the election's biggest donors. According to a CNBC analysis of Federal Election Commission records, 14 of the 20 largest donor groups backed Republicans almost exclusively. Crypto, AI and online gambling interests poured in more than $377 million.

Elon Musk, the cycle’s largest individual donor, spent at least $474 million, nearly half of it in Texas, and lost both of the state’s marquee races. It was a warning sign for the wave of tech companies and founders who left California for Texas in search of a friendlier business climate.

"Data centers in space looking pretty appealing right now," Musk wrote in a post on X.

Within hours of the polls closing, Democrats were already calling for AI executives to testify under oath.

Among the first to seize on the results was Rep. Greg Casar, the Texas Democrat who chairs the Congressional Progressive Caucus.

"The House Democratic majority must make it a top priority to protect Americans' jobs, freedom, and lives from AI billionaires," Casar said in a statement after Democrats were projected to win the House. "AI company CEOs should be called to testify under oath."

Casar also accused Republicans of being "bought and paid for by AI billionaires," arguing that voters had rejected that influence and warning Democrats to "hold them accountable instead of cashing their checks."

He said the data center fight is only the beginning.

"The revolt against data centers was one of the top issues of the 2026 election," he said. "And in a few years we will look back on it as just the early tremor of a much bigger AI earthquake upending American politics."

Rep. Robert Garcia, D-Calif., the presumptive next chair of the House Oversight Committee, summed it up in two words: "Lawyer up."

— CNBC's Ashley Capoot contributed to this article.

#3009. Coupang: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Korean retail margins are pegged to a mid-2027 recovery, while Taiwan logistics spending is being pulled forward ahead of demand.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 3, 2026, Coupang (CPNG +11.95%) announced consolidated net revenues of $10 billion. The performance shows how the online retailer expands its international footprint while working through temporary margin pressures. Three things management said stand out.

Developing Offerings, the segment that houses international expansion and delivery services, accelerated growth as customer adoption increased. Management pulled forward logistics capital outlays to establish fulfillment capacity ahead of local retail demand.

Product Commerce, the core online retail business in Korea, faced margin compression from promotional spend and fixed operational costs. Management expects profitability to return to historical levels as elevated marketing tapering takes effect.

Automated packaging technology and dense logistics networks reduce per-order costs while speeding order processing. Expanding Rocket Fresh, the nationwide grocery delivery service, deepens shopping frequency across the customer base.

Customer spending remains consistent across core cohorts, and management isn't hiding its satisfaction with underlying shopping behavior. Coupang leverages its owned last-mile delivery network and automated centers while maintaining disciplined marketing spending.

The business continues scaling overseas operations while maintaining delivery profitability. Capital allocation focuses on long-term shareholder returns and customer engagement.

#3007. What Trump losing power over Congress means for Iran, Ukraine and foreign policy

With Democrats taking control of both houses of Congress, U.S. President Donald Trump could intensify his focus on foreign policy in his final two years in office, while facing greater scrutiny over the wars in Iran and Ukraine, experts told CNBC.

Democrats will have a Senate majority and won control of the House, giving them greater scope to scrutinize the administration, shape spending and challenge Trump's foreign-policy agenda.

"We are going to see a president that is somewhat constrained on matters of oversight and investigation, but on foreign policy, he does still retain a lot of latitude," Laurel Rapp, director of Chatham House's U.S. and North America Programme, told CNBC's "Squawk Box Europe."

Rapp said the Iran war was a "major issue" in this election cycle amid soaring fuel prices, food costs, and inflation. The war began when Trump launched joint strikes with Israel on Feb. 28.

"Republicans don't really have an off-ramp here at this stage. We know the Democrats will seek to take votes in the House and the Senate to constrain the Republicans, to restrain the administration on Iran war powers. But whether that will bind the administration is another matter," Rapp added.

Democrats could use Congress' spending powers to try to restrict funding for the war, she added. Any bill directing Trump to end U.S. involvement would need two-thirds support in both chambers to override a presidential veto.

In a Wednesday note, Berenberg's chief economist, Holger Schmieding, predicted a final round of bombing in Iran, with Trump ultimately striking a deal to reopen the Strait of Hormuz by 2027, as voters feel the sting from high prices and fuel costs.

Mitchell Reiss, a fellow with defense and security think tank RUSI, predicted that Trump would replace Secretary of Defense Pete Hegseth following the results.

"Traditionally, presidents do replace cabinet officials. Certainly, after midterm results like this one, if you remember, George W. Bush replaced Donald Rumsfeld as Secretary of Defense after midterm losses, so that wouldn't be a surprise," Reiss told CNBC on Wednesday.

J.C. Lintzenich, visiting senior fellow at the German Marshall Fund, said that the president is not a "lame duck."

"Remember that this election changes the Congress; it does not change the president," Lintzenich told CNBC's "Squawk Box Europe" on Wednesday.

"The President of the United States still has lots of power, lots of authority. We still have pending action in Iran. We still have an unresolved situation with Russia and Ukraine."

He added that while Congress has changed, foreign policy is still executed by the president and his appointed officials.

"What we'll now see is a little bit more congressional oversight, the application of funding, for example, Ukraine, that's required by statute and law," Lintzenich said.

Lintzenich said the outgoing Congress was "more willing to let the White House slide," but Democratic control of both chambers would mean a more assertive Congress, using its Article I powers over legislation, spending, and oversight to hold the White House more closely to existing law and congressional requirements.

Berenberg's Schmieding warned that Trump could face "more trouble ahead" as Democrats take control of the Senate.

He said Congress was unlikely to fund "an expensive campaign similar to the Iran war, but added that Trump, "as a perennial attention seeker without a consistent long-term strategy," may be "even more eager to pursue dangerous geopolitical adventures."

Speaking as voting took place on Tuesday, Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy said the U.S. midterm elections would "shape the future."

"The world will be watching," he said in Berlin. "Right now, things are deeply uncertain. Many hopes may once again end in disappointment."

Meanwhile, the Kremlin said it hoped the outcome of the midterms would not impact Trump's efforts to broker a peace deal between Ukraine and Russia, Reuters reported, citing local media.

The results could complicate Trump's ability to secure congressional backing or funding for a deal, but they do not prevent him from pressing Kyiv diplomatically. Berenberg's Schmieding said Trump could try to push Ukraine toward a deal favorable to Russia, though he considers that a "tail risk."

Schmieding said Trump's influence over Republicans could weaken after the midterm defeat, noting that Republicans, including Secretary of State Marco Rubio, are warier of Putin. "That can help to contain the risk of Trump trying to impose an unacceptable deal on Ukraine," he said.

If Trump did pressure Ukraine, Europe would likely increase its support, Schmieding said, but it could not fully replace critical U.S. military and intelligence assistance.

Christel Schaldemose, a vice president of the European Parliament, said the result "could benefit the support for Ukraine" but added: "The EU must continue the course of standing stronger and standing on its own two feet."

#3005. AMD Stock Jumped After Its Report. I'd Wait for a Lower Price to Buy.

The chipmaker now expects its revenue to rise over 80% next year. What's a fair price to pay for that?

Advanced Micro Devices (AMD +1.01%) gave investors a big number on Tuesday, Nov. 3. Along with its third-quarter results, management said on the earnings call that it now expects revenue to rise over 80% in 2027. That's more than double the annual growth in the long-term model AMD set out at its financial analyst day last November.

Shares jumped. After closing Tuesday at $483.90, the stock gained about 7.5% in after-hours trading to roughly $520. The stock has given back most of that gain since, and at around $490 as I write, it's about 25% under $649.42, the record close on Oct. 6.

On Oct. 27, I wrote that I'd hold a small position in AMD through 2030, though I wouldn't expect the stock to triple again. The report doesn't change that long view. What it does change is how much weight I'd give next year's earnings -- enough to set a price on them.

Showing how fast the company is scaling, AMD's third-quarter revenue climbed 45% year over year to $13.4 billion, and non-GAAP (adjusted) earnings per share jumped 74% to $2.09.

The data center segment, which sells server processors and artificial intelligence (AI) chips, powered most of that growth. Its revenue almost doubled, climbing 97% from a year ago to a record $8.5 billion, up from $6.7 billion in the second quarter and $5.8 billion in the first. Notably, its quarter-to-quarter growth picked up from 16% in the second quarter to 27% in the latest one. And the segment now makes up 64% of AMD's revenue, up from 47% a year ago.

The fourth-quarter forecast is even steeper.

AMD expects around $17 billion in revenue, up about 66% from a year earlier and 27% from the third quarter. Management expects growth to speed up as AMD scales production of Helios, its rack-scale AI system.

Earnings are soaring, but the cash isn't keeping up. AMD's free cash flow dropped to $554 million in the third quarter, or around 4% of revenue, down from $1.5 billion in the year-ago quarter. And it has fallen in both of the past two quarters, from around $2.6 billion in the first quarter to $1.6 billion in the second.

Most of the gap between profit and cash went to inventory. AMD's stockpile grew around $3 billion from June to September, to $11.5 billion, bringing this year's build to about $3.5 billion. Capital spending is climbing, too. AMD spent around $650 million on property and equipment in the third quarter, up from about $260 million a year ago.

And management expects to keep spending on both.

"We expect to continue to invest in inventory and capacity to support our strong growth in 2027," CFO Jean Hu said on the call.

Put simply, AMD is paying up front for next year's sales. Yes, that's arguably the right move for a company whose CEO, Lisa Su, said demand is outpacing supply. But it also means earnings come in well before cash does.

Of course, if customers put off their deployments, $11.5 billion of inventory might become a problem.

On average, analysts expect AMD's adjusted earnings per share to hit about $16 in 2027. That's around double the $8 or so it looks on track to earn this year: $5.12 through September, plus about $2.90 implied by its fourth-quarter guidance for revenue, margins, spending, tax and share count.

Management's forecast makes the 2027 estimate look reachable. Besides the revenue growth of over 80%, Hu said AMD expects operating expenses next year to rise at under half that pace, which could lift its profit margin.

At around $490, the stock has a price-to-earnings ratio of about 31 on AMD's expected 2027 earnings. For comparison, the forward price-to-earnings ratio for the S&P 500 (^GSPC +1.58%), based on the next 12 months of expected earnings, is about 19. AMD can justify a hefty premium to that if its earnings double as expected. Still, I don't want to pay much more than around 30 times expected 2027 earnings, mostly because of the cash.

At 30 times earnings, my buy price is about $480. That's near Tuesday's close and a little below where the stock trades now.

Overall, the report made me more confident about AMD's 2027 outlook. But the company still needs to convert those earnings into cash, and the stock already prices in much of that growth.

At or below $480, I think AMD stock is worth considering for a small position. At roughly $490, I'd wait.

Daniel Sparks and his clients do not have positions in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Advanced Micro Devices. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#3003. Intuitive Machines and Rocket Lab: A Comparison of Spiking Sales Versus Steady Revenue

Intuitive Machines went flat for two years before tripling revenue, while Rocket Lab grew steadily -- but the gap is closing fast.

Intuitive Machines (LUNR -5.25%) primarily generates revenue by developing specialized aerospace products and delivering support services for robotic space exploration.

Between August and November, it secured tracking satellite contracts for the Space Development Agency and the Jet Propulsion Laboratory, and it completed the in-orbit commissioning of an SXM-11 spacecraft, while also booking a commercial order for two platform systems.

Rocket Lab (RKLB -3.79%) primarily earns revenue by providing orbital launch vehicle services and manufacturing advanced spacecraft components for global clients.

During the past three months, it completed its 99th mission for its Electron rocket, and finalized its largest commercial Electron launch contract to date, encompassing 20 launches. It also advanced its pending acquisition of Iridium Communications after stockholders approved the transaction and simultaneously adjusted the target launch timing for its developing Neutron vehicle.

Revenue shows investors the total money a business brings in before expenses. This metric reveals whether an organization is successfully attracting customers and growing its overall business volume over time.

Intuitive Machines vs. Rocket Lab Revenue over the last 8 quarters.

Data source: Financial Modeling Prep. Data as of Nov. 2, 2026.

The revenue trends for Intuitive Machines and Rocket Lab speak volumes about each's space-based businesses. Rocket Lab's steady sales growth over the past two years demonstrates its diversified revenue streams. While some of its income relies on government contracts, it has significant commercial launch sales.

Rocket Lab's success is evidenced by its consistent quarter-over-quarter top-line expansion since the start of 2025. This trend is expected to continue into the third quarter, with management forecasting revenue between $250 million and $265 million. The company's impending acquisition of Iridium Communications only adds to its aggressive revenue growth.

Intuitive Machines is heavily reliant on U.S. government contracts, leading to lumpy quarterly sales results. Its massive revenue jump in 2026 was driven by contract wins with agencies such as NASA, contributing to the company's record backlog of $1.8 billion at the end of the second quarter.

That said, Intuitive Machines' 2026 sales explosion was also helped by acquisitions, which should accelerate its commercial revenue growth. This will shift the company from a purely government-dependent lunar lander specialist into a diversified commercial space infrastructure provider.

#3000. IonQ vs. SoundHound AI: Which Technology Stock Is a Better Buy for 2027?

IonQ trades at a premium, while SoundHound AI shows a clearer path to profitability.

Emerging technology sectors offer high growth potential but often carry significant risks for early investors. Let's explore whether IonQ (IONQ -1.50%) or SoundHound AI (SOUN +4.85%) is the better long-term choice for your portfolio.

IonQ is a leader in quantum computing, a field that uses subatomic physics to perform calculations beyond the reach of traditional computers. SoundHound AI specializes in conversational voice technology for various consumer and commercial applications. Both companies are in their early expansion phases and represent different ways to play the broader artificial intelligence (AI) trend.

IonQ specializes in trapped-ion quantum computing, selling hardware access and networking solutions to a variety of commercial and government clients. Its technology is available through major cloud platforms like Amazon (AMZN +6.12%), Microsoft (MSFT +6.68%), and the marketplace for Alphabet. Customer concentration like this adds a layer of risk to the business. The company also operates within the broader semiconductor stocks category by developing specialized hardware for quantum sensing and positioning applications.

In FY 2025, revenue reached nearly $130 million, representing a significant increase of approximately 201.9% compared to the previous year. Despite this rapid growth, the company reported a net loss of close to $510.4 million for the period. The company is still spending heavily to develop its technology and scale its workforce.

SoundHound AI provides conversational voice software that powers digital assistants in cars, restaurants, and consumer devices. The company expanded its reach in the technology sector by acquiring LivePerson in 2026. This move helps the firm offer a more comprehensive platform for customer service and automated voice interactions. Its strategy focuses on building long-term partnerships with original equipment manufacturers and hospitality brands to secure recurring revenue streams.

In FY 2025, revenue reached close to $168.9 million, which is an increase of approximately 99.4% over the prior year. The company reported a net loss of nearly $14 million for the fiscal period. Its net margin improved significantly year over year, reaching roughly negative 8.3%, suggesting the business is moving closer toward breaking even more than some of its peers in the emerging tech space.

IonQ faces significant risks as an early-stage company with a history of operating losses and no track record of large-scale commercial production. Key risks include intense global competition from well established technology companies and the unproven nature of its business model. The company also faces potential delays in technical roadmaps and high revenue concentration among a small number of customers.

SoundHound AI is subject to risks related to its substantial operating losses and negative cash flows as it continues to scale its operations. The company has identified material weaknesses in its internal control over financial reporting, which could impact the accuracy of its financial disclosures. It faces competition from massive technology firms and relies on a few key customers and original equipment manufacturing partners. Integration challenges following acquisitions such as LivePerson and Amelia also create operational and legal risks as the company deploys generative AI solutions.

SoundHound AI offers a lower price-to-sales (P/S) ratio, which measures market value against sales over the past twelve months, compared to the much higher premium assigned to IonQ.

This is a tough battle because you have two high-growth stocks that are part of two massive investing trends: quantum computing and AI. For quantum computing, IonQ is considered one of the few pure-play options out there, and if you're going to own any quantum stock, it's high on the list of options to consider. Ultimately, I can see both fitting into a portfolio as small, speculative positions. But if just one stock is going to be picked between the two, I would go with SoundHound.

The simple reason is that quantum computing is still just in its very early stages. According to Fortune Business Insights, the global quantum computing market was valued at $1.3 billion in 2025 and is expected to climb to $17.8 billion by 2034. There's solid growth there, but it's just not as large as the AI industry. In comparison, according to Statista, the global AI market is forecast to reach $1.9 trillion by 2032.

SoundHound also has an advantage in that it currently offers more mainstream use cases for its technology, with clients like Five Guys and Panda Express, financial services companies, automakers, and more. We'll soon see more updates on how the company has recently fared, as it is expected to report earnings on Nov. 5. Still, even if those results aren't favorable, between the two, for the foreseeable future, I would rather own SoundHound because the quantum market may take longer to develop and have less market value than AI for some time.

Jack Delaney has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Amazon, IonQ, Microsoft, and SoundHound AI. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#2996. SpaceX Just Picked Qualcomm for Its Own 5G Network. I Still See the Stock as a Hold.

Tens of thousands of cell sites start going up in 2027. How much of that belongs in the stock price now?

SpaceX (SPCX -3.81%) now has a supplier for the ground side of its plan to become a U.S. wireless carrier. Qualcomm (QCOM +5.33%) said on Wednesday that SpaceX chose its Dragonwing cellular infrastructure platforms for Starlink Mobile's nationwide ground-based 5G network. SpaceX plans to start the rollout in 2027, with tens of thousands of sites deployed over the multiyear agreement. Neither company shared financial terms.

SpaceX stock slid anyway. Shares traded near $136 as I write, off about 3% on the day and almost a third under their record close of $201.80.

Does the deal turn Starlink Mobile into a true rival to the big carriers? I think it pushes the plan forward, but I doubt it changes much for the stock yet.

Qualcomm is providing two platforms, its QRU100 for the radio units and its QDU100 for processing signals, plus the Layer 1 software that runs the network's physical radio layer. The radios will use 64 transmit and 64 receive paths, an antenna setup designed to handle more traffic on the same airwaves.

SpaceX, in other words, is still the one designing and rolling out the network.

"We're excited to collaborate with Qualcomm Technologies and leverage their industry expertise and platform to accelerate our timeline to develop our base station end-to-end architecture," said Michael Nicolls, Starlink's senior vice president, in Qualcomm's release.

SpaceX has spent over a year buying the airwaves this network needs. It agreed to pay about $19.6 billion, in shares and payments on EchoStar's debt, for EchoStar licenses covering up to 65 MHz of spectrum, a deal it expects to close near the end of November 2027. And on Oct. 8, it agreed to buy a nationwide portfolio of 800 MHz airwaves, which SpaceX expects to own by the middle of next year.

On SpaceX's Oct. 20 call about third-quarter results, President Gwynne Shotwell said S-band airwaves should handle most of the traffic, while the 800 MHz spectrum is there "to basically penetrate buildings." But the carrier service is still a way off. Shotwell said SpaceX's short-term priority is flying its Mobile V2 satellites through 2027 and integrating its spectrum purchases to start service in early 2028.

Starlink Mobile's revenue shows up in SpaceX's enterprise and government sales, according to its latest quarterly filing. That line climbed 91% year over year to $2.1 billion in the third quarter. But SpaceX doesn't break out the mobile part, and the filing credits most of the line's growth to more aircraft activations.

Of course, it's all in the segment that already makes most of SpaceX's profit. Connectivity revenue climbed 69% year over year to $5.2 billion in the third quarter, a bit faster than its 66% growth in the second quarter. The segment's operating income almost doubled, up 99% to $2.4 billion. Its operating margin jumped to around 45%, up from about 38% in both the year-ago quarter and the second quarter. And connectivity made up about 85% of SpaceX's total operating income of $2.8 billion. SpaceX's artificial intelligence (AI) business, which it calls super intelligence, earned $431 million in operating income.

CEO Elon Musk said on the call that he sees "a clear path to several hundred billion dollars a year in connectivity revenue." Connectivity is running at around $21 billion a year today, so that path would mean growing the segment many times over.

The cell sites also add to a big tab. Management expects fourth-quarter capital spending alone to land at $35 billion to $40 billion as more AI computing capacity comes online. And SpaceX hasn't said what the ground network will cost.

SpaceX's valuation is around $1.85 trillion now. Management sees $33 billion to $37 billion in revenue for the first quarter of 2027, and if the midpoint held for a full year, the stock would trade at around 13 times sales. I think that price already assumes years of fast growth -- and a phone network that might not bring in much revenue before 2028 arguably adds little to the next couple of years of it.

In the end, the Qualcomm deal makes Starlink Mobile's carrier plan more concrete. SpaceX now has deals for the airwaves, a supplier for its base stations, and a 2027 start date. It just doesn't have revenue from any of it yet.

I still see SpaceX stock as a hold at about $136, and this deal doesn't change that for me.

Daniel Sparks and his clients do not have positions in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Qualcomm. The Motley Fool recommends Space Exploration Technologies. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#2994. Hyperscalers and Apple surge after election results remove investor uncertainty

Many of the biggest technology companies are surging on Wednesday, after Democrats scored a seismic victory in the midterm elections, flipping both the House and Senate.

During trading on Wednesday, Amazon surged 8%, Google rose over 6%, Microsoft rose 4%, Meta was up 3%, and Apple — which faced Trump's tariffs and recently got refunds — climbed nearly 7%.

Tuesday's results remove uncertainty in the market, David Kelly, chief global strategist at JPMorgan Asset Management, said on "Squawk on the Street."

He said the divided government would lead to "moderation in growth, moderation in inflation, less uncertainty, and I think that's all helping the stock market move up today."

However, some stocks, particularly those tied to Trump advisor Elon Musk as well as the AI data center buildout, fell on Wednesday.

As results came in last night, some observers saw bad news for big technology companies because of their bets to build large AI-focused data centers. There were 41 local ballot measures on Tuesday to place restrictions or clear the way for AI data centers, and voters sided against data centers in 40 of them. In a CNBC Voter Poll, 56% of voters said data centers were a crucial or major factor in their voting decision.

Analysts said that Democrats who proposed data center moratoriums won gubernatorial races in Texas, Georgia, and Ohio, raising fears that projects could get snarled in regulation or blocked by politicians.

"Recall that backup power emissions, zoning, water, interconnect/grid, and tax considerations are often overseen by state regulators," Macquarie analyst Paul Golding said in a note on Wednesday.

Tesla stock fell 2%, and SpaceX was down 3%. Nvidia, which makes the chips for most of these AI data center projects, slipped just over 1%.

Many of the technology stocks rising on Wednesday are what are known as hyperscalers, or the largest cloud providers that also pair massive capex spending with robust free cash flow and have the most experience and capability to construct AI data centers.

"We believe higher regulatory barriers entrench incumbents, and increase risks (and raise required ROICs) for new entrants," wrote Needham analyst Laura Martin in a note on Wednesday. "Hyperscalers are AI incumbents."

Smaller AI cloud providers, often called neoclouds, got whacked on Wednesday. CoreWeave stock fell 10%. Baird analysts said in a note that the election results create a "sentiment headwind" for neoclouds.

Golding, the Macquarie analyst, wrote that he remains bullish on AI infrastructure, and that well-capitalized, high-quality operators stand to benefit the most if projects in some states slow down.

"Neoclouds may pay more to lease powered shells for GPUaaS commitments (and pass on costs), while hyperscaler/AI lab capacity needs could intensify competition for even more limited supply," Golding wrote.

Tuesday's results could signal restrictions on new data center developments in some states, but it doesn't necessarily signal a nationwide slowdown in construction, Chris Miglino, CEO of Axe Compute, said in an email. Companies are still raising capital, enterprises are spending, and there's still a need for more capacity.

Miglino said that increased regulation in some regions could cause investors and customers to look more closely at different cloud computing providers, and opt for those with the capability to deliver capacity, or which offer AI inference to a specific industry niche.

"If you've already secured power, have the financing in place and understand how to deploy and operate compute infrastructure efficiently, you're in a much stronger position than a company that's still trying to figure those things out," Miglino said.

#2993. McDonald's: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

The margin story rests on a 98% franchised target, and whether baseline traffic can carry that model through 2028 is still open.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 4, 2026, McDonald's (MCD +6.53%) announced global comparable sales growth of 1.5% for the quarter. For long-term investors, customer visit frequency and menu pricing power remain critical factors for sustainable profit growth. Three things management said on the call stand out.

Management is testing new everyday affordable meal options across 40% of U.S. restaurants to address entry-level pricing. Fixing baseline guest traffic remains key for franchise profitability and store volume.

Specialty beverages are expanding across global markets to capture non-peak customer traffic. Orders containing these beverages generate check totals that are larger than the full-day average check.

Refranchising efforts in international operated markets (IOM, company-operated international stores) continue as the business aims for a 98% franchised portfolio by 2028. This asset-light model protects operating margins and capital efficiency.

Baseline guest traffic is turning positive heading into the fourth quarter following targeted promotional interventions. Management is watching pricing discipline and store execution across international markets.

The company maintains a vast global store footprint, strong brand equity, and unmatched supply chain scale. Future value depends on executing its multi-year growth plan, McDonald's > NEXT, through digital engagement and modern store remodels.

The Motley Fool recommends the following options: long January 2028 $320 calls on McDonald's and short January 2028 $340 calls on McDonald's. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#2991. Apollo to buy nearly 10% stake in Abu Dhabi-backed alternative asset manager Mubadala Capital

Investing giant Apollo Global Management is finalizing a deal to buy nearly a 10% stake in Mubadala Capital, the alternative asset manager subsidiary of Abu Dhabi's sovereign wealth fund, Mubadala Investment Co., according to people familiar with the matter who spoke on condition of anonymity.

The deal is expected to be announced later Monday morning, they said. Financial terms were not immediately known.

The transaction is significant on multiple fronts. It further advances Apollo's ambitions in Abu Dhabi often called the "capital of capital." The firm has spent the past 15 years building key strategic relationships there, with both the country's investment arm and its core leadership.

Apollo already has a multibillion-dollar partnership with Mubadala Investment Co., first established in 2022 and extended two years ago. At the time of that extension, Apollo said the agreement would strengthen the firm's Capital Solutions platform by enabling the firms to jointly source and provide customized debt and equity financing to companies worldwide.

In 2023, the institutions formed a $2.5 billion joint venture designed specifically to co-invest in global private credit opportunities over a five-year horizon.

For Mubadala Capital specifically — the region's only large-scale asset manager, advising and administering more than $755 billion in assets — the deal further advances its growth strategy under the direction of CEO Hani Barhoush, and its relevance on the global investing stage. It also marks an inflection point for the company's core alternatives business, which has grown to more than $60 billion.

John Zito, co-president of Apollo's Asset Management arm, and Barhoush have worked closely together over the past several years and, according to sources, their close-knit relationship and the success of prior partnerships led to talks of deepening ties. So did the belief that scale in the asset management business matters more than ever.

"Capital as a moat is a big thing," said one person familiar with the deal.

Importantly, the deal also continues a notable shift in the relationship between American investment firms and the Middle East. While the region, and Abu Dhabi specifically, has long been a sought-after source of capital, this deal further reverses that dynamic, with Apollo making a significant direct investment in the country and its investment vision.

Mubadala Capital's international offices include London, New York, Rio de Janeiro and San Francisco.

Apollo's investment in Mubadala Capital is similar to deals the firm has done with other asset management platforms, including Diameter Capital, Thoreau, Motive Partners and others.

Spokespersons for both firms declined to comment.

#2989. History Says This Is the Best Time of the Presidential Cycle To Invest. Could Wednesday's Rally Spark an Epic Bull Market?

Historically, stocks have soared in the third year of a presidential term.

Stocks soared on Wednesday as investors reacted to Democrats' sweeping win in the midterm elections.

The Dow Jones Industrial Average finished up 3.2%, while the S&P 500 (^GSPC +1.58%) gained 1.8%, and the Nasdaq Composite closed 1.4% higher.

The surge seemed to take Wall Street by surprise as stocks had initially fallen when results started to come in on fears of a data center backlash. However, by the time markets had opened on Wednesday, those fears seemed fully absorbed. While stocks with exposure to the AI infrastructure market fell on Wednesday, the rest of the market soared as investors cheered decisive results, President Trump's acceptance of his party's defeat, and a renewed potential for interest rates and inflation to come down, and for the war in Iran to end, bringing down oil prices.

Stocks tend to go up after an election as investors appreciate the degree of certainty that's been revealed, but Wednesday's gain was especially strong. Does it signal more to come? Historically, stocks perform particularly well during the third year of a presidential term.

Let's take a look at what history says about the current market cycle, and why it could be a great time to invest.

Historically, the third year of the four-year term is the strongest and most reliable period for the stock market. On average, the S&P 500 has gained 13.7% in the third year of the presidential cycle since 1980, and positive returns happen 78%-91% of the time, depending on the period that's cited.

According to the Presidential Election Cycle Theory, the midterm election often leads to economic stimulus as incumbent administrations typically look to improve the economy ahead of the next presidential election. Historically, the Federal Reserve has also been more accommodative during the third year, though that may happen in this cycle. Finally, Wall Street also prefers the sense of political certainty coming out of the midterm election.

From the week before the midterm to the end of the following year, a period of about 14 months, the S&P 500 has gained every time in the last 19 cycles, a remarkable streak, with an average gain of 20.3%.

In addition to the tailwind from this stage of the presidential cycle, stocks also look poised for further gains because investors, in particular, like a divided government, and with Democrats taking solid control of both houses of Congress and President Trump still in the White House, the government is clearly divided between both parties.

Wall Street prefers a divided government because it creates gridlock and makes it less likely that any extreme legislation passes. Investors also typically move back into stocks after the election as the risk of unsatisfying results has passed.

Finally, stocks tend to perform better from November through April than from May through October. There's no perfect explanation for that trend, but institutional investors tend to rebalance in the fourth quarter, corporate and holiday bonuses get put into the market, and tax-loss harvesting at the end of the year can also help. Alternatively, trading volumes tend to be lower in the summer when people go on vacations and aren't as plugged into the market.

Investors should keep in mind that these are just historical averages or trends, and that this historical pattern won't be able to overcome a black swan event or deeper, structural challenges, should they arrive.

However, the market's reaction on Wednesday bodes well for more gains. Though AI infrastructure stocks fell on worries about a data center slowdown, hyperscalers popped as investors bet that they would benefit from a slower AI expansion plan.

Nine out of 11 stock market sectors moved higher, with only energy and industrials falling. It's just one session, but stocks are off to a good start post-midterms, and the pieces are there for more gains.

Jeremy Bowman has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

#2988. Snap shares fall over 10% on third-quarter revenue miss

Snap shares sank over 10% when the Snapchat parent missed on revenue and daily active users in its third-quarter earnings report Wednesday.

Here's how the company did compared with analysts' expectations:

Sales in Snap's third quarter jumped 14% year-over-year while net income came in at $56 million, compared to the $104 million net loss it logged the prior year.

Snap's third-quarter adjusted earnings came in at $383 million, topping the $337 million that analysts were projecting, according to StreetAccount.

The Snapchat parent said fourth-quarter revenue should come in between $1.95 billion and $1.99 billion. The midpoint of that figure is higher than Wall Street's estimates of $1.95 billion.

Fourth-quarter adjusted earnings are expected to come in between $490 million and $525 million. Analysts were expecting that figure to be $498 million.

Snap executives said in an investor letter that the company plans for globally monthly active users to become its "primary audience metric" starting in its first quarter for 2027. The company also said it has 977 MAU for the quarter.

"Daily engagement has historically been stable as a percentage of monthly engagement, but we do not intend to optimize Snapchat around daily use at the expense of long-term value for our community," the investor letter said. "A longer-term horizon better aligns our reported metrics with the product and safety decisions that strengthen relationships, create lasting utility, and support broader, more durable audience growth."

Snap chief commercial officer Ronan Harris told analysts during an earnings call that lower spending by unspecified consumer packaged goods, or CPG, companies impacted third-quarter sales. Harris attributed the curbed CPG spending to "macro pressures in the U.S. and also the Middle East.""As we look ahead, we're seeing very positive signs of growth from diversified verticals like financial services in the U.S.," Harris said. "So part of our strategy here is to look outside of the pressures in CPG and find traction in these other verticals."

#2985. Figma sinks after earnings beat as company prioritizes long-term growth with AI changes

Figma shares tumbled 7% in extended trading after the company reported third-quarter results and guidance that surpassed Wall Street's predictions.

Here's how the company performed relative to LSEG consensus:

Figma's revenue grew about 43% year over year in the quarter, according to a statement. Net loss narrowed to $81.3 million, or 15 cents per share, compared with a loss of $1.10 billion, or $2.72 per share, a year earlier.

"If there's certain actions that we can power for our customers at lower latency, lower cost, or similar quality to third party, we're open to that as well," Melwani said.

Figma is increasing its focus on cybersecurity, said Dylan Field, Figma's co-founder and CEO. In recent months, AI agents have infiltrated systems at Hugging Face and other companies.

Clients aren't asking Figma to become a security vendor, but the company must scrutinize "the security of all of our vendors right now," Field said.

#2983. Top Wall Street analysts prefer these 3 dividend stocks for stable income

Global stock markets remain volatile amid persistent geopolitical tensions and concerns about the sustainability of AI trade. Given this backdrop, investors seeking steady income can add dividend-paying stocks to their portfolios.

Tracking recommendations from top Wall Street analysts can help investors pick attractive stocks, as these experts assess a company's ability to pay dividends consistently.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Integrated oil and gas giant Chevron (CVX) is a dividend king. The company has increased its dividend for 39 consecutive years. At a quarterly dividend of $1.78 per share (annualized dividend of $7.12 per share), CVX offers a yield of 3.14%. Chevron will announce its third-quarter results on October 30.

In an earnings preview note on U.S. oil majors, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on Chevron with a price target of $241. The analyst is focused on the company's international growth prospects across Latin America, the Middle East, West Africa, and the Eastern Mediterranean.

Specifically, Mehta highlighted management's commentary on solid execution in Venezuela, with gross production expected to more than double to nearly 600 kbd (kilo barrels per day) by 2031 via three joint ventures. He also noted a recent oil and gas condensate discovery in offshore Angola and is awaiting commercial updates on the West Qurna 2 and Nasiriyah projects in Iraq.

Furthermore, Mehta expects CVX to report improved productivity and capital efficiency across domestic shale assets. He expects the improvement mainly in the Permian Basin, where Chevron projects advanced chemical treatments to drive about a 10% recovery uplift in new wells and bring down capital spend per barrel by about 25% year-over-year. Overall, Mehta expects Chevron to report Q3 2026 EPS of $4.95, above the consensus of $4.89.

"While unfavorable timing effects and volatile commodity prices may present quarterly earnings headwinds, we highlight healthy U.S. refining margins and strong asset utilization as partial offsets," said Mehta.

Mehta ranks No. 381 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 12%. See Chevron KPIs on TipRanks.

Kinetik Holdings (KNTK) is a fully integrated, pure-play, midstream company operating in the Delaware Basin. Earlier this month, the company declared a dividend of $0.81 per share, payable on October 30, 2026. With an annualized dividend of $3.24 per share, KNTK offers a yield of 5.82%. Kinetik will announce its Q3 earnings on November 4, with a conference call scheduled the next day.

Ahead of Q3 2026 earnings, Goldman Sachs analyst John Mackay reiterated a buy rating on Kinetik stock and slightly raised his price target to $58 from $57. He expects the company to report Q3 2026 EBITDA of $279 million, higher than the Street's expectation of $270 million but lower than the second quarter's EBITDA of $281 million.

The 5-star analyst expects Kinetik's Q3 performance to gain from higher gas volumes after notable curtailments in the second quarter. Mackay also expects the quarter to benefit from higher production and steady commodity margins, even though some lower-margin contracts and weaker Waha spread marketing gains could weigh on results.

Overall, Mackay increased his Q3 2026 estimates to reflect higher volumes, driven by rapid Permian egress pipeline commissioning, stronger throughput at Kings Landing I, and higher M2M commodity prices, offset by increased costs.

"As natural gas takeaway constraints ease across the Permian, investor focus centers on KNTK's YE26 volume trajectory and durability of its multi-year growth," said Mackay.

Mackay ranks No. 569 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.9%. See Kinetik's Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Moving to Crescent Energy (CRGY), an exploration and production company with activities focused in the Eagle Ford, Permian, and Uinta Basins. With a quarterly dividend of $0.12 per share (annualized dividend of $0.48 per share), CRGY's dividend yield stands at 3.56%. Earlier this month, Crescent announced an agreement to acquire Eagle Ford assets from Devon Energy (DVN).

Heading into Q3 earnings on Nov. 2, Evercore analyst Stephen Richardson reiterated a buy rating on Crescent stock and slightly lowered the price target to $17 from $18, citing dilution from new shares.

"CRGY's recent acquisition of DVN's Eagle Ford assets marks a strategic, growth-oriented move, potentially enhancing cash flow performance," said Richardson.

The 5-star analyst noted that the acquisition of the Eagle Ford assets at a net purchase price of $3.85 billion will add about 68 MBoe/d of net production and over 600 net Tier 1 locations. Richardson added that a solid track record of synergies from past deals in the Eagle Ford region increases confidence about Crescent achieving efficiencies with this deal.

Additionally, Richardson noted that the financial implications of the acquisition seem impressive, with a projected increase in cash flow per share of about 9% for 2027 to 2029.

The analyst believes that Crescent is not just expanding its business through acquisitions but positioning itself as a more durable company, regardless of market conditions.

Richardson ranks No. 478 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.2%. See Crescent Energy Statistics on TipRanks.

#2981. Qualcomm stock drops 7% on weak earnings forecast as memory shortage weighs on smartphone sales

Qualcomm reported quarterly results on Wednesday that topped Wall Street estimates, but the chipmaker's earnings forecast came in lighter than expected. The stock slid 7% in extended trading.

Here's how the chipmaker did versus LSEG consensus estimates:

For the current quarter, Qualcomm said it sees earnings per share of between $2.15 and $2.35 on revenue of $10.4 billion to $11.2 billion. Analysts expected earnings per share of $2.27 on revenue of $10.48 billion.

Most of Qualcomm's chip sales come from system-on-chips and modems for smartphones. It's a market that's currently in turmoil as rising memory costs force handset makers to raise prices, hurting demand. Adjusted company revenue fell 7% during from a year earlier.

Qualcomm raised prices in September. Still, handset chip sales during the quarter fell 26% to $5.14 billion, trailing a StreetAccount estimate of $5.21 billion. Qualcomm said it's starting to see sequential growth in the category, and that Android shipments in China have bottomed, even though the company predicts the handset market will be flat in 2027.

Under CEO Cristiano Amon, Qualcomm has tried to turn investor attention to other kinds of chips. The company is looking to enter the fast-growing market for AI data center infrastructure. It had $650 million in data center revenue during the quarter, mostly from customizing and connectivity work, Amon said.

Amon said Qualcomm expects $5 billion in data center revenue in fiscal 2027. He said the company recently signed a customer for optical connectivity and had engaged a global hyperscaler.

"We don't have a demand issue; it's a supply issue," Amon said. "If I had more supply, I would probably be doing better."

Qualcomm also sells chips for cars and other automotive uses. Revenue in that division soared 89% on an annual basis to $1.99 billion, beating StreetAccount expectations of $1.66 billion.

Internet of things revenue includes low-power chips, smart glasses SoCs, laptop chips, as well as the company's bid to provide cellular infrastructure. Earlier on Wednesday, Qualcomm announced a deal to provide technology to SpaceX's cellular buidlout. IoT revenue increased 4% from a year earlier to $1.88 billion, slightly higher than estimates.

Qualcomm's QTL division includes revenue from licensing its intellectual property for 5G and other technologies to other companies. Qualcomm said during the quarter that it had renewed licensing agreements with Apple, Huawei, and Transsion.

"QTL will maintain its scale going forward," Amon said.

QTL revenue sank 7% to $1.3 billion, in line with a StreetAccount estimate.

#2976. Prediction markets cement their status after forecasting Democratic sweep of Congress

Prediction market platforms cemented their role in election forecasting on Tuesday, when markets accurately predicted a host of races before the polls closed, and projected outcomes before news outlets.

Democrats were seen as favored to flip both the U.S. House of Representatives and Senate on prediction markets before the first polls closed at 6:00 p.m. ET Tuesday, and were seen as narrow favorites to take at least 53 Senate seats, while a 54th was forecast as a toss-up.

So far, Democrats have won 54 Senate seats — including wins in Iowa, which prediction markets placed at 50-50 odds, and South Carolina, where Republicans narrowly favored. Outstanding results remain in Mississippi and Alaska, where prediction markets give Democrat Scott Colom and Mary Peltola 90% odds of winning.

Leading prediction market platforms were actively projecting races on X Tuesday night. Those projections, at times, came before many media outlets made their own projections, or before even a substantial number of votes had been counted.

Kalshi was confident making those calls, as long as odds for a candidate were above 99% and trading activity for the opposite candidate "dried out,"  said Jack Such, a spokesperson for the platform.

Polymarket celebrated on X how it projected races, saying that in 10 key races, the platform called races ahead of outlets including CNN, Fox News and the Associated Press by a median 31 minutes.

"It's now routinely hours between when prediction markets call it versus the TV networks," said Polymarket CEO Shayne Coplan in an X post. "No knock to them, but now there's Polymarket, and many people are realizing they want to know what's happening in real time."

Even in races where markets initially favored the candidate who eventually lost, they quickly adjusted once initial vote counts came in. In South Carolina, Democratic Senate candidate Annie Andrews was seen as the favorite on Kalshi to win shortly after 7:30 p.m., only 30 minutes after polls closed. In Texas, where Democratic state Rep. Gina Hinojosa ousted Republican Gov. Greg Abbott, Hinojosa's odds on Polymarket jumped from 39% to 73% within five minutes of initial poll closings.

"You now have financially motivated number crunchers doing this, around the world, that give you that signal just that much faster," said Flip Pidot, a member of the board of directors at the Coalition for Political Forecasting.

Still, a successful night for prediction markets shouldn't stop efforts aimed at stronger regulation of the platforms, said Michelle Kendler-Kretsch, a researcher at the Anti-Corruption Data Collective, a nonprofit. She cited a recent Reuters/Ipsos survey that found most Americans are against using prediction markets to trade on elections.

"Risks and vulnerabilities didn't change overnight just because the markets were accurate this time around," she said.

Right before the midterms, prediction market forecasts had missed major global elections, including Brazil's presidential race, where Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva only had about 20% odds to win on Kalshi before polls closed.

Prediction markets also whiffed in some high-profile Democratic primaries over the summer, forecasting extremely high odds for progressives in Wisconsin and Michigan, for example. The Michigan Democrat, Abdul El-Sayed, narrowly won his primary before going on to win Thursday's general election.

Academics who've researched prediction markets for decades say the platforms aren't wrong when candidates with low odds on markets end up winning races.

"If you toss two coins, there's a 25% chance that you'll throw two heads in a row, and nobody would be terribly surprised if you did," said Rajiv Sethi, an economics professor at Barnard College.

Despite their early success on Tuesday, Dartmouth College economic professor Eric Zitzewitz said it's still not clear if casual observers understand how market probabilities work.

"Some people who are coming to [prediction markets] for the first time will see 55 by 45 odds and think that, 'Oh the candidate with 55 is way ahead,'" he said.

As prediction markets gain traction, however, the public will improve at understanding how to interpret their odds, Zitzewitz said.

With another election under their belts, prediction markets will remain a force through the next presidential election, experts said.

"Last time around [in 2024], prediction markets were in the discussion, but they were in the discussion in a way that was a little bit unsure," said Harry Crane, a professor of statistics at Rutgers University. "This [was] the first true prediction markets election."

Disclosure: CNBC and Kalshi have a commercial relationship that includes customer acquisition and a minority investment.

#2965. Investors are doing just as well this year by shunning AI. How to invest if the tech trade keeps fizzling

A version of the S&P 500 without AI briefly pulled ahead of the traditional index this year and is now neck-and-neck as investors rotate into other areas of the market.

A proprietary Goldman Sachs index tracking the S&P 500 without stocks classified as "AI enablers" was up 12.4% through Oct. 22, edging ahead of the standard S&P 500's 12% gain. The Goldman index is now neck-and-neck with the S&P 500 as tech rallied in Monday trading.

Tech and AI-related companies make up about 45% of the S&P 500. That heavy weighting has helped power the index for years, but it's turned into a liability lately; the S&P 500 tech sector is down about 7% this month while other sectors have held up.

Investors have soured on debt and equity issued by companies like CoreWeave and Oracle. Shares of Meta Platforms sank almost 7% Thursday after it outlined more plans to spend heavily on capex and issue additional shares.

Companies supplying power and equipment for the buildout have been hit hard — names like GE Vernova, Bloom Energy, Vistra and Quanta Services all suffered steep declines during the recent selloff.

Goldman, which launched the measure earlier this year with S&P, uses the S&P 500 as its starting universe and excludes companies perceived to be enablers of AI before applying S&P 500 Index weighting methodology to the remaining universe of companies.

The ex-AI index lagged the S&P 500 for most of the year as investors continued to favor chipmakers, cloud-computing companies and other firms benefiting from heavy AI spending. However, that advantage has been narrowing and it disappeared last week.

Meanwhile, investors are rotating into defensive areas. ETFs like State Street Consumer Staples Select Sector SPDR ETF, State Street Health Select SPDR ETF and State Street Utilities Select Sector SPDR ETF are up 7% to 10% this month. Energy is ahead 10% too, though that's more related to the war in Iran.

Some analysts recommend sticking with non-AI companies for diversification and protection against more pain in tech. The "AI pendulum has swung back in favor of non-AI exposure over the last two weeks," Oppenheimer technical analyst Ari Wald said in a note on Oct. 24.

Two industries that Oppenheimer favors now are commercial services and insurance. The firm upgraded those industries to overweight last week, recommending Republic Services, Cintas, Verisk Analytics, Broadridge Financial Solutions, MetLife and Arthur J. Gallagher.

Berkshire Hathaway is also capitalizing on the rotation out of tech. Its Class B shares are up 8% this month, though it's still lagging behind the S&P 500 this year.

Berkshire owns a wide swath of operating companies, including insurance giant Geico and Burlington Northern Santa Fe railroad. It also has a $364 billion stock portfolio including companies like Apple, Coca-Cola, Alphabet and Bank of America. That diversification may help Berkshire continue to outperform if the tech sector drags down the S&P 500.

Trivector Research sees potential in "non-tech compounders." Healthcare giant Eli Lilly is one idea. The stock is up 22% on the strength of Lilly's weight-loss drugs. The company has more in the pipeline, including a next-generation obesity drug that Lilly plans to file for FDA approval early in 2027.

Viking Holdings, a cruise operator, is also proving durable. The company said on its August earnings call that 40% of its river capacity and 60% of its ocean capacity was already booked for 2027. "Based on recent events, we've seen our guests have proved to be resilient and are continuing to book 2027 and future seasons," the company told investors.

Viking stock is up 15% this year but it may still have upside. The average Wall Street target is $109, above recent prices around $82. Barclays recently upgraded the cruise operator to overweight with a $102 target, citing the company's "compelling risk/reward."
