#2963. Every stock that moved because of the election

The blue wave across the country has rippled through the stock market too, driving healthcare and clean energy names higher and data center plays and defense companies tumbling.

The broader market soared after Democrats won control of both the House of Representatives and the Senate, and key gubernatorial races in Texas and Georgia. In Washington, the Democratic Congress will now serve as a counterweight to President Donald Trump's agenda, which has included tax cuts and policies that sent inflation, like tariffs and the Iran war.

Local and state elections will also have an impact on data center stocks, with construction limits passed in some states and a Democratic governor-elect in Texas who promised a moratorium.

"Democratic control could slow the push for deregulation in financials, energy and digital assets," Wells Fargo Investment Institute said in a note Wednesday. "We still believe both parties have an incentive to compromise on data center location and AI generally."

Here are some of the biggest movers as a result of the election outcome.

Nextpower jumped more than 11.2 % and Canadian Solar rallied 6.7%, while Plug Power gained 3.6%, First Solar climbed 4.6% and Sunrun ticked 1.3% higher. Nextera Energy added 4.9%. Investors are hoping that Democrats will push back against the Trump administration's rejection of clean energy policies.

Healthcare companies rallied on investor optimism over a slowdown or reversal in healthcare spending cuts, and the potential that enhanced subsidies will extended in Affordable Care Act plans.

Molina Healthcare and Centene gained 8.9% and 12.6%, respectively. HCA Healthcare soared 10.3% and Universal Health Services jumped 10.9%, while Tenet Healthcare rallied 9.6% and Elevance Health added 4.9%.

Elon Musk's SpaceX and Tesla shed 3.8% and 3.3%, respectively, after Democrats won the gubernatorial, Senate, and attorney general races in Texas, as well as wresting control of the Texas House of Representatives . Musk, CEO of both companies, spent at least $474 million backing Republican candidates in the midterms, including $224 million in the Lone Star State, where both Tesla and SpaceX are headquartered.

Defense companies could face headwinds if Democrats challenge military spending. Shares of RTX slipped 2.4%, Lockheed Martin tumbled 6.9%, Northrop Grumman sank 4.5% and Howmet Aerospace fell 4.3%. Palantir Technologies shed 2.1%.

The Trump administration has requested $1.5 trillion for national defense in fiscal 2027. It is also separately asking for an $87.6 billion supplemental appropriation from Congress for fiscal 2026, largely due to the Iran conflict.

Drone stocks also fell. Kratos Defense & Security Solutions dropped 12.3%, while AeroVironment lost 8.3% and Red Cat fell 10.4%. Ondas shed 7% and Unusual Machines tumbled 9%.

Names connected with data centers moved lower amid concern about the potential for restrictions or moratoriums being placed on AI infrastructure buildouts.

Neocloud provider CoreWeave, the poster child for fears over massive AI-related debt, slumped 9.9%. Peers Nebius and Iren dropped 6.7% and 11.2%, respectively.

AI infrastructure stocks also tumbled, with Comfort Systems losing 15.1% and Vistra sliding 13.8%. Micron Technology fell 7%.

That said, five of the Magnificent Seven rallied. Apple rose 5.3% and Microsoft jumped 6.7%, with Apple touching an all-time high. Alphabet gained 3.2% and Meta added 1.4%.

A consumer relief trade also took hold, sending housing, travel and consumer staples stocks higher. Affordability was a big theme in the election, with Americans feeling the pressure of high prices.

Housing stocks like Pulte, D.R. Horton and Lennar gained roughly 7.2%, 8.1% and 7.1%, respectively. Airlines also moved higher, with United Airlines rising 8% and American Airlines climbing 6.3%. Southwest Airlines jumped 6.8% and Delta Air Lines climbed 7.2%. Cruise operator Carnival increased 7.3%, while Royal Caribbean and Norwegian Cruise Line added 6.4% and 6.8%, respectively. Booking Holdings rallied 9.8%.

Consumer staple names also caught a bid. Dollar Tree soared 9.6%, Walmart gained 3.5% and Constellation Brands climbed 5.3%. Dollar General advanced 8.5%, while Conagra Brands increased 7%.

The GOP's wide losses also extended dragged down President Donald Trump's social media and tech company, which plunged 24.5% and hit a 52-week low.

— CNBC's Luke Fountain, Dominic Chu and Nicholas Wells contributed reporting.

#2959. Federal Reserve Board requests public comment on two proposals related to establishing a regulatory framework for Board-supervised payment stablecoin issuers under the GENIUS Act

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 2:30 p.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Thursday requested public comment on two proposals related to establishing a regulatory framework for Board-supervised payment stablecoin issuers under the GENIUS Act.

The first proposal would require that Board-supervised payment stablecoin issuers fully back their stablecoins with certain permissible reserve assets, such as short-term Treasury bills and certain other high-quality, liquid assets. The proposal would also establish standardized capital requirements to address certain credit and operational risks of payment stablecoin activities, as well as risk management standards, in accordance with the law. Separately, the proposal would introduce rules for Board-supervised firms that safekeep the assets backing payment stablecoins. It would also clarify the permissibility of stablecoin and related activities for Board-supervised banks.

The second proposal would establish a tailored application process for Board-supervised banks applying to issue payment stablecoins. These applicants would be required to submit a business plan and financial information, among other documents. The proposal would also create a process governing appeals, hearings, and final determinations for applications.

The comment period will close 60 days after publication in the Federal Register.

For media inquiries, please email [email protected] or call (202) 452-2955.

Federal Register notice: Application Procedures for Board-Supervised Insured Depository Institutions Seeking Approval for a Subsidiary to Issue Payment Stablecoins

Federal Register notice: Implementing the Federal Reserve Boardâs Responsibilities under the GENIUS Act

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 4:00 p.m. EDT Share

The Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation and the Federal Reserve Board today published feedback letters for resolution plans submitted in October 2025. The agencies did not identify any shortcomings or deficiencies in these resolution plan submissions.

Resolution plans, also known as living wills, describe a banking organization's strategy for orderly resolution in the event of material financial distress or failure. The agencies conducted a joint review of the 2025 resolution plan submissions from 15 banking organizations with over $250 billion in assets.

In addition, the agencies determined that the shortcoming previously identified in the 2021 BNP Paribas resolution plan has been satisfactorily addressed.

#2954. Crude oil prices fall below $90 a barrel as Vance blames GOP election defeat on Iran war

Crude oil prices tumbled Wednesday after Vice President JD Vance blamed the war against Iran for Republican midterm election reversals.

U.S. West Texas Intermediate lost 8.3% to close at $88.12 a barrel. Brent crude futures, the international benchmark, shed 8.7% to settle at $94.62.

"The reason we lost is the war," Vance said in a social media post Wednesday. "The president himself said so last night."

Democrats swept the House and Senate in Tuesday's elections, giving the party leverage to pressure President Donald Trump to end the unpopular war that has spiked gasoline and diesel prices.

"The elections are leading the market to conclude that Trump might now be inclined to make a deal," said Andy Lipow, president of Lipow Oil Associates.

The Democrats may try to restrict funding for the war, Lipow said. Trump's announcement earlier this week that the U.S. will cut some funding to Israel could force Jerusalem to accept Washington's decisions, he said.

Trump told the Financial Times on Tuesday that he has no immediate plans to strike Iran. The oil market had priced in the risk of a major escalation after the elections, gaining more than 10% in October.

Oil futures have fallen more than 14% this week as senior U.S. and Iranian officials indicated they're open to a diplomatic settlement of the conflict, which has disrupted global energy supplies for eight months.

The sell-off "could be getting a bit overdone," said Rebecca Babin, senior energy trader at CIBC. The market is in a "shoot first, ask questions" later mode as emotions run high after the unexpected election results, Babin said.

Many observers expected a Democrat tsunami could push Trump to escalate with Iran, Babin said. "Vance's commentary and the public lack of backing from key allies have challenged that assumption," she said.

But the odds still favor military escalation in the region, warned Bob McNally, president of Rapidan Energy and a former advisor to President George W. Bush.

"Iran is hardened in its uncompromising position," McNally said. "Trump, having now paid a huge price for his determination to prevent Iran from obtaining a nuclear weapon, is unlikely to let go of this legacy goal and walk away."

#2952. Federal Reserve Board finalizes changes to enhance the transparency and public accountability of its stress test and reduce volatility in its stress test-related capital requirements

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 9:00 a.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Wednesday finalized changes to enhance the transparency and public accountability of its stress test and reduce volatility in its stress test-related capital requirements. The two final rules are largely similar to the proposed rules from 2025. The Board also requested comment on a proposal to better capture differences in banks' business models to generate fee income.

The Board conducts stress tests to ensure that large banks are sufficiently capitalized and able to lend to households and businesses even in a severe recession. In December 2024, the Board announced it would modify the test to improve its resilience.

"The stress test is an essential component of our regulatory capital framework," said Vice Chair for Supervision Michelle W. Bowman. "Today's changes preserve its resilience by ensuring that it is transparent, granular, and risk-sensitive. The public will now have greater assurance that the risks banks take will be reflected appropriately in their stress test losses and their capital requirements."

The first final rule requires the Board to invite public input annually on the stress test scenarios and any material model changes. It also updates the framework that guides the design of the hypothetical scenarios and adopts the models that will be used for the 2027 stress test. In addition, the final rule makes several adjustments to the stress test calendar. The final rule also updates the global market shock component, which is a set of shocks to specific market components applied to banks with large trading books. These banks will now be tested against two global market shock components each year, and the Board will use the shock that produces the largest losses for each firm to calculate the firm's stress test results.

The second final rule requires the Board, when calculating stress capital buffer requirements, to average the results from the two most recent annual supervisory stress tests for firms subject to the stress test in both years. The Board will begin averaging stress capital buffer requirements in 2028 to ensure that only models incorporating public input are used in the calculation.

The proposal would request public comment on a revision to the Board's noninterest income model that would improve the stress test's ability to capture business model diversity across firms. If adopted, these adjustments would replace the current noninterest income model that projects each bank's fee income under stress. Comments are due 60 days after publication in the Federal Register.

Together, the changes are likely to reduce year-over-year volatility in capital requirements by approximately 50 percent and are not expected to materially affect aggregate capital requirements.

For media inquiries, please email [email protected] or call 202-452-2955.

Federal Register notice: Modifications to the Capital Plan Rule and Stress Capital Buffer Requirement

Federal Register notice: Enhanced Transparency and Public Accountability of the Supervisory Stress Test Models and Scenarios; Modifications to the Capital Planning and Stress Capital Buffer Requirement Rule, Enhanced Prudential Standards Rule, and Regulation LL

Federal Register notice: Request for Comment on Model Changes for the Boardâs 2027 Supervisory Stress Test

#2951. Prediction market traders say an Anthropic IPO is near-certain this year after company filed with SEC

Anthropic on Monday unveiled its Form S-1 filing, taking the company one step closer to completing its long-awaited move to the public markets, which is now seen as almost guaranteed to come this year.

Speculators on prediction market platform Kalshi place 95% odds that the artificial intelligence giant officially announces an initial public offering by the end of 2026.

Kalshi defines officially announcing an IPO as either the Securities and Exchange Commission declaring an S-1 effective, a priced IPO or an exchange assigning a ticker to a stock.

The Anthropic S-1 envisions a Nasdaq Stock Market ticker symbol of "ANT."

Anthropic is aiming to start trading the week of Nov. 16, according to a report in the Wall Street Journal. Traders on Kalshi place just a 14% chance that the company begins trading before then.

On the Polymarket platform, traders give a 69% chance that Anthropic will list on a public exchange and open for trading by Nov. 20.

Disclosure: CNBC and Kalshi have a commercial relationship that includes customer acquisition and a minority investment.

#2949. Democrats map out an agenda to address affordability and corruption after midterm sweep

The newly elected Democratic majorities in the Senate and the House are planning to pass a flurry of bills as soon as they take the reins next January, CNBC has learned, and are preparing to tussle with an antagonistic President Donald Trump.

Follow CNBC's live coverage of the 2026 midterm election results

The legislative push aims to show a united Democratic caucus taking quick action on the issues they were elected to solve, especially oppressively high costs on everything from gas to groceries.

In their first 100 hours after being sworn in, House Democrats plan to bring to the floor bills reinstating the enhanced health insurance subsidies that expired under the GOP-held Congress, and reversing the Medicaid cuts Trump signed into law last year.

Democrats — likely led by Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries (D-N.Y.), who is poised to become the first Black Speaker of the House in U.S. history — also plan to push forward a bill that would undo Trump's sweeping tariffs and reassert Congress' role in setting import taxes. And they will again vote on legislation to end the Iran war or force Trump to seek congressional authorization to keep it going. Some details of the Democrats' plans had been previously reported by the New York Times.

But Democrats will need to strike a balance between these legislative priorities and a robust oversight and investigatory agenda that the party is mapping out to scrutinize Trump, his family, cabinet and supporters in the business world.

"We haven't evaluated who are going to be the targets of the investigation right out of the gate. We'll have those conversations over the next couple of weeks," Jeffries told CNBC on Wednesday.

"But clearly, things aren't going right for the American people. Capitalism is broken in the way in which it is being experienced by so many working-class Americans and middle-class Americans. We've got to make sure we build an economy, a free market economy, that works for everyday Americans," Jeffries said.

Democrats on the campaign trail for months hammered home a message of affordability. And as corruption emerged as a top issue for voters in the waning months of the election, they tried to connect the Trump administration's perceived bad behavior to Americans' pocketbooks.

Already, prominent Democrats are taking a similar tack as they prepare to assume majorities in both chambers.

Sen. Ron Wyden of Oregon, the top Democrat on the Senate Finance Committee, said in a statement on Wednesday that the panel's top priorities "will be affordability for us, accountability for them."

Wyden said the committee would double down on its efforts to make sure billionaires pay their fair share in taxes, address the "corporate chokehold on our nation's health care system" and reassert congressional authority over trade. He also vowed to target the "special interests" that had cozied up to Trump in the past two years.

"From the banks that helped cover up Epstein's crimes, to insurance companies that are delaying and denying health care to churn a profit, to the pharmaceutical company CEOs that price gouge while enjoying Trump's hand-delivered windfalls, when I am chair, they will be forced to answer to the Senate Finance Committee for their role in hurting the American people and enabling Trump's corruption," Wyden said.

Rep. Robert Garcia of California, the top-ranking Democrat on the House Oversight Committee, has vowed to take on Trump's family, corporate corruption, Department of Homeland Security and Immigration and Customs Enforcement overreach and the handling of files related to disgraced sex offender Jeffrey Epstein.

Garcia issued a pointed, two-word statement as results tipped toward Democrats on Tuesday night: "Lawyer up."

And Rep. Pramila Jayapal, D-Wash., a former chair of the Congressional Progressive Caucus and member of the House Judiciary and Foreign Affairs committees, said in a recent interview with CNBC that she believed Democrats should target figures like Elon Musk, as well as cabinet secretaries like Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth, Secretary of Health and Human Services Robert F. Kennedy Jr., and Attorney General Todd Blanche.

But she acknowledged the challenges that could come with pursuing a large number of potential targets all at once, especially with a potentially unwieldy and ideologically diverse Democratic caucus.

"I think what we should do is pick one or two cases that really exemplify the worst of the corruption, and that we feel we have the most evidence of wrongdoing," Jayapal said. "There are so many things — I think it's important to focus on a couple."

Meanwhile, other Democrats are angling for some kind of ethics reform package that could include prohibitions on stock trading and use of prediction markets for government officials, a federal ban on gerrymandering and limiting super PAC spending.

"My expectation is that when Democrats are the majority, we will pass a strong bill to ban stock trading in Congress, in the executive branch, and beyond, and that this will be part of a larger package of ethics reforms that we pursue," said Rep. Seth Magaziner, D-R.I., a longtime advocate for such legislation.

#2945. Democrats take Congress in a blue wave. Here's what it means for your money

With Democrats flipping control of Congress in the midterm election, the shift in power on Capitol Hill could reshape household budgets — from student loan bills and health insurance premiums to housing costs and taxes.

Experts say results show that rising costs were top of mind for voters.

"I see this election as a repudiation of the anti-affordability agenda from the Trump administration," said Daniel Bunn, president and CEO of the Tax Foundation, a nonpartisan, nonprofit research organization.

"Whether that's the conflict in the Middle East or the tariffs, just top to bottom, it's clear to me that was a real concern of the electorate," he said.

White House spokesman Matthew Middleton said in an emailed statement that the Trump administration has delivered "tangible wins to make life more affordable for American families." Officials "will continue to put hardworking American's first with or without support from Democrats," he wrote.

Democrats won control of both the House of Representatives and Senate, which could make it easier for the party to advance legislation. They can also use hearings and investigations to press federal agencies and companies to answer for policies and practices that impact consumers, and exert leverage over confirmation of President Donald Trump's nominees.

But turning campaign promises into changes people feel in their finances won't be easy. Trump can veto bills a Democrat-controlled Congress passes, limiting how much of their agenda could become law. Based on projections, Democrats will lack the numbers needed to override a presidential veto.

Any changes will also take time: Newly elected members of Congress won't take office until January.

Here are eight ways Democrats’ new congressional majority could affect your money.

"Not surprisingly, the Dems are going to be keyed in on affordability with respect to healthcare," said Zach Gaumer, vice president of health policy at the Bipartisan Policy Center, a nonpartisan think tank.

OBBBA made Trump's 2017 tax cuts permanent and added temporary tax breaks. These included new deductions for tip income, overtime earnings, seniors and auto loan interest, which will expire after 2028 without an extension.

Democrats may support extending these provisions. But as the Democrat-controlled Congress maps out its agenda, lawmakers will have to address the federal budget deficit. For fiscal year 2026, the budget deficit ballooned to nearly $2 trillion, the Treasury announced in October.

Newly elected Democratic senators James Talarico of Texas, Troy Jackson of Maine and Annie Andrews of South Carolina all campaigned on expanding the child tax credit. Earlier this year, Senate Democrats also floated ideas to expand the standard deduction and create income tax exemptions for lower-earning Americans — which are costly proposals, according to Bunn from the Tax Foundation.

"I've called these termites in the tax code," he said. "You cannot consistently narrow your base, especially for the income tax, and expect your spending priorities to be well-funded."

With Democrats in control of Congress, they are likely to hold hearings and subpoena Trump officials over the administration's student loan policies, said higher education expert Mark Kantrowitz.

One priority may be pressing the Education Department to fix the popular Public Service Loan Forgiveness program, which cancels the remaining federal student debt of eligible government and nonprofit workers after a decade. Over the summer, Trump officials reassessed repayment timelines, delaying some borrowers' forgiveness date by years.

Broad student loan forgiveness is unlikely: Trump could veto such a bill, and the Supreme Court blocked the Biden administration's plan. More attainable changes could include restoring tax-free treatment of forgiven student debt, raising the new borrowing caps and expanding repayment options — measures that may attract bipartisan support, Kantrowitz said.

Trump has also said he may support legislation capping federal student loan interest rates, a proposal that could give Democrats common ground in their push to ease borrowers’ costs.

Housing affordability was a key election issue, and Democrats will likely explore ways to deliver relief, such as introducing measures to help first-time homebuyers, according to an Oct. 30 analysis by Desjardins, a Canadian financial services company.

However, lawmakers generally have little direct impact on home prices, which are based on market conditions. Mortgage rates, too, are influenced by forces generally beyond congressional control. The yield on the 10-year Treasury, which remains higher than it was before the Iran war — due at least partly to concerns about inflation eroding the value of those bonds — influences mortgage rates.

"Jumpstarting the housing market relies on a calmer geopolitical environment and bond market in the short run," plus more housing supply and a "more dynamic labor market in the long run," said Jake Krimmel, senior economist at Realtor.com.

The bipartisan 21st Century ROAD to Housing Act, which automatically became law July 11 without the president's signature due to a constitutional provision, is "a model for what can pass in a divided Washington," Krimmel said. It includes measures to encourage housing construction, reduce regulatory barriers, expand financing options and limit purchases of single-family homes by large institutional investors.

The incoming class of senators will have to address Social Security insolvency.

The program's trustees project that the trust fund Social Security relies on to help pay retirement benefits, formally known as the Old-Age and Survivors Insurance Trust Fund, may be depleted in the fourth quarter of 2032. Without action from Congress before then, benefits could face an across-the-board cut of 22%.

A number of Democratic lawmakers and some Republicans have called for increased payroll taxes on high earners to help shore up Social Security.

A potential SECURE 3.0 bill — a follow-up to bipartisan retirement-savings laws passed in 2019 and 2022 — could help expand workers' access to workplace retirement plans like 401(k)s, for example, among a roster of other reforms, experts said.

"Trump promised to cap credit card rates at 10%," said Aaron Klein, a senior fellow in economic studies at the Brookings Institution, a think tank. "A Democratic Congress might be willing to do that."

Any such cap is likely to be closer to 36% than to the 10% cap Trump has sought, Jaret Seiberg, a financial services policy analyst at TD Cowen, an investment bank, wrote in a research note Wednesday. Lawmakers in the current Congress have proposed legislation with these thresholds.

A cap would likely be welcome news for low- and middle-income Americans, Klein said. They are more likely than wealthy households to use debt to finance their lives and to carry a credit card balance — and therefore owe interest on their charges, he said.

Cardholders might see other impacts of such a policy, such as lower credit limits and a clawback of certain card rewards, he said.

Democrats' takeover of Congress could stall efforts to put crypto-friendly regulations into federal law, but everyday investors shouldn't expect much to change immediately.

"Platforms will keep offering the products they offer today, and they still have friendly regulators at the SEC and CFTC who are approving an ever-wider range of crypto products," said Lee Reiners, a cryptocurrency regulation expert and lecturing fellow of economics at Duke University.

The election results could threaten the Clarity Act, which would give the Commodity Futures Trading Commission greater authority over cryptocurrency trading while reducing the Securities and Exchange Commission's role. The bill passed the House in July 2025 by a 294-134 vote, but failed to advance in the Senate in September on a 49-50 procedural vote. "CLARITY is dead," said Reiners, adding that he sees little chance of the bill passing during the lame-duck session before Democrats take control in January.

Any future legislation aimed at creating a regulatory framework for digital assets would likely need to address Democrats' concerns about investor protections and conflicts of interest involving Trump's crypto businesses, said Martin Mulyadi, an accounting professor at Shenandoah University.

The SEC and CFTC can also continue developing crypto-friendly rules without Congress. But those rules are easier for a future administration to reverse than legislation, said Marcel Miu, a certified financial planner and founder of Simplify Wealth Planning based in Austin, Texas. "I'd tell investors to size their crypto investments as if the rules could change repeatedly over the coming years," Miu said.

The Iran war has led oil prices — and those of gasoline, jet fuel, heating oil, diesel and other fuels refined from oil — to surge. That has trickled into higher prices across the U.S. economy, experts said.

Oil prices fell on Wednesday on hopes that the election outcome will push Trump to make a deal to end the war.

"Ending the war in Iran would be the biggest policy lever to reducing inflation," the Brookings Institution's Klein said.

A Democrat-led Congress could try to limit Trump's ability to continue the war, perhaps by cutting funding.

"Congress' biggest power is the power of the purse," Klein said. "Wars are expensive."

— CNBC's Lorie Konish and Stephanie Dhue contributed reporting.

#2942. As AI becomes more fractured, one name stands out: AMD

As Anthropic rushes to takes its shares public, the AI trade is showing cracks, and they are not confined to fringe players.

We recently wrote about CoreWeave, which fell 15.5% on Oct. 14 as yields on its 2032 notes touched 15%, and its default-swap spreads nearly doubled in October. IREN is down more than 20% this year. Last Thursday, the contagion reached chipmakers, which makes sense since much of CRWV and IREN's spending is going there. AMD dropped 8.1% as Meta's financing plans and a supply warning from Lam Research exacerbated data-center fears about the pace of AI spending.

Because when you screen for factors that demonstrably generate outperformance over time, the list narrows quickly. My screen: price above the 200-day moving average; revenue growing at least as fast as the S&P 500 (5%+ per annum); EPS growing at least as fast (7%+); rating upgrades; a PEG below 3; and ROE and ROIC of at least 12%. Of the names reporting in the next two weeks, AMD is effectively the only one that makes the list. Yes, you can (and arguably should, as our staples article described) position your portfolio with slightly less tech exposure than the broad indices provide, but we can't ignore the market's biggest and most transformative sector entirely.

Especially because the growth in this case doesn't just meet my criteria; it blows past them. Consensus for Q3 calls for EPS of $1.94 on revenue of $13.06 billion, versus $1.20 and $9.25 billion a year ago. That's YoY growth of nearly 62% and 41%, respectively. The recent drawdown came from a sector selloff, not anything AMD reported, but it shows that if some names in the space get hit, even the larger players are likely to feel it. On Oct. 6, Lisa Su said AMD would substantially increase supply in 2027 because AI orders keep outrunning capacity.

So how do you balance factors we know work for a fast-growing company against a backdrop of cracks in the AI trade? Options.

Owning the stock outright means absorbing a roughly 9% to 11% implied earnings-related move (day of). Earnings are about a week out, and over the past four quarters the smallest move over a similar window was more than 9%, and the largest move, after the company reported its Q1 26 results, was 38%! Of course, that kind of volatility means options aren't cheap, and that rich premium is also a reason not to simply buy calls.

A call spread risk reversal does both jobs: it reduces risk if the cracks widen, and the short options offset the elevated volatility we would otherwise pay for. The bottom chart shows that, by sacrificing about $1,700 of upside for $5,000 in outperformance during a severe downside move, and given how jittery this trade has been lately, this is a way to stay in it with considerably less risk. $5,000 less, specifically. Of course, selling that out will tie up some cash, but less so than simply buying the stock. And selling both the out and the call reduces long call purchase by 66%

Disclosures: Tidal owns/holds all the securities mentioned in the article.

#2941. NextDC shares surge 43% after Goodman, Digital Realty stake purchases

Shares of Australian data center operator NextDC surged more than 42% Thursday after Goodman Group and U.S.-listed data center operator Digital Realty moved to acquire stakes of up to 9.9% in the company.

In a regulatory filing on the same day, NextDC said the two companies conducted an aftermarket bookbuild Wednesday, seeking shares at A$9.50 apiece.

NextDC's board called the move "opportunistic," noting that it came two weeks after the company disclosed a power supply delay at its M3 Melbourne data center and revised its fiscal 2027 guidance.

The company said it was not aware of the outcome of the share purchase. Goodman and Digital Realty told investors they had no current intention of making a takeover bid, but reserved the right to do so.

NextDC's rally came against a backdrop of weakness in U.S. data center stocks overnight. Shares of CoreWeave fell about 9%, while Iren dropped roughly 11.2% on Wednesday.

The sell-off followed sweeping Democratic victories in the U.S. midterm elections, which raised expectations of tighter scrutiny and potential restrictions on data center development that could slow the AI infrastructure buildout.

#2934. A 'demand decade' ahead for energy? BP, Baker Hughes CEOs weigh the investment outlook

Rising energy demand is creating new investment opportunities even as geopolitical risks and uncertain oil prices challenge the industry.

Baker Hughes CEO Lorenzo Simonelli is optimistic about the longer-term outlook, pointing to AI, population growth and developing nations as factors supporting continued energy demand growth.

"I think we have a 'demand decade' ahead of us," Simonelli told CNBC's Dan Murphy on "Access Middle East" at the ADIPEC energy conference in Abu Dhabi. "We're looking at very much seeing capital investments improve and increase over time," as growing consumption will require additional infrastructure to support demand.

However, turning that demand into profitability may be a challenge.

Markus Krebber, CEO of German energy company RWE, said AI and electrification were driving electricity demand, but geopolitical tensions, supply chain pressures and higher interest rates were complicating investment decisions.

"You have huge investment opportunity, but on the other hand, a very challenging investment environment," Krebber said during a separate ADIPEC discussion. On planned expansions in computing capacity and the power needed to support them, he said "it's going to come, but it probably comes slow."

The wide range of global energy demand forecasts is an important consideration in investment decisions, given that new oil and gas projects can take years to develop and remain in production for decades, BP CEO Meg O'Neill said at ADIPEC.

She also highlighted the uncertainty surrounding oil prices, saying that "we need to plan our business for a lower-price environment."

Industry activity is expected to rise by a low-single-digit percentage in 2027, according to Simonelli. The Middle East conflict has highlighted the need for more resilient energy infrastructure, including additional storage and pipelines, he said.

"If you look at the energy system, though, what it really means is it's going to have to be more resilient," Simonelli said. "That means more storage. It means more pipelines. It means eliminating choke points that exist today."

Schneider Electric CEO Olivier Blum noted that some projects in the Persian Gulf have slowed because of the Middle East conflict. However, "in the mid-term, I would say the opportunity for more infrastructure has even increased," he told CNBC.

The investment equation also depends on the supply outlook, of course.

Oil markets could face oversupply in 2027 and 2028, Richard Bronze, co-founder and head of geopolitics at energy consultancy Energy Aspects, told CNBC in a separate interview.

Supply-demand forecasts, including those from Energy Aspects, "project an oversupplied market 2027, 2028 — if we get Middle Eastern flows back to normal," Bronze said.

#2930. Austin Reaves, Luka Dončić have been impressed with Walker Kessler’s recent play

In his return to Utah on Oct. 25, Walker Kessler struggled, scoring two points (1-of-2 shooting) and committing four fouls. Afterward, he conceded that he let his emotions overwhelm him and never found a rhythm against the Jazz. Since then, he's recorded three double-doubles in five outings — all of which have come the past three games — and averaged 2.6 blocks each night.

During the Los Angeles Lakers' win over the Miami Heat on Monday, he tallied 15 points (7-of-13 shooting), 15 rebounds, two assists and two steals. A day prior in a victory against the Toronto Raptors, he posted 16 points (6-of-6 shooting), 15 rebounds, three blocks, one assist and one steal.

Through eight games, Kessler's acquisition has been a smash hit for Los Angeles. He's averaging 14.4 points (69.8% true shooting), 10.5 rebounds, 2.5 blocks and 1.1 steals.

"He's a big key for us. I think when he plays good, it gives us a way better chance to win," Luka Dončić told reporters postgame on Monday. "He just need to keep going like this. Like we told him, we have his back. Obviously, he's not going to play every game great, but what he's been doing, it's helping us win a lot."

The Lakers' success this season will continue being shepherded by Dončić, Kessler and Austin Reaves. Each of them has kicked off 2026-27 in excellent form, so it's no surprise Los Angeles finds itself 6-2 with the league's second-ranked offense and seventh-best net rating (plus-8.8). When those three share the floor, the Lakers have outscored their opponents by 104 points in 145 minutes while notching a 138.3 offensive rating and 105.8 defensive rating, per NBA.com.

Reaves, the longest-tenured Laker, seems to enjoy playing alongside his new starting center and understands his importance.

"He's been great. It's fun to watch," Reaves said Monday. "Glad to have him on our team and he's going to continue to get better every single game. But he's been massive for us this whole year."

Kessler and the Lakers will look for a third consecutive win Wednesday night on the road against the Sacramento Kings at 7 p.m.

#2928. White House panel delays hearing on Fed's Lisa Cook to Dec. 14

A White House committee of inquiry postponed from Thursday to Dec. 14 a hearing with Federal Reserve Governor Lisa Cook for an investigation of whether allegations of false statements in mortgage applications warrant her firing by President Donald Trump, a White House official said Wednesday evening.

But it was unclear if Cook, whom Trump unsuccessfully tried to fire in August 2025, would agree to attend.

The official said the postponement came at the request of Cook.

Her lawyer Abbe Lowell last month said questions about the committee's fairness and objectivity would have to be addressed before the hearing could occur.

Lowell also said the hearing could not take place until December, at the earliest, because she "will be executing her official duties as Governor at financial and monetary policy meetings in Europe."

The New York Times first reported the hearing's rescheduling.

Trump in early October established the committee to investigate allegations that Cook made false statements related to "one or more mortgage instruments."

"The Committee shall investigate the allegations against Governor Cook and report to me whether there is 'cause' for Governor Cook's removal within the meaning of" a federal law that was cited in a Supreme Court ruling in June that temporarily blocked her firing.

The committee is comprised of Kevin Hassett, assistant to the president for economic policy, Andrea Lucas, the chair of the Equal Employment Opportunity Commission, and Office of Government Ethics Director Keith Sonderling.

In a lawsuit challenging her firing, she argued that Trump's claim about the mortgage statements is pretextual, and that his real reason for trying to oust her is her refusal to agree to his demands that she vote for lower interest rates.

Trump created the panel more than three months after the Supreme Court ruled that the president, for now, could not fire Cook from the central bank's board.

The ruling kept in place a lower court's order preventing Cook from being terminated while the suit challenging her dismissal proceeds.

The decision did not determine whether Trump ultimately has the power to fire a Fed board member. But it said she could remain on the board as her suit was pending because she was not given due process to challenge her firing.

The committee of inquiry Trump set up may be designed to show the Supreme Court that Cook has now received due process.

#2926. Federal Reserve Board releases results of the 2025 Survey of Consumer Finances, which provides the public and policymakers with detailed insights into the economic condition of American families

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 10:00 a.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Friday released the results of the 2025 Survey of Consumer Finances, which provides the public and policymakers with detailed insights into the economic condition of American families.

The data provide a representative picture of what Americans own, how and how much they borrow, and how they bank. The current version of the survey has been undertaken every three years since 1989 and provides the public with an extensive time series of thorough information on the economic conditions of American families. Alongside the survey data, the Board issued its summary report entitled Changes in U.S. Family Finances from 2022 to 2025 (PDF), which examines changes in income, net worth, assets, debt, and financial vulnerability.

Key findings from the 2025 survey include:

In addition to the summary report, an interactive chartbook displaying changes in these financial characteristics over time is available here. Additional data from the survey, including extensive data examining other economic conditions of American families, is made available to researchers and the public here.

The survey is conducted for the Board by NORC, a social science research organization at the University of Chicago. Participants in the study are chosen at random from 119 geographic areas, including metropolitan areas and rural counties across the United States, using scientific sampling procedures that aim to produce a sample representative of American families.
